   Tools to Design a Biomimetic Humanoid Robot Actuated
by Pneumatic Artificial Muscles and a Synthetic Neural Network

                                                                                         ii




                                        Abstract




Biomimetic robots apply biological knowledge to robotics and enable controlled exper-
iments in biomechanics and neuroscience. This dissertation addresses four needs for a
human-proportioned robot with braided pneumatic actuators (BPAs): force prediction
across resting lengths, corrected joint-torque predictions, accessible sensory-afferent ev-
idence, and tools for transferring and testing neural controllers between biomechanical
models. Festo BPA characterization across resting lengths, contractions, and pressures
produced a resting-length-dependent maximum-force model and a three-coefficient nondi-
mensional force surface. Joint-torque design factors account for length offsets, effective
series compliance, and wrapping-induced loss of usable actuator length. Redesigned
paired BPA flexor and extensor routes met or exceeded selected human-muscle reference
torques at the measured knee angles. The Sensory Afferent Database (SADb) connects
experimental findings and model assumptions through searchable notes and citation navi-
gation. Neural-model tools, graphical blocks, and simulation interfaces enable controlled
experiments in MuJoCo and Simulink/Simscape. Evaluations establish selected circuit
responses, implementation agreement, and virtual-harness-assisted balance and walking
behaviors. Together, these contributions support experimentally informed robot design
and neuromechanical experiments, with potential prosthetic and orthotic applications.

                                   Acknowledgments




This work was funded by the Department of Mechanical and Materials Engineering at
Portland State University, the National Science Foundation (NSF) grant for NeuroNex:
Communication, Coordination, and Control in Nervous Systems (C3NS) #2015317, NSF
grant #1943483, and the NIH BUILD EXITO program (grant numbers 5RL5GM118963-06,
5TL4GM118965-06, 5RL5GM118963-07, and 5TL4GM118965-07).
   I would like to thank my advisor, Dr. Alexander Hunt, for his guidance, patience,
and the freedom to pursue this project, and my committee members — Dr. Bin Jiang, Dr.
Radhika Reddy, and Dr. David Turcic — for their time and thoughtful feedback.
   I am grateful to my collaborators and labmates: Mohammad Elzein and Lawrence Pang
for their work on the actuator characterization experiments; Lindie Burgess and Connor
Morrow for their work on the knee torque experiments; Cody Scharzenberger for building
the Muscle Mutt platform and for his assistance proofreading; Alex Steele, who designed
the initial biomimetic four-bar knee linkage used in the torque tests and generously shared
solid models and prototype experience; and Jasmine Bradley, whose work reworking the
figures improved their aesthetic quality and accessibility for readers with color vision
deficiencies.
   Finally, I thank my family for their support throughout this long process.

                                       1   Introduction


1.1    Motivation

This dissertation develops actuation models, a sensory-feedback resource, and simula-
tion tools for a biomimetic humanoid robot. The broader goal is to use principles from
biomechanics and neuroscience to improve robot autonomy, balance, and adaptability
in unstructured terrain [Frey et al., 2026, Ha et al., 2025, Ijspeert, 2020]. The potential for
compliant actuation and neural control to improve locomotor energy economy is a further
motivation for this approach.
      The reciprocal goal is to use robots as controllable physical models of biological move-
ment. Musculoskeletal humanoids and limb systems provide ways to investigate motion
generation, internal force transmission, and neural control hypotheses [Asano et al., 2017,
Hitzmann et al., 2018]. Robotic models permit repeatable changes to individual actuators
or sensory pathways, including interventions that would be impractical or unethical in
human participants. They complement animal studies and numerical models by allowing
the mechanical and neural contributions to be manipulated together [Shin et al., 2018,
Goldsmith et al., 2021]. Work with a salamander robot demonstrates this exchange: biolog-
ical evidence informed a spinal-cord model, and robotic tests generated predictions that
were examined in biological preparations [Ijspeert et al., 2007]. A further, translational
goal of this biology–robotics cycle is to inform prosthetic and orthotic design.
      Artificial muscles are a key enabling technology for this class of robot. Among the
                                                                                            2

available actuation technologies, McKibben-style braided pneumatic actuators (BPAs)
combine low mass, high force-to-weight ratio, and intrinsic compliance with a force–length
curve that qualitatively resembles that of skeletal muscle [Liang et al., 2020, Hunt et al.,
2017a]. BPAs have been used in quadrupedal robots [Aschenbeck et al., 2006, Hunt et al.,
2017a, Scharzenberger, 2019], musculoskeletal arms [Hitzmann et al., 2018, Kurumaya
et al., 2016], and dexterous manipulation systems [Chen et al., 2019]. However, BPAs also
differ from biological muscle in important ways: their maximum tensile force occurs at
resting length rather than at an optimal fiber length, their maximum contraction ratio is
substantially lower than that of human muscle, and their force output is a highly nonlinear
function of internal pressure, contraction, and loading state [Hunt et al., 2017a, Sárosi
et al., 2017, Liang et al., 2020]. My experience with 10 mm actuators made the design gap
concrete: at the short resting lengths used in my leg designs, they delivered less force and
contraction than I had anticipated from manufacturer specifications. Existing empirical
models describe useful pressure–contraction relationships [Sárosi et al., 2017, Martens and
Boblan, 2017, Hunt et al., 2017a], but selecting actuators for this robot required predictions
across the resting lengths, pressures, and contractions of the tested Festo BPAs. I therefore
characterized that dependence experimentally and developed a force model in which
resting length is an explicit design input (Chapter 3).
   Actuator force alone, however, does not determine the torque a joint produces. The
robot knee transmits BPA force to the skeleton through mounting brackets, artificial
tendons, and paths that wrap over the joint, and each of these elements departs from the
rigid-body idealization: brackets and fixtures deflect under load, tendons stretch, and a
wrapping actuator loses usable length as the knee flexes. For this robot, the size of these
                                                                                            3

transmission losses and the usefulness of a common correction across knee configurations
had not been established experimentally. The earlier robot routes were selected from
predicted muscle lengths, moment arms, and torque profiles [Bolen and Hunt, 2019]. The
placement searches developed during this dissertation extended that design approach
[Morrow et al., 2020], but their rigid-body predictions still required experimental validation
at the knee.
   Researchers implementing neural control models face two related problems. Sensory-
afferent findings are distributed across species, preparations, and experimental protocols,
so selecting a feedback pathway requires locating its measured properties and the condi-
tions under which they were observed [Van de Crommert et al., 1998, Windhorst, 2007,
2022]. I addressed that evidence-access problem with the Sensory Afferent Database. A
second problem arises when a published neural circuit is coupled to a different mechanical
model: its behavior depends on the actuator mechanics, sensory signals, support condi-
tions, and numerical implementation as well as on the circuit itself. Published architectures
and software supply essential components [Rybak et al., 2006, McCrea and Rybak, 2008,
Markin et al., 2010, Nourse et al., 2023], but the behavior of their particular combination
must be established. I developed coupled human and artificial-muscle models, graphical
neural blocks, and controlled evaluation procedures to distinguish these contributions.
Representative tests establish circuit agreement across implementations, corrective reflex
behavior, and the dependence of a supported stepping configuration on selected feedback
pathways.
   I address these gaps through four connected contributions: BPA force characterization
with resting length as a design variable, isometric knee-torque prediction and route design,
                                                                                            4

the Sensory Afferent Database, and neuromechanical modeling tools. Together, they pro-
vide a basis for selecting actuators, predicting joint output, selecting sensory pathways, and
testing neural controllers in coupled models, including virtual-harness-assisted locomotor
evaluations.


1.2    The Biomimetic Humanoid Robot Project

This dissertation is part of a long-running effort at Portland State University to build
a biomimetic humanoid robot from the trunk down, actuated by pneumatic artificial
muscles and controlled by a synthetic nervous system (SNS). The project connects mechan-
ical design and neural-control development through successive physical prototypes and
computational models.
      The present work builds directly on a series of studies by me and my collaborators. In
my master’s project, the kinematics and dynamics of a biomimetic robot leg were matched
against the Gait2392 OpenSim model, artificial muscle attachment and wrapping locations
were determined for the robot legs, and MATLAB software was developed for evaluating
muscle lengths, moment arms, and isometric joint torques over the full range of motion
[Bolen, 2019, Bolen and Hunt, 2019]. That work showed that simply transplanting human
muscle attachment locations onto a BPA-actuated robot does not reproduce human torque
profiles; the placement optimization developed during this dissertation addresses this
limitation and is introduced in Section 4.1.5. The rest of the platform advanced in parallel,
and this dissertation builds on its hardware and demonstrations: the biomimetic four-bar
knee linkage with a migrating instantaneous center of rotation, designed and validated by
                                                                                                 5

Steele [Steele et al., 2017, Steele, 2018, Steele et al., 2018], is the knee of the robot leg whose
torque transmission Chapter 4 measures, and the canine-inspired quadruped platform
Muscle Mutt, formerly called DoggyDeux, demonstrated antagonistic BPA pairs under
synthetic nervous system control on a physical robot [Scharzenberger, 2019], extending
an earlier AARL demonstration of central pattern generator control of a pneumatically
actuated robot [Hunt et al., 2017b]. The humanoid leg of this dissertation carries that line
forward.


1.3   Research Objectives

This dissertation investigates actuator force and knee torque experimentally, consolidates
sensory-afferent evidence in the Sensory Afferent Database [Bolen et al., 2023, Bolen and
Hunt, 2024], and develops neural-model and simulation interfaces with representative
verification checks. The objectives are:


   1. Characterize the isometric force of Festo BPAs with uninflated diameters of 10 mm
      and 20 mm over a wide range of resting lengths, contractions, and pressures; de-
      rive improved maximum-force and normalized force–strain–pressure models; and
      compare them against existing models (Chapter 3).

   2. Validate and improve the prediction of isometric knee torque produced by BPAs
      on pinned-joint and biomimetic four-bar knees; identify and model losses caused by
      constant length offsets, series compliance, and actuator wrapping; and compare the
      resulting torque envelopes with human capabilities (Chapter 4).
                                                                                             6

  3. Consolidate the sensory afferent literature into a searchable resource, the Sen-
        sory Afferent Database, by auditing and curating the published record of afferent
        properties across animal species and their role in motor control, and by evaluating
        AI-assisted screening of candidate papers for expanding the collection (Chapter 5).

  4. Develop and demonstrate neuromechanical modeling tools by implementing the Li–
        Deng walker, Gait2392-based models with alternative neural controllers and artificial-
        muscle actuation, SNS-Simscape blocks for neural pathways, and staged tuning and
        experiment procedures; verify representative circuit responses, implementation
        agreement, and neural–mechanical signal exchange (Chapters 6 and 7).


1.4    Dissertation Organization

Chapter 2 introduces the robot, BPA actuation, musculoskeletal design, locomotor con-
trol, and the scientific rationale for robotic models. Chapter 3 presents the isometric
force characterization and actuator models, adapted from my publication [Bolen et al.,
2026]. Chapter 4 tests the joint-torque predictions, identifies compliance and wrapping
corrections, and uses them to redesign actuator routes.
      Chapter 5 presents the Sensory Afferent Database, its curation, and its use limits. Chap-
ter 6 begins with attempts to transplant published controllers into a different mechanical
model, identifies the development requirements exposed by those attempts, and presents
the tools and representative checks built to address them. Chapter 7 discusses the contribu-
tions together and develops the future work; Chapter 8 states the conclusions. Figure 1.1
summarizes these connections.
                                                                                             7

   Appendices A–C provide the muscle calculations, neural equations, model geometry,
and supporting mechanical results. Appendix D collects the larger simulation evaluations,
and Appendix E provides the database coverage and pathway tables.

                                      2   Background


I review the robotics, actuation, biomechanics, and neural-control background needed
for this dissertation, followed by simulation tools and the ethical rationale for robotic
experiments. The Sensory Afferent Database, my earlier AnimatLab walker [Bolen and
Morrow, 2020], and the simulation interfaces I developed are described in Chapters 5
and 6.


2.1   Biomimetic and Musculoskeletal Robots

Biomimetic robots offer many potential applications, ranging from assisting in factories
and environmental exploration to serving as physical models of human movement. By
developing high-fidelity biomimetic robots, we can perform tests that help improve our
understanding of both biomechanics and the underlying neuromechanical systems that
control movement [Shin et al., 2018, Asano et al., 2019, Ijspeert, 2020, Goldsmith et al.,
2021, Hunt et al., 2017b, Schilling et al., 2022]. A few such biomimetic robots exist, such as
Kengoro and Kenshiro robots of Asano et al. [2017] and Asano et al. [2019], which have
proportionally human bodies, anatomically correct musculotendon arrangements, rolling-
sliding joints, and joint ranges and output power comparable with human-measured
values; their artificial muscles are implemented with brushless DC motors, wires as
tendons, gears, and integrated tension measurement. Kengoro, the more recent of the two,
stands 1670 mm tall, weighs 56.5 kg, and carries 174 degrees of freedom when the end
                                                                                           9

effectors are included [Asano et al., 2017]. A dog-like robot developed by Aschenbeck et al.
[2006] and actuated with Festo BPAs was designed from a planar kinematic model scaled to
an adult greyhound, with three sagittal degrees of freedom per leg driven by antagonistic
BPA pairs; its front-leg pair alone lifted a 13.5 kg payload, almost double the complete
robot’s predicted mass. That platform was used to show how central pattern generators
provide robust locomotion patterns that are adaptive to perturbations in ground speed
[Hunt et al., 2017b].
      Robots built with artificial muscles thus provide opportunities to investigate how
muscles are controlled to produce desired motions; however, artificial muscles do not
exactly replicate muscle properties, and existing robots do not capture all of the degrees of
freedom and joint torques of the animal limbs they mimic. The biomimetic leg developed
and tested in this dissertation builds on this approach: a human-proportioned skeleton,
muscle-like actuators, and the goal of human-matched joint torques.
      Figure 2.1 illustrates the human-scale skeletal structure and tendon routing of this
approach.


2.2     Robot Actuation Technologies

2.2.1    Overview

A wide range of actuation technologies have been explored in robotics, including electric
motors, hydraulic systems, smart-material actuators, and artificial muscles [He and Gao,
2020, Liang et al., 2020]. Electric motors (Figure 2.2(A)) are widely used due to their con-
trollability and maturity, but they are limited by thermal constraints, gearing requirements,
                                                                                    10




Figure 2.1: Kengoro human-mimetic musculoskeletal humanoid in neutral and upper-limb
poses, illustrating its human-scale skeletal structure and densely routed tendon-driven
musculature [Asano et al., 2017].
                                                                                           11

and reduced compliance compared to muscle-like actuators [Veale and Xie, 2016, Suzumori
and Faudzi, 2018]. Hydraulic actuators (Figure 2.2(C)) offer exceptionally high power
density and have enabled highly dynamic legged systems, but they remain relatively
heavy, less efficient than biological muscle, and require a dedicated fluid infrastructure
[Suzumori and Faudzi, 2018].
   Among smart-material actuators, dielectric elastomer actuators (DEAs), a class of
electronic electroactive polymers, contract by a different mechanism: an applied voltage
squeezes a soft polymer film between compliant electrodes through electrostatic stress, and
the thinning and radial spreading of the film produces strains that can exceed biological
muscle (Figure 2.2(D)) [Madden et al., 2004]. Comparative studies report high specific
work and good bandwidth for DEAs [Liang et al., 2020], but their typically kilovolt-range
driving voltages, electromechanical instability, and compliant-electrode requirements
complicate integration into a human-scale leg. Thermally activated artificial muscles reach
the most extreme performance figures of any technology surveyed by Liang et al. [2020]:
twisted-fiber muscles support loads more than one hundred times those of a biological
muscle of the same length and weight, with output stresses of 22 MPa. Their activation
route, however, is shared with shape-memory alloys, whose heating is fast but whose
cooling is limited by heat transfer away from the element, and that asymmetry sets the
cycle time.
   Closer to muscle, series-elastic and pneumatic actuators trade peak force and band-
width for compliance, a trade that wearable and rehabilitation robotics exploit because
force control and backdrivability matter most when a machine interacts with a human
[Aksöz et al., 2019, Leibach, 2020, Li et al., 2020]. A recent review of pneumatic artificial
                                                                                           12

muscle technology documents continued progress in actuator design and in assistive
applications [Zhagiparova et al., 2025]. None of these technologies yet combines muscle-
like force, strain, and compliance in one actuator, which is the combination a biomimetic
humanoid leg demands and that braided pneumatic actuators approximate most closely.

   (A) Electric torque motor                   (B) Braided pneumatic actuator

                                                           Internal pressure




          Electromagnetic shaft rotation           Radial expansion; axial contraction


   (C) Hydraulic cylinder                      (D) Dielectric elastomer




       Fluid pressure produces linear force
                                                  Electric field: thinning and expansion

Figure 2.2: Representative actuator mechanisms: electric motor (A), braided pneumatic
actuator (B), hydraulic cylinder (C), and dielectric elastomer (D) [Suzumori and Faudzi,
2018, Liang et al., 2020].
                                                                                              13


2.2.2   Braided Pneumatic Actuators

In contrast to the technologies above, McKibben-style braided pneumatic actuators (BPAs)
combine low mass, high force-to-weight ratio, and intrinsic compliance with a force–length
curve that qualitatively resembles that of skeletal muscle [Liang et al., 2020, Hunt et al.,
2017a]. A BPA consists of an inflatable bladder inside a braided sleeve; pressurizing the
bladder expands the bladder radially and contracts the sleeve axially, producing tensile
force (Figure 2.2(B)). As shown by Chou and Hannaford, the force–length relationship
of McKibben actuators is governed by geometric constraints imposed by the braided
shell, leading to a nonlinear dependence of force on actuator length through the braid
angle [Chou and Hannaford, 1996]: actuator force is proportional to pressure and to a
nonlinear geometric term (3 cos2 θ ´ 1), which governs both the maximum contraction
limit and the curvature of the force–length relationship. Predicting their force as a function
of length and pressure is therefore the first improvement of the torque tools developed in
this dissertation, and the state of that prediction is the subject of the next subsection.
   Figure 2.3 shows the change in actuator shape with inflation.
   However, BPAs exhibit several important differences from biological muscle. First,
BPA maximum tensile force occurs at resting (i.e., uninflated) length (l0 ) rather than at
an optimal fiber length [Liang et al., 2020]. Second, their maximum contraction ratio
is substantially lower than that of human muscle, presenting challenges for achieving
biomimetic joint ranges of motion [Hunt et al., 2017a, Sárosi et al., 2017, Liang et al., 2020].
Third, BPAs have a highly nonlinear dependence on internal pressure (P), contraction
(ϵ), and loading state [Hunt et al., 2017a, Martens and Boblan, 2017], which complicates
                                                                                        14




Figure 2.3: Festo BPAs in the test fixture: the inflated actuator is wider and shorter than
the deflated actuator beside it.
                                                                                           15

control and motivates high-fidelity force–length–pressure modeling. Despite these differ-
ences, their muscle-like properties have made them attractive for biomimetic quadrupeds
[Aschenbeck et al., 2006, Scharzenberger, 2019] and musculoskeletal arms [Hitzmann et al.,
2018, Kurumaya et al., 2016, Chen et al., 2019].


2.2.3   BPA Force–Length–Pressure Models

Previous studies have generated empirical models of the force–length–pressure relation-
ship of BPAs. Sárosi et al. [2017] discuss several high-fidelity BPA force models. They
present a static force model with a 21-coefficient polynomial function for a Festo MAS-
20-200N (i.e., I20 mm, l0 = 200 mm) with an impressive R2 = 0.9994, as well as Sárosi’s
static force model for a Festo DMSP-20-400N-RM-RM (i.e., I20 mm, l0 = 400 mm), with
six coefficients and a R2 = 0.9995 [Sárosi, 2012, Sárosi et al., 2013]. Martens and Boblan
present an even more accurate model, in terms of absolute error, using five coefficients for a
DMSP-10-250 (i.e., I10 mm, l0 = 250 mm) and a DMSP-20-300 (i.e., I20 mm, l0 = 300 mm)
[Martens and Boblan, 2017]. However, as demonstrated in this work, different resting
lengths produce different force–length curves, preventing the use of these single-length
models for designing systems with different resting lengths.
   Hunt et al. [2017a] looked at six resting lengths of I10 mm Festo BPAs, accounting for
differences in maximum contractile percentages, and elucidated the force-length-pressure
relationship. In particular, for a given contraction percent and force F (in Newtons), the
scalar pressure P (in kPa) required to produce this force for a I10 mm Festo artificial
muscle can be determined by solving the equation:
                                                                                           16


                                                                               
                                                                  ϵ
                   P = P0 + k F F + k S S + Pt tan c a                    ´ cb           (2.1)
                                                             ϵmax ´ k E F

where F is the required force, ϵ is the contraction, S encodes hysteresis (S = 1 shortening,
S = ´1 lengthening, S = 0 static), and the seven coefficients are defined in Table 2.1.
The coefficients have been updated with corrected values, as those reported in Hunt et al.
[2017a] contained typographical errors. The contraction ϵ and the maximum free-load
contraction at 620 kPa, which appears as ϵmax in Equation (2.1), are defined in Section 3.1.1,
together with the relative contraction ϵ˚ = ϵ/ϵ620 on which the force surface developed
in this dissertation is built. Because Equation (2.1) expresses pressure in closed form as
a function of force and contraction, it can also be solved onto a grid of pressures and
contractions to build a force–pressure–relative-contraction lookup table for design studies.

Table 2.1: Coefficients of the 10 mm Festo BPA pressure model of Equation (2.1), corrected
from Hunt et al. [2017a].

      Symbol    Value                Role

      kF        1.23 kPa N´1         linear force-to-pressure gain
      kS        15.6 kPa             hysteresis offset (sign of S)
      Pt        192 kPa              tangent-term amplitude

   Festo provides a manufacturer tool, MuscleSIM, for predicting force as a function of
pressure and contraction [Lang, Robert, 2005], but experimental comparisons indicate that
it does not adequately account for variation in l0 or actuator-specific contraction limits.
Comparisons with experimental data indicate that none of the existing models effectively
                                                                                            17

take into account differences in maximum force due to initial actuator length, and the Hunt
model was taken with low external forces (ď106 N), so it is unclear how well it captures
actuator behavior at higher forces. Chapter 3 compares these models with data collected
for this dissertation and develops the improved characterization on which the torque and
controller work of the later chapters depends.


2.3    Human Lower-Limb Biomechanics

Designing a biomimetic leg requires a quantitative benchmark of human muscle–joint
behavior. This dissertation uses OpenSim [Delp et al., 2007, Seth et al., 2011, 2018] and the
Gait2392 model, named for its 23 degrees of freedom and 92 musculotendon actuators.
Gait2392 descends from the interactive lower-extremity model of Delp et al. [1990] and
includes musculoskeletal geometry from Hoy et al. [1990] and planar knee kinematics
from Yamaguchi and Zajac [1989]; related lower-limb models of the same family are
characterized and validated by Arnold et al. [2010]. Muscle moment arms, force-generating
properties, and functional roles in such models have been characterized extensively [Zajac
and Gordon, 1989, Brand et al., 1982, Menegaldo et al., 2004, Millard et al., 2013, Sherman
et al., 2013, Suderman and Vasavada, 2012], and standard reporting conventions for
kinematics and joint mechanics are established by the International Society of Biomechanics
[Wu and Cavanagh, 1995, Wu et al., 2002, 2005, Derrick et al., 2020].
      Moment arms relate muscle force to joint torque. For a fixed-axis joint, the signed
moment arm is rm = â ¨ (r ˆ û), where â is the joint-axis direction, r locates a point on
the muscle’s line of action relative to the joint, and û is the force direction [Brand et al.,
                                                                                             18

1982]. The tendon-excursion method instead evaluates the sensitivity of musculotendon
length to the joint coordinate, with sign determined by the coordinate and force conven-
tions [Menegaldo et al., 2004]. For multiarticular muscles, the result also depends on
adjacent-joint configurations [Zajac and Gordon, 1989, Young et al., 2019]. OpenSim uses
length sensitivities, so the reference moment arms in this dissertation are properties of the
Gait2392-derived geometry [Delp et al., 2007, Seth et al., 2018]. Chapter 4 compares the
robot’s resulting torque predictions with test-stand measurements.
      Knee modeling requires particular care because the instantaneous center of rotation
changes with flexion. Four-bar descriptions reproduce this behavior through linkage geom-
etry [Romero et al., 2020, Steele, 2018]. Gait2392 instead prescribes planar translations as
functions of knee flexion [Yamaguchi and Zajac, 1989]. These different kinematics produce
different moment arms for the same nominal attachment locations. Joint-coordinate con-
ventions must therefore be stated consistently when comparing models and measurements
[Wu et al., 2002]. Gait2392 provides the human reference in this dissertation, while Steele’s
four-bar mechanism supplies the physical robot knee.


2.4    Biomimetic Knee and Bipedal Robot Model

Steele’s biomimetic knee joint supplies the physical knee used in this dissertation [Steele
et al., 2017, Steele, 2018, Steele et al., 2018]. It is a one-degree-of-freedom four-bar linkage
whose crossed links reproduce the posterior migration of the human knee’s instantaneous
center of rotation (ICR) during flexion. The rolling-and-sliding behavior of the human joint
therefore emerges from the link geometry itself rather than from cams or gear profiles, and
                                                                                       19

the design was calculated to reproduce an average sliding-to-rolling ratio of 0.079 over a
151˝ range of motion [Steele et al., 2018]. Because the ICR migrates, muscle moment arms
about the knee change with flexion angle in a human-like way, and a torque prediction
tool for this knee must capture that variation rather than assume a constant arm. The
biomimetic knee tests of Chapter 4 therefore exercise the corrected torque model against
this geometry, and Figure 2.4 summarizes the mechanism.

  (A)                              (B)

   In my master’s work, muscle lengths, moment arms, and isometric joint torques were

human model while many fell far short for part of the RoM [Bolen, 2019, Bolen and Hunt,
2019]. Hand placement of muscle origins and insertions was a painstaking and iterative
task. This resulted in the creation of a placement optimization algorithm [Morrow et al.,
2020], discussed in Chapter 4. Figure 2.5 places the laboratory skeleton beside the robot

2.5    Neural Control of Locomotion: Central Pattern Generators

Spinal central pattern generators (CPGs) can produce alternating flexor and extensor activ-
ity without rhythmic input or proprioceptive feedback [Brown, 1911, Rybak et al., 2006].
Brown’s half-center hypothesis attributes this alternation to reciprocally inhibitory central
populations [Brown, 1911]. In the model of Deng et al. [2019], persistent sodium currents
with slow inactivation support alternating bursts in the rhythm-generating neurons.
      Experiments on the deafferented stick-insect thoracic nerve cord provide a direct
example of central rhythm generation: application of pilocarpine induced alternating
rhythmic activity in antagonistic leg motoneuron pools [Büschges et al., 1995]. This
preparation separates rhythm production in the nervous system from the sensory feedback
generated by movement.
      The two-level model of Rybak et al. [2006] separates a rhythm generator (RG), which
sets cycle timing, from a pattern-formation (PF) network, which regulates motor output
(Figure 2.8). This distinction accounts for deletions of motoneuron activity that leave
locomotor period and phase intact: an intervention can suppress pattern output without
resetting the rhythm. Afferent inputs reach both levels. McCrea and Rybak [2008] reviewed
the electrophysiological evidence for this organization, and Rybak et al. [2015] related it
to genetically identified interneuron classes. Commissural pathways coordinate the left
and right networks [Molkov et al., 2015, Danner et al., 2016], while the rhythmogenic core
remains under experimental investigation [Rancic and Gosgnach, 2021].
      The extension of Shevtsova et al. [2016] includes dedicated pattern-formation pop-
ulations for the two-joint posterior biceps–semitendinosus and rectus femoris muscles
                                                                                     24




Figure 2.8: Two-level CPG: rhythm generation (RG), pattern formation (PF), motor output,
and afferent feedback. Reproduced from Rybak et al. [2006, Fig. 1].
                                                                                             25

(Figure 2.9). Coordinating these muscle pools presents a convergence problem that also
arises when a robot muscle crosses two joints. Section 2.6 shows how related circuit models
incorporate afferent feedback; the controller developed in Chapter 6 uses this two-level
organization.


2.6    Sensory Feedback in Locomotion

Sensory feedback is an integral part of locomotor pattern generation. Reviews of afferent
control of the CPG [Van de Crommert et al., 1998] and muscle proprioceptive feedback and
spinal networks [Windhorst, 2007, 2022] summarize the classical picture: group Ia afferents
from muscle spindles signal muscle length and velocity, group Ib afferents from Golgi
tendon organs signal force, and group II afferents signal length. Load-related feedback
plays a role distinct from length-related feedback during locomotion [Dietz and Duysens,
2000].
      Primary experiments show how the effect of a sensory signal depends on the prepara-
tion and step phase. In decerebrate, paralyzed cats undergoing fictive locomotion, Guertin
et al. [1995] found that stimulating ankle-extensor group I afferents enhanced hip, knee,
and ankle extensor activity during extension and could reset flexion to extension. Group
I includes both Ia and Ib afferents, making the stimulation conditions important when
interpreting this result.
      Joint position also contributes to phase transitions. In chronic spinal cats, Grillner and
Rossignol [1978] found that moving a held hindlimb backward could initiate swing, with
lift-off depending on hip position and contralateral step phase. In walking decerebrate cats,
                                                                                                                     26

feedback while preserving the distinction between the two phase transitions.
   Removing proprioceptive feedback degrades locomotor patterns in mice [Akay et al.,
2014]; proprioceptive input regulates and adapts locomotor activity [Lam and Pearson,
2002]; and the relative contributions of proprioceptive and vestibular systems remain under
study [Akay and Murray, 2021, Akay, 2020]. Coupled models show how feedback interacts
with biomechanics to shape CPG output [Markin et al., 2010, Deng et al., 2022]. Tonic
central and sensory stimuli can facilitate involuntary air-stepping in humans [Selionov
et al., 2009]. The proprioceptive and contact feedback channels designed in Chapter 6
follow this afferent organization.
   The rat hindlimb controller of Deng et al. [2019] combines RG/PF control with muscle-
receptor pathways, interneurons, Renshaw cells, and motoneurons (Figure 2.10). Its
sensory and central pathways converge on the motor output rather than acting as separate
controllers.

2.7    Computational Neuroscience

Computational neuroscience expresses hypotheses about neural circuitry as executable
models. In conductance-based models, membrane voltages evolve through capacitance,
leak, synaptic, and applied currents, and connections represent identifiable pathways
[Nourse et al., 2023]. Model predictions can be compared with recordings or with responses

rather than infer it from a wiring diagram alone. Synthetic nervous systems use this
approach to control physical and simulated bodies.


2.8    Synthetic Nervous Systems

Synthetic nervous systems (SNSs) implement candidate neural circuits with conductance-
based spiking and non-spiking neurons. Work in the Quinn laboratory at Case Western
Reserve University and related laboratories, including my own, has applied this approach
to pneumatic dog-robot control [Hunt et al., 2017b], human balance [Hilts et al., 2019], rat
hindlimb mechanics [Young et al., 2019], the neuromechanical walker of Deng et al. [2019],
and Muscle Mutt, the canine-inspired BPA robot developed at the Agile and Adaptive
Robotics Laboratory (AARL) at Portland State University [Scharzenberger, 2019]. Related
work includes sensory-entrained bipedal CPGs [Li et al., 2017], insect-inspired inter-leg
coordination [Nourse et al., 2019], reinforcement-reflex hybrids [Goldsmith et al., 2021],
and adaptive hindlimb walking controllers [Schilling et al., 2022].
      The SNS-Toolbox [Nourse et al., 2023] makes such networks practical: it is an open-
source Python package that simulates hundreds to thousands of spiking and non-spiking
neurons in real time or faster, supports multiple software and hardware backends including
GPUs and embedded platforms, and couples directly to physics simulators such as MuJoCo
for musculoskeletal control. The field it serves is active and growing: the perspective
review of Szczecinski et al. [2023] traces neuromorphic legged-locomotion control from its
insect-robot beginnings through current platforms to open problems, and physical testbeds
                                                                                          32

in this lineage now extend from insect-scale robots to a robotic cat hindlimb [Armstead
et al., 2025].


2.9    Neuromechanical Simulation

Neuromechanical simulation couples a musculoskeletal model to a neural controller. Three
partially overlapping approaches are relevant here. The first encodes muscle reflexes
directly: Geyer and Herr [2010] used muscle- and phase-specific pathways, including
positive and negative force or length feedback, contact-dependent switching, and trunk-
posture control, to produce human-like walking without a CPG. Hybrid work tests the
contribution of feedback alongside other control signals: Haeufle et al. [2018] combined
feed-forward muscle activations with neuronal reflex feedback and found that the robust-
ness benefit of each depends on the muscle.
      The second line replaces hand-designed circuitry with machine learning. Song et al.
[2021] trained deep reinforcement learning policies on a physiologically realistic mus-
culoskeletal model in OpenSim, reproducing human kinematics and muscle activations
across locomotion tasks, with the learned policy re-usable for experiments that would be
difficult in the laboratory. This approach inherits the fidelity of the musculoskeletal model
but encodes control in opaque function approximations: the trained policy can reproduce
behavior, but the individual “neurons” of the controller do not correspond to identifiable
physiological circuits, so the class of scientific questions it can answer, such as lesions,
deletions, and pathway stimulation, is narrower than its behavioral repertoire. Recent
perspectives on predictive musculoskeletal simulation [De Groote and Falisse, 2021] place
                                                                                         33

both learned and optimal-control approaches in the same frame: the models are mature,
and the open problems are the cost of solving them and the biological interpretability of
the control they find.
   The third approach uses physiological circuit models, including the CPG-plus-afferent-
feedback models of Markin et al. [2010] and Shevtsova et al. [2016] (Sections 2.5 and 2.6).
SNS-Toolbox supports conductance-based networks at speeds suitable for selected real-
time robotic applications [Nourse et al., 2023]. Related work includes motor-module
bipedal walkers [Ichimura and Yamazaki, 2019], a musculoskeletal macaque model
[Nishizaki et al., 2026], and muscle-driven robotic hind legs [Fukuoka et al., 2021]. For
the humanoid studied here, the methodological question is how to implement and
verify candidate spinal circuits with human musculotendon mechanics and custom BPA
actuation while distinguishing neural effects from conversion or coupling errors. Chapter 6
addresses that question through model implementations, graphical neural blocks, and
controlled verification and feedback tests.


2.9.1   Simulation Tools

All three lines depend on a software substrate that is itself part of the method: elaborate
musculoskeletal geometry and complex neural models. The tools used in this dissertation
are the following.
   OpenSim [Delp et al., 2007, Seth et al., 2018], distributed through the SimTK (Simulation
Toolkit) repository, provides the biomechanical modeling side: musculoskeletal models
with detailed bone geometry, muscle routing, and force-generating properties, along with
                                                                                      34

tools for analyzing moment arms and isometric torque. These models are approximations
with known limits rather than exact anatomies: muscle paths are interpolations over
discrete attachment and wrapping data [Bolen, 2019]. Their geometry is accurate for a
purpose, namely moment arms and torque predictions over the functional range of motion,
and that is the standard against which the robot model of this program is compared.
   The same SimTK infrastructure hosts SCONE, an open-source package for predictive
simulation and optimization of neuromuscular control of biological motion [Geijtenbeek,
2019].
   AnimatLab [Cofer et al., 2010] provides a graphical environment in which physical
bodies and synthetic neural systems are constructed and co-simulated; the AARL’s prior
walker (Section 6.1) was built in it.
   MuJoCo [Todorov et al., 2012] provides the physics-engine side: fast, accurate simula-
tion of rigid-body dynamics with contacts, along with muscle-like actuators that include
activation dynamics.
   MyoConverter bridges the biomechanical and physics-engine worlds by converting
OpenSim musculoskeletal models to MuJoCo format with optimized muscle kinematics
and kinetics [Caggiano et al., 2022, Wang et al., 2022].
   SNS-Toolbox supplies the neuron and synapse models used to simulate the controller
[Nourse et al., 2023].
   I used these tools to develop a robotic OpenSim leg model, convert it to MuJoCo, and
couple it to a synthetic nervous system. Chapter 6 describes the assembled interfaces
and their evaluation. The coupled simulation supports repeated parameter, stimulation,
and pathway-removal tests under controlled conditions. Whether those tests support a
                                                                                         35

biological hypothesis depends on the model’s validation and the experimental comparison,
as well as simulation speed.


2.10   Testing Neuromechanical Hypotheses on Robots: The Ethical Case

Destructive interventions, such as deleting a muscle or lesioning spinal circuitry, cannot
be used as controlled experiments on healthy human volunteers. Tonic stimulation is a
different case: human studies have used tonic central and sensory stimuli to facilitate
air-stepping [Selionov et al., 2009]. Robotic and computational models additionally permit
selective, repeatable pathway stimulation and removal without invasive procedures on
a living subject. The same experiments in animals also carry real moral cost, and their
justification is governed by the Three Rs framework: replacement of animal subjects with
non-animal methods, reduction of the numbers required, and refinement of the remain-
ing procedures [Russell and Burch, 1959, Tannenbaum and Bennett, 2015]. Robotic and
computational models allow proposed neural interventions to be tested under controlled
conditions before corresponding animal experiments are considered. The same model
and initial conditions can be reused across interventions to isolate the effect of a changed
pathway. Repeating a model does not create independent biological subjects, and its
relevance to animal experiments depends on validation against biological observations.
   The invasive preparations that motivate replacement are not always apparent in circuit
schematics of decerebrate-cat locomotion, which emphasize the neural circuits under study
but often leave the surgical preparation out of view. A simplified drawing of a walking cat
can make the animal appear intact, alert, or even contented. That appearance should not
                                                                                       36

be confused with the condition of the experimental animal: a brainstem transection has
disconnected the forebrain, while neural circuits below the transection can still generate
treadmill stepping [Brown, 1911, Severin et al., 1967]. Figure 2.13, taken from archival
footage, shows the physical preparation behind the circuit diagrams [WhiteCoatWaste,
n.d.]. This distinction makes the ethical meaning of replacement concrete: the robotic
experiments discussed here are intended, in part, to reproduce selected neural mechanisms
without repeating these invasive preparations in living animals.




Figure 2.13: Archival decerebrate-cat treadmill experiment [WhiteCoatWaste, n.d.]. The
frame is restored and machine colorized; color and fine detail are inferred. It provides
documentary context, not gait evidence.


   The procedures behind that record are documented in films that remain publicly
                                                                                           37

available. A 50 min classroom recording posted by the UTHealth Houston School of
Dentistry first shows an intact cat playing with its handler, righting itself when lowered and
placing its paws accurately on the edge of a table, and then documents the decerebration
performed for the class [UTHealth Houston School of Dentistry, 2025]: the animal is
anesthetized with open-drop ether, more than the horse in the same demonstration series
received, the carotid arteries are ligated and a tracheal cannula installed, and the skull
is opened with a trephine before the brainstem is transected between the superior and
inferior colliculi. The treadmill preparations that established locomotor neurophysiology
are likewise preserved in the historical literature: Stuart [2007] reproduce the arrangement
of the high-decerebrate cat treadmill experiments, with electromyographic recording from
the hindlimb muscles, from the original Russian-language reports [Severin et al., 1967].
   The honest accounting is murkier than the Three Rs framing alone suggests, because
the models a robot implements are themselves products of animal experimentation. The
half-center organization, the two-level rhythm and pattern architecture, and the length,
force, and load signaling of the afferents were each established in preparations like the
ones described above [Brown, 1911, Severin et al., 1967, Rybak et al., 2006], and the
databases of afferent properties that parameterize sensory feedback are compiled from
animal recordings [Bolen et al., 2023]. A synthetic nervous system therefore replaces
future experiments; it does not replace, and cannot repay, the animal work on which its
architecture is built. Recognizing this inheritance sharpens rather than undermines the
case for robotic subjects: the knowledge purchased at that cost is precisely what makes a
robotic replacement scientifically meaningful, and the robot is the instrument that keeps
that knowledge productive without adding to the ledger.
                                                                                              38

   For a robot to serve as a replacement subject, however, it must first earn standing as
a scientific model. Webb’s influential analyses of bio-robotic methodology [Webb, 2001,
2002] argue that a robot is a good model of biological behavior when the mechanism it
implements is tested at the same level of organization as the target phenomenon, matching
dynamics, control structure, and sensory ecology rather than surface appearance. The
validation chain of this dissertation is constructed to that standard: the actuator force model
is validated against bench data (Section 3.2.1), the joint torque model against isometric
measurements and human torque envelopes (Section 4.2), and selected controller responses
are compared with published tonic stimulation and deletion phenomena [Selionov et al.,
2009, Rybak et al., 2006], the verification step that precedes locomotion tuning in this
program. Only then can a robot experiment be read as evidence about the hypothesized
biological circuit rather than about the robot.
   The argument is ultimately bidirectional, completing the loop described in Section 1.2.
Experiments that are unethical, impractical, or statistically unreachable in humans and
animals become routine on the robot; the resulting insights into neuromechanical control
feed back into biology, into the design of the next robot iteration, and directly into prosthetic
and orthotic devices that face the same problem of controlling muscle-like actuators for
human users, whose central pattern generator controllers are an active topic in lower-limb
exoskeleton research [Duan et al., 2024, Pery et al., 2026]. The ethical case and the scientific
case are the same case: the robot is the instrument that makes these experiments possible.
                                                                                          39




         3   Isometric Force Characterization of Braided Pneumatic Actuators


Publication note: Sections 3.1.1–3.1.2, Section 3.2.1, and Sections 3.3.1–3.3.5, with their
associated figures and tables, appeared in Bolen et al. [2026] (Bolen, Elzein, Pang, and
Hunt, Actuators, 2026). For that work, B.B. performed the conceptualization, methodology,
software, validation, formal analysis, investigation, data curation, visualization, and
original draft preparation; M.E. and L.P. contributed methodology, software, investigation,
and data curation; and A.H. supervised the work, administered the project, and acquired
funding. All co-authors contributed to writing, review, and editing.
   This chapter develops and validates the actuator force models on which the rest of
the dissertation builds, and it reads as one self-contained study, from methods through
results to discussion. Sections 3.1.1 and 3.1.2 present the methods: the force equations used
throughout this work, and the test jig and measurement procedure used to characterize
Festo BPAs with 10 mm and 20 mm uninflated diameters over a wide range of resting
lengths, contractions, and pressures. Section 3.2.1 reports the resulting maximum-force
and normalized force–strain–pressure models and compares them with the manufacturer’s
tool. The remaining sections discuss the effects of resting length, the nondimensionalized
force prediction, the comparison with other published BPA models, the implications
for real-time control and simulation, and the analogy between these artificial muscles
and biological muscle. The validated force model is the one used for the knee-torque
predictions of Chapter 4 and for the BPA actuation of the neuromechanical simulations in
                                                                                             40

Chapter 6.


3.1     Methods

3.1.1    Actuator Force Model

The actuator force model used throughout this dissertation is described here. First, the
amount of contraction ε in a BPA is calculated as


                                                  ( l0 ´ lm )
                                          ε=                                               (3.1)
                                                       l0

      where l0 is uninflated resting length of the BPA measured from hose clamp to hose
clamp. Current muscle length is lm .
      Similarly, ε 620 is the amount of contraction in a BPA without external load when inflated
at 620 kPa, calculated as


                                                   (l0 ´ l620 )
                                        ε 620 =                                            (3.2)
                                                        l0

      where l620 is the muscle length measured at 620 kPa and free from external loads.
      The amount of relative contraction of a BPA ε˚ can then be calculated by dividing
equation (3.1) by (3.2), such that


                                                       ε
                                            ε˚ =                                           (3.3)
                                                     ε 620

      describes the BPA’s current contraction as a normalized percentage of its total.

   The amount of normalized isometric force F˚ in a Festo BPA is then calculated as

                                    F (ε˚ , P˚ , l0 ) = F˚ (ε˚ , P˚ ) ¨ F620 (l0 )                (3.6)

   as the equation for isometric force F in a Festo BPA.


3.1.2   BPA Isometric Force Characterization Experiment

To measure artificial muscle force as a function of length and pressure, we built a test
jig from extruded aluminum and 3D printed parts. Festo BPAs with 10 mm and 20 mm
diameter were tested with various resting lengths and different amounts of contraction.
                                                                                        42

These data are used to develop an improved model that more accurately predicts maximum
muscle force based on l0 , current muscle length lm , and pressure P. The results are
compared with existing models and the new data are used to create an updated model.
   A test jig was made of 80/20 brand 1515 series extruded aluminum (Figure 3.1). Artifi-
cial muscles were placed vertically in the jig one at a time. The upper end was attached to
a load cell. Force data was collected using a CALT DYLY-103 100 kg S-shaped load cell for
the I10 mm BPAs and a Loadstar RAS1-2HKS-S for the I20 mm BPAs. The load cell was
used in conjunction with a HX711 Load Cell Amplifier. Pressure data was obtained from a
Freescale MPX5700 GP 5 V pressure sensor. Building air supply pressure was controlled
with two pressure regulators in series. The first was a Parker model 20R113GC 0 ´ 120 psi
pressure regulator. The second was a Husky 3/8 in. High Performance Air Regulator
HDA72200. A Festo VTUG-10-MRCR-S1T-26V20-T516LA-UL-532S-8K valve manifold
VTUG-G was used to deliver air from the pressure regulator to the actuator. This manifold
is comprised of eight two-in-one bidirectional normally closed Festo VUVG-S10-T32C-
AZT-M5-1T1L valves.
   Pressure and load cell amplifier data were sent to MATLAB 2021b via an Arduino
Uno style Sparkfun BlackBoard C microcontroller. The computers that used MATLAB
were running either Windows 10 or Windows 11. During phases when the Arduino was
collecting force and pressure data to send to MATLAB, the Arduino would also trigger
(via MATLAB) an Onsemi 2N4401 NPN transistor to make the valve manifold open or
close the valve. For other data collection, the valve was manually opened and closed.
   The inner distance between the hose clamps on each BPA was measured with a FANUC
tape measure to determine the muscle’s length l and resting length (l0 ). This is how
                                                                                           43

Festo AG & Co. KG [2026] defines l0 , although in Hunt et al. [2017a] it was measured
to also include end cap length. We then inflated each BPA to P620 = 620 kPa, with one
end allowed to move freely in the axial Degree of Freedom (DoF), and measured the
length l620 to calculate maximum contraction at 620 kPa using Equation (3.2). The distance
between the crossmembers was then controlled to obtain different amounts of contraction,
the muscles were inflated to various pressures, and the contractile force was recorded
at the pressure–contraction pairs. This was done for I10 mm BPA resting lengths (l0 ) of
112 mm–518 mm. For I20 mm BPAs, resting lengths (l0 ) of 300 mm–509 mm were used.


3.2     Results

3.2.1    BPA Force Characterization

This section reports the results of the isometric force characterization experiments described
in Section 3.1.2. First, maximum isometric force at 620 kPa is modeled as a function of
resting length. The remaining data are then normalized to build a predictive force surface,
and the resulting model is compared with the manufacturer’s tool and with other published
BPA models.


Maximum Force at 620 kPa

Data of the maximum force from BPA characterization tests of the 10 mm and 20 mm
diameters at 620 kPa show a dependency on resting length (Figure 3.2). This is a previ-
ously unreported characteristic of these artificial muscles. Detailed analysis of the Festo
                                                                                       44




                 Test
                 Frame

                 Load
                 Cell                                 Air
                                                      supply

                  End
                  caps


                         Hose                       BPA
                         clamps                     Length




                 Air to pressure
                 sensor


Figure 3.1: A picture of a BPA in the isometric force test stand with components labeled.
                                                                                         45

tool [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2026] does in fact predict a change in maximum force with the
resting length; however, the Festo tool predicts increased force with shorter lengths, while
our collected data indicate decreasing force with shorter lengths. The data show a force
response resembling an arctan curve along the l0 dimension. Using the Nonlinear Least
Squares method and a Least Absolute Residual robustness, we fit an arctan curve to the
data to get the maximum force at 620 kPa given a resting length as:


                    F62010 = 303.5 N ¨ arctan (19.03 m´1 ¨ (l0 ´ 0.0075))              (3.7)


                    F62020 = 922.4 N ¨ arctan (15.37 m´1 ¨ (l0 ´ 0.013))               (3.8)


The length is offset by 0.0075 m and 0.013 m because solid modeling showed that the end
caps contact each other at these lengths. At these lengths, the actuator would not be able
to contract to produce force.


Force as a Function of Pressure and Resting Length

Data from BPA characterization tests of the 10 mm muscles resulted in force–pressure
pairings for different muscle resting lengths (l0 ) (Figure 3.3a). Similar to the maximum
force data, these data show a force response resembling an arctan curve along the l0
dimension with a more linear response to changes in pressure. Using the Nonlinear Least
Squares method and a Least Absolute Residual robustness, we fit an arctan curve to the
data to get the maximum force given a resting length and pressure as described by the
model form given in Section 3.1.1, where a1 = 0.4895 N kPa´1 and a2 = 0.030 68 kPa´1 m´1

Maximum Contraction at 620 kPa

Figure 3.3(b) shows an attempt at a linear fit for maximum contraction at 620 kPa (ϵ620 ) as
a function of resting length (l0 ). Error bars have been included to show the effect of ˘1 mm
accuracy with measurements of l0 and l620 . There was a large amount of variance in the
data, with the linear fit giving an adjusted R2 = 0.4124 and an RMSE = 0.0083. Since
there is no direct, predicable relationship between maximum contraction and l0 , this value
should be recorded in each muscle used on a robot to best predict the force it may produce
at different pressures and contraction.


Force as a Function of Pressure and Contraction

In addition to being a function of the pressure and resting length, the force produced
by the actuator is also a function of the amount of actuator contraction, with less force
being applied as the actuator contracts. To build this model, the collected data of force,
pressure, and contraction are normalized by dividing by F620 , P620 , and ϵ620 , respectively.
This had the effect of compressing the data into a 3D surface ranging from 0 to 1 on all
axes. Normalized data are compared with pressure isoclines of force predicted by the

      We therefore derived our own equation for isometric force in the BPA as a function of
pressure and contraction. Visual analysis of the experimental data shows an exponential
relationship between ϵ˚ and F˚ , and a linear relationship between P˚ and F. We fit a surface
to the data using Nonlinear Least Squares and a Least Absolute Residual robustness such
that it is described by Equation (3.4). For the I10 mm BPAs, the result of the improved fit
can be seen in Figure 3.4a. The adjusted R2 = 0.9998, a RMSE = 0.004537, and a maximum
absolute residual of 10.6%. Solving (3.4) for the I20 mm BPA, yields different coefficients,
and results are seen in Figure 3.4b. Coefficient values and goodness-of-fit statistics are
found in Table 3.2.


3.3     Discussion

3.3.1    Effects of Resting Length

We have developed new F620 equations for 10 mm and 20 mm diameter Festo BPAs that
capture the change in maximum force produced by the actuators at 620 kPa as a function
of their resting length. When examining Equation (3.7) and measured data in Figure 3.2b,
it can be seen that as l0 goes to infinity, F62010 goes to 470 N. When the Festo tool [Lang,
Robert, 2005] was queried at l0 = 1 m as an approximation of infinity, it predicts the
I10 mm BPA can produce F = 490 N at P620 . This is within the 10% variability that Festo
states may occur in manufacturing tolerances. Similarly, the Festo data predict a I20 mm

                                           500                200 kPa, model          200 kPa, data

Figure 3.4: Surface fit for F˚ (ϵ˚ , P˚ ). Solid lines are from our model. Dashed lines from

                                                                                          52

BPA will produce F = 1570 N. Our results for Equation (3.8) show that F62020 goes to
1460 N as l0 approaches infinity. Although the difference is much larger, it is also within
the 10% manufacturing tolerance.
   Our measured data differ significantly from the Festo model as l0 goes to zero. For the
measured data, as the length gets smaller, the force does as well, whereas the Festo tool
predicts exponential force increase (see Figure 3.2). As a specific example, when specifying
l0 = 112 mm for the 10 mm diameter BPAs, the Festo predicted F620 is 498.6 N. However,
using Equation (3.7), F620 is calculated to be 335.3 N. Actual force in the l0 = 112 mm was
measured at 350.9 N. Therefore, the error in predicted F620 for the I10 mm BPA is 4.5% for
our model and 42.1% using the Festo tool. Researchers using BPA resting lengths under
300 mm should take note of these results.
   Festo AG & Co. KG [2026] (p. 12) states that force will be reduced by approximately
10% for a minimum nominal length. In Festo AG & Co. KG [2018] (p. 14) they say the force
can deviate by 10% based on factors such as production variation, material variation, and
deviation from nominal length (l0 ). Festo AG & Co. KG [2013] (p. 13) notes that testing
was done with BPAs that are ten times the uninflated diameter, and that theoretical force
can increase by up to 10%. In each datasheet, the characteristic curves appear to be the
same. As demonstrated above, the 100 mm I10 mm BPA should have much less force
than what is shown in the curve. This is our experience and the reason for the testing. It is
unclear why the MuscleSIM tool [Lang, Robert, 2005] then predicts higher force as resting
length decreases. It could be a reversed sign coefficient in their internal curve-fit model.
We have shown here that as the nominal length goes to zero, so does force.
   BPA models commonly predict higher forces than are measured experimentally because
                                                                                            53

they do not account for end effects in regions of the pressure vessel where those effects
dominate. Nominally, the clamped ends fix the inner and outer diameter while allowing
axial and radial displacement. In the isometric case, there is only radial displacement.
The boundary conditions force steep gradients in stress and strain near the ends. Near
the boundary conditions, axial, radial, and circumferential strains deviate from analytic
solutions for a cylindrical model. For a constant pressure, the end effect region appears to
have the same length. Therefore, in shorter BPAs, the end effects dominate. The axial length
scale of the boundary section is comparable to l0 , and the active force differs substantially
from that of an assumed perfect cylinder.
   Manufacturing variations seem to play an important role in the behavior of these
actuators. It remains to be seen how ϵ620 can be known a priori. We still suspect that it is a
function of l0 , but it might also be a function of other factors such as product batch number
or UV light exposure [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2026] (p. 15); [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2018] (p. 6);
[Festo AG & Co. KG, 2013] (p. 13). We found there to be larger variations in the 10 mm
BPAs than in the 20 mm BPAs. Modeling work on ideal McKibben actuators describes how
the relationship between contraction and initial length is a function of the total number
of twists and the angle of twist in the usable muscle area [Chou and Hannaford, 1996]. It
is unclear if there are variations in the angle and number of twists in the Festo BPAs due
to manufacturing. Uncovering the relationship between ϵ620 and l0 for the Festo DMSP
artificial muscles, if it exists in a meaningful way, will require additional controlled tests.
   Similarly, it is not clear how the maximum force might change with manufacturing
variations. This was reasonably corrected for by normalizing the equations to each in-
dividual’s maximum contraction, but more nuanced details might emerge with more
                                                                                               54

data collection. Not knowing these relationships a priori means there is still a need for
robots and other systems to go through an iterative design stage if it is discovered that
the system does not produce the torque or have the RoM that the design team expects.
However, the data and models provided here should enable faster redesigns with fewer
additional measurements.


3.3.2   Nondimensionalized Force Prediction

We have also introduced the concept of nondimensionalized isometric force that is a
function of relative strain and relative pressure (Equation (3.4)). This elegant equation
has only three coefficients with low error (see Table 3.2). In the work presented here, we
introduce the relative pressure term, P˚ = P/P620 . Our data show that by normalizing
force, contraction, and pressure, we are able to create a simplified force equation as
a function of contraction and pressure that scales well with initial actuator length, as
expressed by Equation (3.6) in Section 3.1.1.
   For example, given l0 = 54 mm and P = 300 kPa, the measured force was
Factual = 103 N. Force prediction using only the Festo tool predicts the force to be
Fpredict = 270 N. This is 161% greater than the measured force. Using Equations (3.7)
and (3.4) with Equation (3.6) gives Fpredict = F˚ (0, 0.48) ¨ F620 (0.054) = 0.49 ¨ 230 N = 112 N.
This is an error of 8%, which is much more accurate than using the Festo tool only.
   The presented work is capable of predicting force output for 10 mm and 20 mm
muscles with different initial muscle lengths, internal pressure, and contraction amount
with minimal initial data collection. However, it remains to be seen if these relationships
                                                                                        55

are maintained over the full lifecycle of the muscles. There are not yet any peer reviewed
studies investigating the long-term behavior of these artificial muscles, though the Festo
datasheet provides some indication of what can be expected [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2026]
(p.19). They report that the 40 mm BPA can complete 1 million cycles at 6000 N (maximum
rated load), or 10 million cycles at 4000 N. The service life of the other size BPAs is not
reported; however, it is mentioned that thermal stressing may reduce the lifespan and they
should be inspected every 500,000 cycles for signs of aging [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2018]
(pp. 10–11). Future research should look at the behavior of these muscles over time.
   In our work, P620 = 620 kPa is the maximum supply pressure for our system, and other
users of this actuator may use a different supply pressure. However, this supply pressure
is not required, and normalizing by this value was chosen as a method for improving the
optimization techniques by reducing the sensitivity of the term associated with pressure.
This equation will work for any pressure supplies below 620 kPa. We also anticipate the
equation will work for pressures up to 800 kPa (the maximum rated pressure for Festo
I10 mm BPAs); however, we do not have the data to confirm this.


3.3.3   Comparison with Other Published BPA Models

The proposed model was compared with the formulations by Sárosi [2012], Sárosi and
Fabulya [2012], and Martens and Boblan [2017], to evaluate their predictive performance
across actuator sizes and operating conditions (see Table 3.3 and Figure 3.5). For the
I20 mm BPA, parameters from [Sárosi, 2012] were used, corresponding to actuators with
resting lengths of 200 mm and 400 mm. These were compared against the experimental
                                                                                          56

data at l0 = 300 mm and l0 = 450 mm, respectively. For the I10 mm BPA with resting
lengths of 257 mm and 233 mm, parameters from [Sárosi and Fabulya, 2012] were applied.
In each case, the maximum contraction parameter (k max ) was determined numerically
by solving F (6.2 bar, k max ) = 0, ensuring consistency with the experimental maximum
pressure condition.
   While the Sárosi [2012] and Sárosi and Fabulya [2012] models capture general trends
in force–contraction behavior, discrepancies were observed when applying parameters
derived from different actuator lengths. In particular, the model tends to over-predict force
at higher pressures and low amounts of contraction, suggesting sensitivity to geometric
scaling effects not explicitly accounted for in the formulation.
   The Martens and Boblan [2017] model is derived from a geometric and material-based
framework and provides a more physically grounded representation of actuator behavior.
However, its accuracy depends strongly on precise geometric parameters, including braid
angle, wall thickness, and fiber length. Small deviations in these parameters can lead to
significant differences in predicted force, particularly at higher pressures.
