                               The Martens and Boblan model was first evaluated at the same pressure–contraction
points as in their experimental data [Martens and Boblan, 2017] (Tables A1 and A2),
                                                                                        58

indicating that it reproduces the general published force map shape but is sensitive to the
assumed initial geometry. Examining their I20 mm BPA, for example, using the explicitly
stated l0 = 300 mm and initial diameter D0 = 20 mm significantly under-predicted the
results compared to using the implied l0 = 296 mm ([Martens and Boblan, 2017] (Table
A1)) and D0 + H0 = 21.8 mm, where H0 = 1.8 mm is the material thickness. The diameter
adjustment can be inferred from [Chou and Hannaford, 1996]. This adjusted diameter was
also included for the I10 mm BPA to give a better fit. As can be seen in Figure 3.5 and
Table 3.3, the highest accuracy is observed when I20 mm and l0 = 300 mm, which is at or
very near their experimental measurements. For the I10 mm BPAs the accuracy decreases
even though their experimentally measured l0 = 250 mm is close to our l0 = 257 mm and
l0 = 233 mm. The largest error occurs at I20 mm and l0 = 450 mm and the model starts
                                                             dD
to predict negative force. We have calculated that the FPE ¨ DL term becomes very large
compared to the others [Martens and Boblan, 2017] (p. 5). This suggests that the model is
sensitive to geometric parameter selection and interpretation, particularly with respect to
initial length and diameter definitions.
   In comparison, the present normalized model demonstrates improved consistency
across actuator lengths by incorporating experimentally derived scaling through F620 and
ϵ620 . This normalization reduces sensitivity to geometric variation and enables a unified
representation of actuator behavior, while maintaining predictive accuracy over the tested
range of pressures and contractions.
                                                                                          59


3.3.4   Implications for Real-Time Control and Simulation

Although pneumatic muscle actuators exhibit hysteresis, thermodynamic effects, and
other dynamic behavior, accurate and high-performance controller designs can still be
achieved using a static force map approach when the underlying static force model is
accurate. Martens and Boblan explicitly note that BPA dynamics are often dominated
by the air dynamics inside the actuator and the mass flow behavior of the valve, such
that precise modeling of the static force characteristic remains crucial for torque control
accuracy [Martens and Boblan, 2017]. This view is consistent with more recent BPA control
work, including a sensor-less torque control interface for BPA-driven joints [Martens
et al., 2018], stable force/position/stiffness control of antagonistic pneumatic-muscle
joints [Ugurlu et al., 2019, Hunt et al., 2017b], and sensor-less angle stiffness control of
antagonistic BPA systems [Shin and Kogiso, 2022].
   In this context, the present model is well suited for real-time use because it reduces the
static isometric force prediction to a low-order normalized surface, F˚ (ϵ˚ , P˚ ), scaled by
the resting-length-dependent term F620 (l0 ). This structure is computationally inexpensive
to evaluate online and is also suitable for lookup-table implementation. While the present
work does not model the full actuator–valve dynamics, it provides the static force rela-
tionship needed as a feedforward or inner-model component in real-time torque control,
simulation, and preliminary design workflows. It is anticipated that improved real-time
control for high-speed and high-precision systems will benefit more from improved mod-
eling of airflow dynamics than from modeling actuator hysteresis, damping, and other
dynamic behavior. Characterizing the actuators under dynamic operating conditions,
                                                                                         60

such as isokinetic (constant-velocity), isobaric, and isotonic (constant-load) contractions,
is a natural next step beyond the isometric characterization reported here, for which the
pressure–force dynamics model of Elzein et al. [2025] provides the modeling foundation.


3.3.5   Biomimetic Artificial Muscle Analogy

BPA artificial muscles are often said to be analogous to biological muscles because they
have force–length curves and can produce force only in tension. This analogy is worth
closer inspection. For example, the passive force–length curve has been described as an
exponential function arising from connective tissue elements, while the active force–length
relationship is typically modeled as a bell-shaped curve reflecting actin–myosin over-
lap [Winters, 1990]. These formulations emphasize that muscle force generation is gov-
erned by nonlinear geometric and material interactions rather than simple linear elasticity.
McKibben-type pneumatic artificial muscles (of which Festo is an example) exhibit anal-
ogous nonlinear behavior, though arising from fundamentally different physical mech-
anisms. As shown by Chou and Hannaford, the force–length relationship of McKibben
actuators is derived from geometric constraints imposed by the braided shell, leading
to a nonlinear dependence of force on actuator length through the braid angle [Chou
and Hannaford, 1996]. In their formulation, actuator force is proportional to pressure
and a nonlinear geometric term (3 cos2 θ ´ 1), which governs both the maximum contrac-
tion limit and the curvature of the force–length relationship. The work presented here
demonstrates that this nonlinear curve can be well approximated by an exponential as
well.
                                                                                            61

   However, the behavior of these artificial muscles differs distinctly from that of biological
muscles. For example, the optimal fiber length of a biological muscle, lOFL , is not equivalent
to l0 in an artificial muscle. Artificial muscles produce their maximum active force at their
resting length, which is also their maximum length. In contrast, the maximum active force
that a biological muscle can produce is somewhere in the middle of the total contractile
length of the muscle.
   Furthermore, biological muscles can adapt the optimal operating length through struc-
tural remodeling (e.g., sarcomere addition or tendon compliance) while BPAs have a fixed
geometric configuration. This allows biological systems to self-optimize for the particular
tasks the animal is subjected to. In order to re-optimize a robot for new tasks, new actuators
would have to be swapped into the system. This distinction highlights a key limitation
of BPAs in biomimetic applications, while also reinforcing the importance of accurate
nonlinear modeling for control and design.
   The analogy also suggests two design observations. First, the force–length penalty is
shared by both families: force falls as the actuator operates away from its optimum, so a
limb design must route each actuator such that its highest-force operating range coincides
with the joint angles where torque is needed most, because the force lost at the extremes
of the range of motion cannot be recovered by pressure alone. Second, the two families
gain moment arm in different ways. Skeletal muscle attaches to the skeleton itself, through
tendons or directly over broad areas of bone, so the moment arms of large animals follow
from skeletal geometry. The robot’s BPAs instead act through brackets that protrude from
the limb segments; that hardware is mechanically exposed, and its series compliance is
one of the loss mechanisms quantified in Chapter 4.
                                                                                           62

   The analogy finally raises a question that this dissertation can pose but not settle. Every
muscle-like actuator loses force as it operates away from its optimal length, and a soft
actuator working against soft structure has no rigid skeleton from which to gain a moment
arm. Given those two facts, it may be no accident that nearly all large land animals are
built on rigid skeletons and that large soft-bodied land animals are essentially absent.
Whether the force–length penalty contributes to that absence, or whether it reflects other
constraints such as support and protection, is a question for comparative biomechanics
rather than for a single actuator study. For the designer, the lesson holds regardless of the
answer: some moment arm must come from somewhere, and on this robot it comes from
protruding brackets whose behavior must be modeled rather than ignored.
                                                                                 63

                                 4   Isometric Knee Torque


Publication note: This chapter is the subject of an unpublished manuscript in preparation
by Bolen, Burgess, Morrow, Elzein, Pang, Scharzenberger, and Hunt [Bolen et al., 2025].
For that work, B.B. performed the conceptualization, methodology, software, validation,
formal analysis, investigation, data curation, visualization, and original draft preparation;
M.E., L.P., L.B., and C.M. contributed methodology, software, investigation, and data
curation; and A.H. supervised the work, administered the project, and acquired funding.
All co-authors contributed to writing, review, and editing.
      This chapter connects the actuator force characterization of Chapter 3 to torque about
the robot knee. The earlier torque model assumed inextensible muscle paths on rigid links.
Isometric tests on pinned and biomimetic knees show where those assumptions fail. The
methods develop corrections for length offsets, attachment compliance, and wrapping
loss, then explain how actuator-placement searches use the corrected prediction. Held-out
tests evaluate the correction terms, and physical tests assess whether the redesigned routes
meet selected human-muscle torque targets.


4.1    Methods

I built a modular test stand to measure flexor and extensor torque with either a pinned knee
or a biomimetic four-bar knee. The tests varied actuator diameter and resting length, joint
contact, and tendon attachment. I first compared measured torque with predictions based
                                                                                          65

on the characterized BPA force model. I then set the length, compliance, and wrapping
corrections using separate training and validation tests, and used the corrected model
to redesign routes against human-muscle torque targets. Isometric tests evaluated the
resulting hardware.


4.1.1   Robot Leg Model and Knee Test Stands

The primary components of the robot leg are the knee joint, femur, tibia, BPA assemblies,
and artificial tendons (Figure 4.1(A–C)). The artificial bone components are 3D printed
using a combination of Onyx, carbon fiber, and high-strength high-temperature fiberglass
on Markforged Onyx One and Mark Two printers, as well as machined links for the knee
mechanism. Onyx is a proprietary Markforged material that consists of chopped carbon
fiber in nylon. Artificial tendons are made with Shimano bicycle brake cable.
   The modular test stand accommodates either knee joint and both flexor and extensor
configurations (Figure 4.1). This allows measured joint torque to be compared with
predictions while changing the joint geometry and actuator route. The extensor test at
high flexion also exposes the actuator deformation caused by contact with the knee.
   The leg assembly geometry and dimensions are based on bones scans of a 6 ft (1.83 m)
tall person [Bolen and Hunt, 2019, Seth et al., 2018, Delp et al., 1990, 2007]. Two knee
joints were tested, one pure rotational joint, and one biomimetic joint with rotation and
translation. Each joint is driven by two antagonistic Festo actuators. Both robot joints have
the same center of rotation in the femur frame at θk = 0 deg.
   Details on the biomimetic robot knee used in this study can be found in [Steele, 2018,
                                                                                66




           A)




           B)                         Femur
                                                                   C)

                BPA                           mounting locations


                                              Knee joint

                                                                                             67

Steele et al., 2017, 2018]. In brief, this 1-DoF joint (Figure 4.2) uses a four-bar linkage to
change the effective moment arm during rotation. The intersection point of the internal
crossed links define the instantaneous center of rotation (ICR). This causes the joint ICR
location in the femur frame to translate posteriorly in the X direction and distally in the
Y direction except for a brief proximal move near full flexion. Reference International
Society of Biomechanics (ISB) coordinate frames are given by Wu and Cavanagh [1995],
Wu et al. [2002]. This is similar to how the human knee behaves [Bolen and Hunt, 2019,
Morrow et al., 2020, Seth et al., 2018, Delp et al., 2007, 1990]. The correct location of the
ICR is important for both theoretical and experimental torque calculations.
   Both knee types use uniarticular flexor and extensor actuators. Each knee joint type was
tested with a single I10 mm Festo BPA per muscle. The biomimetic knee was additionally
tested with I20 mm Festo BPAs in a paired configuration: the extensor with two BPAs
in parallel, and the flexor with two BPAs whose pathways are mirrored about the Z axis.
Each artificial muscle is pinned at the muscle origin location on the femur while the other
end is either pinned or attached to the muscle insertion location on the tibia via an artificial
tendon.
   The modeled geometry and torque calculation follow Bolen and Hunt [2019], Morrow
et al. [2020]. Torque about the knee is generated by the moment of the BPA force about the
joint’s instantaneous center of rotation (ICR). Two vectors enter that moment: the position
vector d, drawn from the ICR to the point where the actuator force is applied, and the force
vector F = ∥F∥û, whose direction û is the line of action of the actuator. The moment of the
force about the joint is M = d ˆ F, and the signed knee-axis torque is Mk = ẑk ¨ (d ˆ F).
For force acting in the plane normal to the knee axis, the moment-arm magnitude is

   This planar moment arm is a scalar distance and can be computed from the vectors or
measured directly. For an out-of-plane force, the knee-axis moment must instead use the
axis projection above; the magnitude of the full three-dimensional moment is not generally
the knee torque. In this study it is calculated using the method specified by Young et al.
[2019]. The scalar moment arm rk about the joint axis ẑk (i.e., the Z axis in the knee frame)
is calculated as


                                                       p f ˆ ẑk
                                   rk = p proj,xy ¨                                        (4.2)
                                                      ∥p f ˆ ẑk ∥

   A muscle path may be modeled as a single segment running from origin to insertion,
or as a chain of segments joined at via points. The segment that crosses from one reference
frame into the other, here from the femur frame to the tibia frame, is the free muscle
segment: because its endpoints ride on different bodies, its length and its direction respond
directly to joint rotation, whereas a segment contained within one body’s frame rotates
rigidly with that body and changes neither. Here p f is the force-path segment vector, and
p proj,xy locates a point on that line relative to the joint, projected onto the plane normal to
the joint axis. The normalized cross product in Equation (4.2) is the in-plane normal to the
force path. Its dot product with the projected position gives the signed moment arm of the
projected force path (Figure 2.6). Multiplying this arm by the in-plane force magnitude
gives the knee-axis torque; the full wrench calculation retains all three force components.
Moment arm is calculated in the tibial body frame.
                                                                                        70


4.1.2   Knee Torque Data Collection and Equipment

A test jig was built to collect knee torque measurements at different knee angles over its
range of motion. The test stand frame is made predominantly out of 80/20 1515 series
extruded aluminum components. The knee joint is free to rotate the tibia while the femur
is fixed to the frame. A force sensor has one end connected to the tibia and the other is
connected to a swing arm. The swing arm is tied to a winch with 3/16 inch Kevlar rope
from Quality Nylon Rope routed over intermediate idler pulleys (see Figure 4.1(B) and
Figure 4.1(C)). At each measured position of θk the winch would be locked and the BPA
would be inflated and then deflated. This test procedure kept the measured results as
isometric as possible. The knee joint is co-axial with gravity to eliminate gravitational
torque (see Figure 4.1(A) and Figure 4.1(C)).
   Force data was collected using one of two different sensors. The first is a MODERN
STEP 300 kg digital crane scale. The second force sensor is a CALT DYLY-103 100 kg S
shaped load cell. The load cell is used in conjunction with a HX711 Load Cell Amplifier.
Pressure is measured with a Freescale MPX5700 GP 5 V pressure sensor. Building air
supply pressure is controlled with two pressure regulators in series. The first is a Parker
model 20R113GC 0 ´ 120 psi pressure regulator. The second is a Husky 3/8 inch High
Performance Air Regulator HDA72200. A Festo VTUG-10-MRCR-S1T-26V20-T516LA-UL-
T532S-8K valve manifold was used to supply air to the actuator. Pressure and load cell
amplifier data are sent to MATLAB via an Arduino Uno style Sparkfun BlackBoard C
microcontroller.
   The length measurements were made using a tape measure. When there was sufficient
                                                                                          71

curvature in the BPA during knee extensor torque tests, a flexible piece of string was used
to mark the axial length and then transferred to the tape measure. Angle measurements
were taken with a Medigauge digital electronic goniometer or with MALENOO analog
goniometers of 6, 8, or 12 inch lengths.


4.1.3   Hybrid Torque Calculation Method

The hybrid torque calculation measures the actual muscle length and angles of the robot to
calculate force and joint torque, isolating which part of the torque equation did not achieve
its predicted value. The classical mechanics way to calculate the torque about a joint is
to take the cross product of the position vector d and the force vector F, both defined in
Section 4.1.1,


                                           M = dˆF                                      (4.3)

   Knee torque is the scalar component of this moment about the joint axis, written Mk to
make its axis explicit. For the planar hybrid calculation, its magnitude is the measured
perpendicular arm multiplied by the actuator-force magnitude,


                                           | Mk | = r F                                 (4.4)

   where r is the scalar moment arm of Eq. (4.1), here measured directly with a tape
measure as the perpendicular distance from the knee axis to the actuator’s line of action,
and F is the scalar force magnitude, calculated by measuring the BPA’s P, l0 , l620 and
                                                                                         72

lm values and using the model fit equations determined in the previous chapter. The
hybrid method therefore replaces the model-predicted moment arm rk of Eq. (4.2) and the
model-predicted muscle length with their measured counterparts, leaving the force model
as the only predicted quantity. The flexor and extensor signs are then assigned using the
adopted knee convention.


4.1.4   Improved Torque Model

The classical torque calculation treated links and attachments as rigid: the routed path
depended on joint geometry but did not change under actuator load. Data collected
during the first flexor experiments (Section 4.2) showed that this assumption does not
hold for a pneumatic system: measured muscle lengths were consistently shorter than
predicted, and measured torque deviated from prediction in a load-dependent manner.
This section describes the corrections that were developed to account for a constant length
offset, series compliance in the attachment hardware and artificial tendons, and the loss of
usable muscle length when a BPA wraps around the joint.
   The correction terms represent the combined effects of the attachment brackets, fixtures,
artificial tendons, and test-stand loading path. They are identified from measured torque
because the torque prediction requires their aggregate effect on actuator length, rather
than a separate structural model of every component. The constant term χ0 corrects the
unloaded length discrepancy, χ1 and χ2 describe effective stiffness in the local bracket
frame, and χ3 scales the wrapping-length loss. The compliance calculation couples force
and displacement: force deflects the attachments, and the resulting change in muscle
                                                                                         73

length changes force. The multiobjective procedure of Section 4.1.4 evaluates candidate
terms on fitting and validation tests.


Length Offset and System Stiffness

Measured muscle length was consistently shorter than predicted at all joint angles, produc-
ing less muscle force, a shifted RoM, and less torque in the experimental setup. Because
the discrepancy persists at ε = 1 and F = 0, where no compliance can act, the correction is
a constant length offset: lm was recalculated as


                                   lm = l LMT ´ 2 l f itting ´ χ0                      (4.5)

   where l LMT is the path length of the musculotendon from origin to insertion point,
l f itting is the length of the end caps, and χ0 is a constant length offset.
   Actuator force deforms the attachments, changing effective BPA length and the pre-
dicted force. I approximate this load-dependent deformation with Hooke’s law along the
bracket-frame principal directions. The fitted stiffnesses χ1 and χ2 represent the combined
attachment system, not direct measurements of individual hardware components. Because
fixtures and loading hardware contribute to that compliance, beam theory alone cannot
determine the fitted values.
   A local bracket frame was established at the attachment bracket. The x̂br axis was
aligned with the vector from the bracket reference point to the BPA attachment point and
represents the stiffest direction of the bracket. The ŷbr axis was chosen to point in the
posterior direction with respect to the tibia frame, corresponding to the primary cantilever
                                                                                                74

bending direction (see Figure 4.3), while the ẑbr axis completes the right-handed frame. In
this frame the bracket is modeled as three independent linear springs along the principal
axes, with stiffnesses k x , k y , and k z in the xbr , ybr , and zbr directions.

the equivalent stiffness is k eq = c´1
                                    eq .




                                           ceq = cû,br + cten                            (4.21)

   The series model assumes quasi-static tensile loading, linear compliance, additive axial
displacements, and a tendon without preload. Rotational compliance and force–moment
coupling are neglected. Within each force-balance calculation the line of action is fixed;
the attachment positions, force direction, and moment arm are then recalculated at the
deflected geometry. Antagonistic loading that lengthens the actuator lies outside this
treatment.
   Since δ is the scalar compliance displacement along the BPA line of action, then the
compliance-corrected active BPA length used in the force balance is


                                           l m ( δ ) = l m (0) ´ δ                        (4.22)

   where lm (0) is the initial muscle length calculated from (4.5).
   For a specified set of correction parameters, the following calculation solves the
attachment-compliance displacement at each knee angle; it is distinct from the optimiza-
tion that identifies those parameters from experimental torque data (Section 4.1.4). The
corresponding relative contraction is ε˚ (δ). Since the BPA force depends on contraction,
                                                                                             80

and contraction depends on the compliance displacement, δ is the root of the nonlinear
force-balance equation


                              Fmax ¨ FBPA (ε˚ (δ), P˚ ) ´ k eq δ = 0                      (4.23)

   MATLAB fzero solves Equation (4.23) between zero displacement and the maximum
usable contraction, using its default tolerance. If the actuator is already at that contraction
limit or the residual has no sign change, the evaluator returns δ = 0. Solver tolerance is
distinct from physical-model accuracy.
   After solving Equation (4.23), I used the force to calculate vector bracket displacement,
transformed the displaced attachment into the limb frame, and recalculated routed length,
force direction, moment arm, and torque.
   The actuator path can carry compliant hardware at both ends: the insertion-side bracket
of the preceding development together with a second, origin-side bracket. Figure 4.5 distin-
guishes the three-dimensional displacement of each bracket from the scalar displacement
along the BPA force path. For bracket j, the displacement resolves into components η1,j ,
η2,j , and η3,j along the local bracket-frame basis, whereas only its projection δbr,j along the
corresponding force direction û j changes the actuator length. The two bracket projections
act in series with the tendon elongation δtendon :

BPA Fictive Contraction

As the knee flexes, the extensor BPA contacts the anterior joint surface and bends around it
(Figure 4.6). The rigid routed-length calculation does not account for the resulting change
in usable contraction. I represent this effect with fictive strain: an empirical increase in
                                                                                           82

the contraction seen by the force model, beyond the actuator’s geometric contraction. The
correction is phenomenological; it does not resolve wall stresses or the deformation of the
braided actuator. A wrapping-loss term χ3 scales the associated reduction in usable length:

                                $
                                &χ3 R |θwrap ´ θk | ζ 2
                                ’
                                ’                         θk ď θwrap
                         ∆l =                                                           (4.25)
                                ’
                                %0
                                ’                         θk ě θwrap


   where ∆l is the change in usable length of the BPA, R is the wrapping radius, |θwrap ´ θk |
is the angle through which the BPA is bent once contact begins, and θwrap is the knee flexion
angle at which the BPA starts to bend over the knee joint, determined from solid modeling.
ζ is the additive complement to relative strain, such that


                                          ζ = 1 ´ ε˚                                    (4.26)

   so the fitted wrapping loss grows quadratically toward resting length and vanishes
at the normalized free-contraction limit. Combining (4.26) into (4.25), and subtracting ∆l
from (4.5) yields


                              lm = l LMT ´ 2 l f itting ´ χ0 ´ ∆l                       (4.27)

   Figure 4.6 shows a sagittal schematic of an extensor BPA crossing a flexed robot knee,
with its braided body following an anterior contact arc between the proximal attachment
and distal tendon. Dashed radii bound the contact arc; R is the BPA-path wrapping
radius, and α = |θwrap ´ θk | is the bend angle after contact begins, expressed in radians in
                                                                                          83

Equation (4.25). The drawing illustrates the local circular-wrap approximation, not the full
multi-contact route. The fitted usable-length loss ∆l is not an arc-minus-chord length.

Identification of the Torque-Model Correction Terms

I set the correction terms in two stages using gamultiobj in MATLAB. The flexor stage
fits (χ0 , χ1 , χ2 ) with compliance at both attachments. The selected training/validation
split trains on the l0 = 48.5 cm and 41.0 cm tests and validates on the 45.7 cm and 40.6 cm
tendon tests. The extensor stage locks (χ1 , χ2 ) to that same flexor-front member and fits
only (χ0 , χ3 ). Its selected training/validation split trains on the 43.6 cm, 43.2 cm-tendon,
and 45.7 cm tests and validates on the 40.0 cm plain, 40.0 cm-tendon, and 46.5 cm tests. The
extensor length offset is allowed to be negative. Appendix C records the test constants,
bounds, stiffness-axis assignments, and adopted values in Tables C1, C4, C10, C9, and 4.9.
   Each candidate is evaluated using RMSE, FVU, and maximum absolute residual. These
metrics jointly measure torque error; none isolates a unique source such as level offset,
waveform shape, or angular misalignment.
   The objective vector of the identification is defined explicitly. For a candidate correction
vector χ, (χ0 , χ1 , χ2 ) in the flexor stage and (χ0 , χ3 ) on the locked stiffness pair in the
extensor refit, the predicted torque curve of test i is interpolated to its ni measured knee
angles θij , giving residuals eij = Mij ´ M̂ (θij ; χ) against the measured torque Mij , and the
three goodness-of-fit components of that test are

                                             g
                                             f1
                                             f ÿ
                                     RMSEi = e    eij2                                    (4.28)
                                               ni
                                                      j


                                                    ř 2
                                      ni ´ 1           j eij
                               FVUi =        ¨ř                 2
                                                                                          (4.29)
                                        ni      j ( Mij ´ M̄i )
                                                                                          85

                                      eimax = max|eij |.                               (4.30)
                                                  j

FVU expresses the residual sum of squares relative to the sum of squared deviations of the
measured torque from its mean M̄i , with the factor (ni ´ 1)/ni carrying the small-sample
adjustment of the evaluators; RMSE and the maximum absolute residual carry torque units,
whereas FVU is dimensionless. For a given test, FVU is proportional to squared RMSE, so
the two metrics are not independent measures of shape and level, and across tests their
different scaling changes the relative weighting of the errors. The solver minimizes the
objective vector                                            
                                                           (0)
                                                g1,i (χ)/g1,i 
                                       1 ÿ     g (χ)/g(0)  ,
                                                               
                              g (χ) =             2,i                                  (4.31)
                                      |T | iPT 

                                                               
                                                           (0)
                                                 g3,i (χ)/g3,i

where g1,i , g2,i , and g3,i are the RMSE, FVU, and maximum absolute residual of Equa-
tions (4.28)–(4.30), the superscript (0) marks the zero-correction baseline value of the same
component, the same kinematics and force model with every correction term disabled, and
T is the set of tests used for training. The mean of the baseline-normalized components
makes the three objectives dimensionless and the training tests commensurate. The search
carried no constraints beyond the parameter bounds of each stage, and the returned Pareto
fronts (the candidate parameter sets for which improving one objective requires worsening
another) were filtered to members scoring at or below the zero-correction baseline on every
component of every test in that stage’s identification pool.
   MATLAB gamultiobj uses NSGA-II to return the Pareto front [Deb et al., 2002, The
MathWorks, Inc., 2025]. Candidate predictions are checked on the training and valida-
                                                                                         86

tion tests. The search uses bounded, normalized parameters, with logarithmic stiffness
coordinates. Population, mutation, crossover, and stopping settings are retained with the
archived run records.


4.1.5   Actuator Placement and Route Redesign

The placement search varies actuator attachments to control moment arm and muscle
excursion over the joint range of motion [Bolen and Hunt, 2019, Morrow et al., 2020].
For the erector spinae, internal oblique, and external oblique at the lumbosacral joint,
the targets are Gait2392 torque surfaces sampled at 100 positions along each of two joint
coordinates.
   At each scale the search perturbs both attachments by ˘ϵ offsets along the Cartesian
coordinates (eight candidates per attachment, 64 candidate pairs), centers on the lowest-
cost pair, and halves ϵ when no candidate improves, from an initial ϵ = 0.1 m down to
1 mm [Morrow et al., 2020].
   The original cost combined the summed absolute torque mismatch over the sampled
poses, actuator length, displacement of the attachments from their starting locations, and a
diameter-dependent penalty. The published weights for the first three terms were 10´4 ,
10´2 , and 1, respectively; the penalties for 10 mm, 20 mm and 40 mm BPAs were 1, 5,
and 15. These were numerical weights chosen to balance differently scaled cost terms.
The geometric terms discouraged impractical placements but did not impose collision
avoidance: attachments could lie away from the modeled bones, and routes could intersect
them. This study established the placement-search approach; it did not establish physical
                                                                                        87

torque accuracy or satisfaction of the human torque requirement at every pose.
   A subsequent knee implementation restricted candidates to mesh points on the femur
and tibia: for the biceps femoris short-head replacement, every origin–insertion pair was
evaluated over 100 knee poses from approximately ´120˝ to 10˝ at a fixed 20 mm diameter,
candidates outside the allowable contraction range were rejected, and the lowest-cost
usable pair was retained. Like the earlier cube-perturbation search, this discrete mesh
search used rigid attachment geometry without the experimentally identified compliance
corrections.
   The knee-flexor mesh search also used a different objective from the original torque-
mismatch cost. It rewarded torque surplus in the human torque’s direction while penaliz-
ing disagreement between the human and robot torque-vector directions:

                                          
                    N                            N
                  1 ÿ             ÿ           wu ÿ                 
       Cmesh = ´       wk Dk,j +   wa Da,j +
                                                    1 ´ M̂H
                                                           j ¨ M̂ R
                                                                  j .                (4.32)
                 3N                           N
                     j =1            aPtx,yu              j =1


                                                     R ´ M H when M H ě 0, with the
Here N is the number of evaluated poses, and Da,j = Ma,j  a,j      a,j

subtraction reversed otherwise; Table 4.1 records the weights and their meaning. A zero
robot-torque vector is assigned the opposite human direction in the direction term, which
assumes a nonzero human torque vector at the evaluated pose. Torques are supplied in
newton meters; this is a numerically weighted score, rather than a sum of normalized
physical errors, and minimizing it favors torque capacity in the required direction without
guaranteeing that the requirement is met at every pose.

4.1.6   Constrained Knee-Route Redesign

The dissertation’s route redesign extends the placement search as a constrained continuous
optimization over the design vector x = [p1 , pend , l0 , ltendon ], the BPA origin p1 , insertion
pend , resting length, and tendon length (Appendix C), so that the flexor and extensor
configurations meet or exceed human knee torque capability over the range of motion.
Because the route prediction (contact activation, wrapping, and compliance correction) is
expensive to evaluate and only moderately smooth, the redesign minimizes a single scalar
cost assembled from weighted penalty terms. The cost has the form

                                                      ř
                       J = ww Jworst + wt Jtorque +       a Ja + Jgeom + Jlen ,             (4.33)


where Jworst is the largest fraction by which robot torque falls short of the human target
at any angle across the range of motion, weighted heavily (ww = 105 ); Jtorque is the mean-
squared shortfall fraction (wt = 103 ), weighted two orders of magnitude below the worst-
angle term so that no shortfall at a single angle can be traded away for a better average;
                                                                                                          89

the Ja are configuration-specific auxiliary penalties; and Jgeom and Jlen are regularizers that
keep the endpoints and actuator lengths near their seed values. The two leading terms are
built from the normalized shortfall
                                                
         max       0, (1 + mr )| MH        R
                                  j | ´ | Mj |                                                1 ÿ 2
  sj =                                               ,   Jworst = max s j ,       Jtorque =      s j , (4.34)
                             τ                                       j                        N
                                                                                                 j


where mr = 0.05 is the required torque margin and τ = max(1, max j | MH
                                                                      j |) is the hu-

man torque scale. The flexor and extensor configurations carry the remaining terms of
Equation (4.33) as


                    Jflx = 105 Jworst + 103 Jtorque + 10´2 Jshape + Joff + Jrest + Jstrain,hi

                           + Jstrain,lo + Jwrap + Jgeom + Jlen + Janchor ,                             (4.35)

                   Jext = 105 Jworst + 103 Jtorque + Jover + Jrest + Jstrain,hi

                           + Jstrain,lo + Jcontr + Jgeom + Jlen .                                      (4.36)


Tables 4.2–4.4 define the normalized penalties and their weights. A candidate is rejected if
its route cannot be constructed or its torque or strain calculation is not finite.
   Human torque targets are computed from the Gait2392 lower-extremity model: biceps
femoris short head for the flexor and vastus medialis for the extensor [Delp et al., 2007,
Seth et al., 2018]. At each knee angle, the OpenSim moment arm is multiplied by the
muscle’s maximum isometric force [Delp et al., 1990, Hoy et al., 1990, Sherman et al., 2013].
Appendix A gives the model parameters and exported values. The search also enforces
bone clearance, feasible wrapping, actuator contraction limits, and tendon geometry; a

   Both configurations use surrogateopt followed by patternsearch refinement [The
MathWorks, Inc., 2025, Audet and Dennis, 2006]. The surrogate/refinement evaluation
budgets are 3,000/15,000 for the direct extensor, 1,000/15,000 for the flexor, and 1,000/1,500
for the cam variant. Search can terminate earlier when its step-size and feasibility tolerances
are satisfied.
   The cam redesign exists because the direct route met the torque envelope at actuator
forces that broke the printed and machined parts during testing; increasing moment arm to
reduce actuator force is the design response. Its objective adds three terms to the extensor
cost,

                                                                                           
                                                                   2                  req
   Jcam = Jext (xfull ) + Jarm + JarmStep + Jreg ,     Jarm = ´10 min         1, min m j        ,   (4.37)
                                                                                  j


         req
where m j      = (| MRj | ´ (1 + mr )| MH
                                        j |) /τ is the margin against the required envelope of
                                                                                           93

Equation (4.34), with the moment-arm step and cam regularizer of Table 4.4. The capped
reward favors additional margin after the required envelope is met, while the step penalty
limits abrupt changes in the contact-release schedule.
      The flexor uses continuous bone-clearance constraints and penalties for off-axis torque
and incomplete wrap release. The extensor uses the contact-elimination rule of Ap-
pendix C.7 and penalizes excessive torque and contraction. Both require torque magnitude
of at least 1.05 times the human target; the prescribed 5 % allowance is a design requirement,
not an experimentally established error bound. The two flexor BPA paths are evaluated
separately, whereas the extensor scales the force of its shared route by actuator count. The
final flexor uses a staggered-offset pair rather than a mirrored origin arrangement that
conflicts with the pelvis.
      The redesign retains the objective of obtaining adequate torque from anatomically
constrained routes, but changes both the prediction and the search formulation. Actua-
tor and tendon lengths become independent design variables, geometry is constrained
continuously, and pointwise torque shortfall is penalized using the corrected force model.
Section 4.3.3 quantifies the redesign, and Section 4.3.4 tests it.


4.2    Results

The pinned-flexor results first compare the Hunt actuator model with the improved BPA
force model. Subsequent comparisons use the improved model before and after design-
parameter optimization. Hybrid calculations substitute measured muscle length and
                                                                                      94

moment arm to help distinguish force-model error from geometric error on the pinned
knee.


4.2.1   Flexor on Pin Joint

The pre-optimization comparisons distinguish force-model error from errors in the pre-
dicted muscle geometry (Figure 4.7). The hybrid calculation uses measured length and
moment arm, whereas the other predictions use the routed geometry. Flexion angles and
torques are negative; the caption identifies the individual tests.
   Both pre-optimization force models overpredicted measured flexor-torque magnitude.
On the common domain where both predictions are defined, the improved BPA charac-
terization gives the smaller errors (Table 4.5). Those scores use a different domain from
the full-test corrected-model scores in Table 4.6. The hybrid calculation, using measured
length and moment arm, follows the measurements more closely, identifying the predicted
geometry as an important source of torque error.

4.2.2   Extensor on Pin Joint

The correction terms reduce mean pinned-knee extensor torque-prediction error in both the
training and validation tests (Table 4.7). Figure 4.9 compares the measured and predicted
torque across knee angle. By inspection, the zero point of the measured torque was shifted
to the right of the original model, consistent with an offset opposite in sign to the χ0 term
in the previous section. The BPA in Figure 4.9(C) illustrates the pattern: the hybrid-method
calculated torque correlates well with the originally predicted torque, while the actual
measured torque diverges. The fitted wrapping term χ3 enters the usable-length correction
in Equation (4.25). Table 4.7 reports the improvement of the fit. Extensor-specific terms
(χ0 , χ3 ) were specified while stiffness terms (χ1 , χ2 ) were kept the same as the previous
section (see Table 4.9).

4.2.3     Extensor on Biomimetic Knee

I tested the extensor configuration on the biomimetic knee with a I10 mm BPA at l0 =
51.8 cm. The measured torque fell well below the original prediction (Figure 4.10). Apply-
ing the extensor terms identified on the pinned knee improved the prediction: at those
terms, the optimized model reached an RMSE of 1.1702 N m, an FVU of 0.6681, and a
maximum absolute residual of 2.2088 N m, against 3.0322 N m, 4.4856, and 5.5870 N m for
the original rigid-body prediction.

4.2.4   Flexor on Biomimetic Knee

The shared correction terms reduce torque-prediction error for both biomimetic-knee flexor
configurations (Table 4.8). Figure 4.11 compares these predictions with the measurements;
the bracket frame for the I10 mm BPA is shown in Figure 4.3(C), and for the I20 mm BPA
in Figure 4.3(D). Figure 4.11(A) presents the I10 mm actuator at l0 = 41.5 cm with a 21 mm
artificial tendon (I1.5 mm Shimano bicycle brake cable wrapped four times), pressurized
to 604 kPa; the optimized model reached an RMSE of 2.8002 N m, an FVU of 0.8835, and a
maximum absolute residual of 4.1201 N m. Figure 4.11(B) presents the I20 mm actuator at
l0 = 41.5 cm with a 14 mm artificial tendon made out of the same brake cable, wrapped
three times so that six cable segments share the load, each wrap contributing two tendon
lengths and cross-sectional areas. The configuration in Figure 4.11(B) was tested at 620 kPa,
for which the optimized model reached an RMSE of 2.2070 N m, an FVU of 0.0748, and a
maximum absolute residual of 3.7320 N m.
   The torque of the single I20 mm flexor actuator shown in Figure 4.11(B) did not exceed
the magnitude of the maximum human isometric flexion torque between 60˝ and 100˝ of
knee flexion, and within a ˘20% band about the human mean it still fell short of human-
level torque between 78˝ and 103˝ of flexion. Because operating at 620 kPa across the
full desired range of motion would load the printed femur beyond its ultimate strength,
single points were also recorded at the reduced pressures 560 kPa, 421 kPa, 325 kPa and
281 kPa, which show that the desired range of motion is achievable at reduced pressure.
This shortfall in flexion torque defines the target that the attachment-route redesign of
Section 4.1.5 addresses.

4.3     Discussion

4.3.1    Reasons for the Correction Terms

The correction terms of Section 4.1.4 exist because the physical transmission between
the actuator and the joint is neither rigid nor lossless, and each term names one family
of physical reasons. A constant length offset χ0 exists because of manufacturing and
positioning tolerances: the printed bone segments, the machined knee links, and the end-
cap fittings each carry dimensional tolerance, and the accumulated discrepancy appears as
a length that is consistently shorter than the predicted path at every joint angle, including
at zero load where no compliance can act. The stiffness terms χ1 and χ2 exist because
loaded components displace: the isometric system is not rigid, and there is compliance in
the winch cable of the test stand, the artificial tendons, and the cantilevered attachment
brackets. The wrapping-loss term χ3 exists because a BPA that bends over the joint loses
usable contraction length that no straight-line model accounts for; at high flexion the same
loading was observed to stretch and compress shorter-l0 actuators and to slip the BPA off
the anterior head of the femur bolt by up to ˘20 mm, changing both the force vector and
the torque.
      Wrapping a BPA around the knee remains an approximation with inherent error
(Figure 4.10): high actuator force and a short moment arm make the torque sensitive to
attachment deflection and small geometric errors [Young et al., 2019]. The pinned-knee
tests therefore separate actuator-force error from attachment-compliance and routed-
geometry error, and applying the identified terms to the biomimetic knee tests them where
the moment arm and the instantaneous center of rotation move with knee angle. Isometric
                                                                                           106

testing establishes a static capacity envelope; velocity dependence, damping, and behavior
under controlled speed and load remain unidentified.


4.3.2   Design-Factor Optimization

The results figures use the two-stage identification of Section 4.1.4. Relative to the zero-
correction baseline, the optimized model reduces mean RMSE by 81 % on the flexor training
tests and 80 % on the flexor validation tests (Table 4.6), and by 60 % on the extensor training
tests and 57 % on the extensor validation tests (Table 4.7); on the biomimetic extensor it
reduces FVU by 85 % (Table 4.8). Table 4.9 records the identified terms: the stiffness pair is
shared, and the wrapping factor was identified on the extensor and applied to wrapping
in the redesigned flexor. The genetic multiobjective solver is retained for the identification,
where the Pareto front is required for holdout validation, and the surrogate-plus-pattern-
search pipeline serves the route redesign (Section 4.1.5); the search bounds are recorded in
Appendix C (Table C10).

Table 4.9: Adopted correction terms. The effective stiffness pair is shared; χ3 was identified
on the extensor and is applied to wrapping in the redesigned flexor.

                  Configuration      χ0 (m)    χ1 (N/m)      χ2 (N/m)       χ3
                  Flexor           0.00394    3.998 ˆ 104   1.473 ˆ 104       –
                  Extensor        ´0.00638    3.998 ˆ 104   1.473 ˆ 104   0.158


   The original placement methods selected routes by predicted torque under rigid-
attachment assumptions; the isometric tests show those predictions require the compliance
corrections before they can guide hardware selection, so the redesigned routes evaluate
every candidate through the corrected force balance. On the biomimetic knee the shared
                                                                                         107

terms transfer at an FVU of 0.8835 for the 10 mm flexor and 0.0748 for the 20 mm flexor
(Table 4.8); the larger error for the smaller actuator limits transfer across configurations,
and the redesigned two-BPA routes provide the further physical test of Section 4.3.4.


4.3.3   Redesigned Routes for the Biomimetic Robot Model

The redesigned routes combine the existing biomimetic robot geometry with human-
muscle torque references. The surrogate-plus-pattern-search procedure of Section 4.1.6
uses the correction terms in Table 4.9, two 20 mm BPAs per route, a 620 kPa pressure ceiling,
and a maximum usable contraction of 0.255. Both direct routes meet the required torque
envelope (Table 4.10).

Table 4.10: Predicted direct-route torque capacity on the biomimetic robot model. Both
margin rows use the selected human-muscle torque magnitude as their denominator.

the converted neuromechanical models remains future work.

4.3.4   Exceeding Human Torque Magnitude

Both redesigned routes met or exceeded their selected human-muscle reference torques
at the measured knee angles. The cam-assisted extensor was tested at nine isometric
configurations and the two-BPA flexor at fifteen, spanning ´119˝ to 5˝ . The analysis
transforms the measured load-cell wrench to the moving knee center and evaluates the
model at each measured angle and pressure. Table C7 in Appendix C records the mea-
sured per-actuator dimensions and distinguishes them from the shared prediction inputs.
Figures 4.13 and 4.14 compare these results with the selected human-muscle targets and
the optimized 620 kPa predictions.
   The extensor measurements meet or exceed the vastus medialis reference torque at the
measured angles. Measured extension torque ranges from 29.02 N m to 51.17 N m, with
margins of 0.24 N m to 3.52 N m, at pressures of 256 kPa to 488 kPa. The measured torque
exceeds the pressure-specific prediction at eight angles, by up to 13.75 N m; at ´86.5˝ , the
measurement exceeds the human reference and is 2.06 N m below the pressure-specific
prediction. This prediction uses the recorded angle and pressure with the modeled route.
It is not the hybrid calculation, which additionally requires measured muscle length and
moment arm.
   The flexor measurements meet or exceed the biceps femoris short-head reference
magnitude, with margins from 0.42 N m at ´105˝ to 17.0 N m at ´14˝ . Agreement with
the pressure-specific prediction remains uneven. These measurements support torque
capacity at the sampled configurations; variable test pressure and discrete sampling do
not establish a measured full-pressure envelope between every pair of angles.

4.3.5   Mono- and Bi-Pulley Tendon Routing

Reeving the artificial tendon through a reverse block-and-tackle trades transmitted force
for tendon travel (Figure 4.15). Let FBPA be the total input force, n the number of cable
parts supporting the moving block, ∆ℓBPA the BPA contraction, and ∆st the tendon travel.
For an ideal massless cable and frictionless pulleys,

                                      FBPA
                               Ft =        ,   ∆st = n∆ℓBPA .                           (4.39)
                                       n

The illustrated two-BPA input is FBPA = 2F20 ; with n = 2, Ft = F20 and tendon travel
doubles. The BPA-origin reaction magnitude is FR,origin = FBPA . Reeving, rather than
the number of pulley wheels alone, determines n; the travel multiplier used in the route
calculations is G = n.
   This trade can extend the RoM over which a route meets its torque requirement. At
G = n = 1, force and travel ratios relative to the combined BPA input are unity, recovering
direct transmission. The design calculations retain internal-load and mount-deflection
effects in their stiffness-aware force balance. Appendix C.8 gives the specific optimized
knee-route comparisons and the five-site transmission study (Figures C3 and C4); its
biarticular evaluation planes hold the adjacent joint fixed, and physical verification remains
future work.
                                                                                                                     114




        Origin mount
        FR, origin = FBPA                       Moving
                            Two parallel BPAs   sheave
                              FBPA = 2F20
                                                     n = 2 cable parts
                                                                         Fixed tackle
                                                                         mount on femur




                                        ΔℓBPA                             Artificial
                                                                          tendon




                                                                                        Tendon travel Δst = nΔℓBPA
                              Femur                                      Ft = FBPA/n

reverse tackle.

4.3.6   Whole-Leg Transmission Capability

As a proof of concept for the whole tool chain, I applied the transmission search to the
robot-leg muscle routing of my master’s model [Bolen and Hunt, 2019]: the expanded
design selects the best transmission configuration at each of the 27 actuator sites (Table C13)
and re-solves actuator and tendon lengths on the inherited routing. Each site is evaluated
against its same-named Gait2392 muscle [Delp et al., 2007, Seth et al., 2018] on a 5 ˆ 5 grid
sampling combinations of two joint coordinates at 620 kPa [Bolen et al., 2026]: a cell meets
the requirement when the taut BPA’s torque has the human torque’s sign and at least its
magnitude, and the reported coverage is the unweighted mean over sites. The baseline
substitutes four parallel 20 mm BPAs at the eight nominal 40 mm sites. Under matched
ideal pressure, braid geometry, and contraction, maximum force scales with diameter
squared, giving this parallel group the same theoretical maximum force as one 40 mm BPA.
The comparison combines transmission, actuator sizing, and the diameter substitution; it
does not isolate any one contribution.
   Mean human-torque coverage improves from 37.28 % to 65.19 %, improving at 23 sites
and tying at four, while mean active-force coverage (positive insertion tension, regardless
of torque) rises from 92.44 % to 98.81 %; positive tension alone does not meet the torque
requirement. Table 4.12 summarizes the means, and Table C14 gives the per-site coverage.

  Table 4.12: Mean torque and active-force coverage across 27 actuator sites at 620 kPa.

        Configuration                    Human torque met (%)       Active force (%)
        Baseline                                  37.28                   92.44
        Transmission configurations               65.19                   98.81
                                                                                    116

   With the actuator side of the leg corrected, physically validated, and redesigned to
meet human-scale torque, the dissertation turns to the sensory side: Chapter 5 assembles
the measured afferent responses that the synthetic nervous system of Chapter 6 draws on.
                                                                                           117




                            5   The Sensory Afferent Database


The Sensory Afferent Database (SADb) is a curated literature resource for studying sensory
feedback in locomotion and posture. It links findings from animal and human studies to
afferent classes, feedback pathways, and computational models. I developed it within
the AARL’s NeuroNex Communication, Coordination, and Control in Nervous Systems
(C3NS) effort. The resource and the evaluation of AI-assisted literature curation were
presented at Neuroscience 2023 and 2024, respectively [Bolen et al., 2023, Bolen and Hunt,
2024].
   SADb addresses the need to recover measured afferent properties, feedback pathways,
and experimental conditions from a dispersed literature. A modeler needs to know what
was measured, how a pathway was stimulated or represented, and the locomotor or
postural state in which it was studied. SADb brings individual studies into a searchable
collection so that researchers can compare findings, locate evidence relevant to a candidate
feedback pathway, and follow each record to its source.
   The primary deliverable is SADb Explorer, a browser application containing 1,393
literature and related records. It has four main tabs (Figure 5.1, callout (A)): Table searches
individual records, Pivot compares classifications, Bubble map explores citation links
among papers, and Search (online) finds candidate additions through OpenAlex. The
records, notes, and citation graph are embedded in the application, so the first three tabs
can be used without an internet connection. Search (online) requires an internet connection
                                                                                          118

and lets the user save candidate records for later curation. The application preserves both
the records retained for active curation and archived records from the collected material.
      The database was curated in Airtable, an online database system, before being trans-
ferred to SADb Explorer. Linked tables connect papers to feedback pathways and com-
putational models, with separate records for reviews and recording methods. Paper
records contain bibliographic information, curation notes, and classification tags; path-
way and model records link these sources to candidate feedback mechanisms and their
implementations.


5.1    Curation Methods

This was a curated research collection, rather than a registered systematic review or an
exhaustive search of every relevant publication. I first collected records from selected
AARL and personal Zotero collections used for previous papers on locomotion and pos-
tural balance, together with a few records already curated in Airtable by my advisor, Dr.
Hunt. Duplicate Zotero entries describing the same publication were combined into a
single record using DOI and title matching. I expanded the collection through keyword
searches, references cited by relevant papers, and searches for subsequent papers citing
them.
      I initially imported the broader Zotero collections as groups, then narrowed the working
base to sensory feedback in locomotion and posture. I retained records reporting sensory-
afferent measurements, investigating feedback in locomotion or posture, or modeling
a relevant sensory pathway. I removed records outside this scope from the working
                                                         B Map controls
