papers (F), and the topic-cluster color key (G).
                                                                                                              119
                                                                                        120

base. I also flagged records whose source text I could not locate, including conference
posters found through ResearchRabbit, and removed them from that base. The application
preserves the broader collection as archived records, which users can distinguish with the
archive filter. For retained papers, I recorded the animal or human study context where
applicable, afferent class, feedback pathway, and the mechanism described by the study.
      I implemented a monthly literature watch that queries OpenAlex and Crossref for
candidate additions matching selected authors and keywords. The script removes DOI
and version duplicates, verifies shortlisted DOIs through Crossref, and writes a shortlist
for manual curation. Source-access limitations are recorded with the shortlist; inclusion
and classification decisions remain manual.


5.2    Results: Application and Corpus Coverage

The application contains 1,393 entries in five record categories: 943 Papers, 247 Models,
157 Review Papers, 42 Feedback pathways, and 4 Recorders. The searchable table includes
the complete 1,393-entry collection. The Pivot and Bubble map tabs use the 943 entries in
the Papers category. Archived records remain searchable in the application.
      Within the application’s Papers category, containing 943 entries, 833 carry curation
notes, 606 have tags for the animals or human participants studied, 316 carry animal-study
assessments, 119 link to feedback pathways, 95 carry an afferent classification, and 363
carry robot-or-simulation translation notes. DOI identifiers are present for 892 papers, and
318 have attached-PDF indicators. Within the Papers category, 730 papers are connected
by 11,130 directed citation pairs. The Louvain algorithm groups these papers by their
                                                                                          121

citation connections into 18 named topic clusters (Figure 5.1, callout (G)), which help users
navigate related studies in the Bubble map tab.
   The Table tab combines text search with filters for animal or human study category,
pathway, afferent class, record type, archive status, year, and note or PDF availability
(Figure 5.2). Selecting a record opens its source information, curation notes, and notes on
how the reported findings could inform a robot or simulation. The Search (online) tab
queries OpenAlex for candidate additions and marks DOIs already represented in SADb.
   The Bubble map tab offers two selectable layouts (Figure 5.1). The year–citations
layout places publication year on the horizontal axis and citation count on the vertical
axis. The topic landscape instead draws papers as nodes joined by citation links. A
spring-layout algorithm balances attraction along links with repulsion between nodes
to arrange connected groups together. Its coordinates arrange the network; neither axis
measures publication year or citation count. The user controls the layout, display style,
record grouping, citation-neighborhood depth, and click action (Figure 5.1, callout (B)).
   Selecting a paper makes it the reference for the displayed citation neighborhood; the
application calls this the focus paper. Its source information appears in the left record area
(Figure 5.1, callout (C)), together with its curation note and notes on possible applications
to a robot or simulation (Figure 5.1, callout (D)). The neighborhood selector displays papers
up to one, two, or three citation links from the focus paper, following either its references
or papers that cite it (Figure 5.1, callout (E)). A two-link neighborhood adds one level
beyond the directly connected papers. Directly connected papers retain color, while papers
reached through additional links are gray. In the illustrated neighborhood of the Grillner
(1985) focus paper, 57 in-corpus papers cite it, it cites 13 in-corpus papers, and 555 papers
                                                                                       122




Figure 5.2: SADb Table view after a text search (top) and the selected record’s detail view
(bottom), showing its source information, linked records, and curation note.
                                                                                           123

lie within two links. Green triangles identify papers that cite the focus paper, and red
circles identify papers cited by it.
   The connection list separates papers citing the focus paper from its references (Fig-
ure 5.1, callout (F)). Selecting a listed paper makes it the new focus paper, allowing a reader
to follow the development of an idea without repeatedly searching for individual titles.
The reference-only and cited-by views isolate either direction, and the detail action opens
the underlying record. A researcher can therefore start from a known experiment, inspect
earlier studies supporting it, follow subsequent models or experiments citing it, and read
the associated translation notes before choosing a candidate pathway for simulation.
   The Pivot tab counts papers by two selected dimensions, including topic cluster, decade,
primary author, animal or human study category, feedback pathway, and afferent classifica-
tion. Figure 5.3 crosses afferent classes with categories of animal and human studies: each
cell counts papers carrying both tags. Rows and columns can be ordered alphabetically
or by count. Selecting a cell opens the corresponding records in the Table tab, and a
return control restores the pivot. The pivot is computed over the 943 entries in the Papers
category, including archived entries, rather than all 1,393 records. Because a paper can
carry several animal or afferent tags, it can contribute to several cells. The counts describe
the distribution of curated records, not independent experimental observations or the
strength of evidence for a pathway.
                                                                                        124




Figure 5.3: SADb Explorer pivot table by afferent classification and animal or human study
category. Each cell counts papers carrying both tags and links to those records.


   The feedback-pathway records extend beyond the mammalian core of Table E2: paired
excitation and inhibition records cover the chordotonal organs and trochanteral hair plates
of insect legs, a load-feedback record covers the campaniform sensilla, and further records
hold fictive locomotion without sensory feedback (12 linked papers), group III/IV fatigue,
and one paper each for somatosensory, vestibular, and visual contributions to postural
control. The controller of Chapter 6 implements the mammalian subset.


5.2.1   AI-Assisted Literature Triage

I evaluated AI-assisted literature identification, summarization, and categorization. This
work was presented at Neuroscience 2024 [Bolen and Hunt, 2024]. The comparison in-
cludes ResearchRabbit, Litmaps, txyz, SciSpace, and MirrorThink. Some tools helped iden-
tify related papers or summarize straightforward material, but categorization remained
inadequate: none received a good rating, and only txyz was rated adequate. Complex
models and nuanced physiological findings required substantial human correction. The
tools therefore offered limited help with the central task of accurately assigning papers to
                                                                                          125

database categories. I made the final inclusion, classification, and removal decisions.


5.3     Discussion

5.3.1    Uses of the Database

SADb helps researchers connect an account of sensory feedback to the experiments and
models supporting it. Searchable notes, linked records, and citation neighborhoods pro-
vide complementary routes through a literature spread across animal studies, human
studies, and modeling disciplines. A reader can begin with a familiar paper, follow its
references and later citing studies, and compare the conditions under which a feedback
mechanism was observed. This makes the resource useful for learning a research area and
for identifying questions that require a closer reading of the primary evidence.
      The database also supports the design of neural models for locomotion and balance.
Table E1 in Appendix E summarizes afferent classes, receptors, and encoded signals for
selecting model inputs. Table E2 gives representative feedback pathways, their actions in
the step cycle, and the studies linked to those records. These source links let a modeler
examine the experimental basis for a candidate connection and the assumptions needed to
implement it.
      Experiments and models answer complementary questions. Ting and Macpherson
[2005] linked muscle-activation synergies to limb endpoint-force vectors in cats responding
to support-surface translations. Latash et al. [2020] compared cat stepping patterns with a
mathematical model to investigate how rhythm-generator operation could change with
control drive under tied-belt and split-belt conditions. The first study connects measured
                                                                                         126

muscle activity to postural output; the second tests a candidate circuit explanation for
locomotor phase patterns. Following the source papers exposes these differences in task,
preparation, and explanation.
   Closed-loop models also show why a neural mechanism must be considered with
its body. Geyer and Herr [2010] generated walking in a human musculoskeletal model
using phase-dependent muscle reflexes. In a rat neuromechanical model, Deng et al.
[2022] found that biomechanical entrainment and sensory feedback reduced behavioral
differences associated with alternative CPG parameter choices. Model records provide
starting points for comparing these approaches and two-level rhythm-generation and
pattern-formation architectures [Rybak et al., 2006]. For the controllers in Chapter 6,
pathway selection is followed by explicit choices and tests of receptor dynamics, gains,
delays, and species-specific assumptions.
   Robotic tests can also return predictions to biology. Ijspeert et al. [2007] implemented a
spinal-cord model in a salamander robot that switched between walking and swimming
as control drive changed. The model predicted lower intrinsic frequencies for limb os-
cillators than for body oscillators, and the authors presented biological data supporting
that prediction. This exchange illustrates the broader motivation for the humanoid project:
biological evidence informs a controllable model, whose behavior can generate questions
for subsequent biological experiments.
   The balance, posture, and multisensory-recalibration clusters provide entry points for
the vestibular and visual extensions proposed in Section 7.2.4. Searches of existing animal
studies let users compare the conditions and tasks behind a proposed feedback rule before
testing it in a robotic or computational model. Reusing this evidence for model-based tests
                                                                                      127

supports the aim of reducing reliance on further animal experimentation (Section 2.10).
   The application makes physiological evidence accessible for controller design, but
selecting a pathway is only the beginning of a neuromechanical experiment. Its encoding,
connection sign, gain, and interaction with the body must still be implemented and tested.
Chapter 6 develops the models and simulation tools needed to make that next step explicit.
                                                                                         128




                        6   Tools for Neuromechanical Simulation


I first transferred a published neural controller to a different biomechanical model and
adjusted it by hand. The combined model produced alternating neural activity and air
stepping, but not weight-bearing walking. This result motivated more efficient ways to
test alternative controllers, first on the complex Gait2392 human model and then on a
robot counterpart with BPAs. This chapter presents the resulting neuromechanical models,
graphical neural blocks, simulation interfaces, and controlled experiments.


6.1    The AnimatLab Bipedal Walker

For a course project in ME 610: Neuromechanical Modeling, Morrow and I combined the
bipedal mechanics of Li et al. [2017] with the two-level central pattern generator (CPG) of
Deng et al. [2019] in AnimatLab [Cofer et al., 2010]. The mechanical model had a pelvis
and bilateral thigh, shank, foot, and toe segments, with sagittal-plane joints and antagonist
Hill-type muscles at the hip, knee, and ankle. I added a passive toe joint. The initial
controller used one rhythm generator (RG) to control both sides, with hip and knee/ankle
pattern-formation (PF) groups for each leg. We added afferent feedback at the motoneuron
(MN) level. Figure 6.1 shows the model and controller organization.
      We adjusted applied currents, synaptic weights, and muscle parameters by hand. The
suspended model developed alternating muscle activity and air stepping, presented at
the 2021 NIH BRAIN Initiative Investigators Meeting [Bolen and Hunt, 2021]. It did not
                                                                                        129

achieve weight-bearing walking.
      I subsequently replaced the shared rhythm generator with one rhythm generator on
each side and linked the two through commissural pathways. The bilateral pattern-
formation and motor organization was already present in the original model. I added
afferent feedback to the RG and PF layers, and heel- and toe-contact signals during
the subsequent development. This later model is referred to as the Walker Two-Layer
(W2L) model. Joint recordings from a separate instrumented AnimatLab configuration
are provided in Figure D2; further circuit schematics and implementation records are in
Appendix D.
      I found AnimatLab’s graphical block editor usable but dated. I wanted a more efficient
way to modify connections, try alternative controllers, and evaluate their interaction with
the many joints and actuators of the Gait2392 model and its BPA-routed robot counterpart.


6.2    Attempting W2L Integration in MATLAB

I later explored MATLAB as one route to those controller experiments, using Simulink for
the neural controller and Simscape Multibody for the mechanical model. This required in-
terfaces for neural activation, muscle force, joint motion, and sensory feedback. Importing
a mechanism and obtaining neural oscillation separately did not establish the behavior of
their coupled model.
      The MATLAB work therefore became a sequence of component and coupling tests.
Selected neural implementations reproduced their reference responses, and mechanical
import provided models that could be assembled and simulated. More extensive tests also
                                                                                 130




(A)




(B)




Figure 6.1: Original AnimatLab walker (A) and shared-rhythm-generator controller (B),
developed by Morrow and Bolen. The four-neuron RG drives hip and knee/ankle pattern-
formation groups on both sides.
                                                                                       131

exposed limits: a full-network export agreed with its reference over an early transient but
later diverged, and one Simulink realization reached a steady, nonoscillating state under
constant neural drive while its NumPy reference oscillated (Appendix D.2). Those results
did not establish a complete W2L walking implementation in MATLAB. They showed
why importing a model or reproducing a short trace could not substitute for testing the
neural dynamics, actuator action, and feedback loop separately.
      I subsequently developed both block-programmed and scripted implementations,
allowing controller experiments through SNS-Simscape or MuJoCo with SNS-Toolbox.


6.3    Knowledge Gaps and the New Tools

Transferring a controller between biomechanical models changes its sensory and actuator
interactions. A circuit that produces air stepping may require different feedback and
coordination under load. I needed to vary neural organization and actuator mechanics
while retaining identifiable model, input, and support conditions. Table 6.1 summarizes
the models and tools developed for these comparisons.
      OpenSim supplied musculoskeletal geometry, MyoConverter translated models to
MuJoCo, SNS-Toolbox supplied neural formulations, and Simulink/Simscape supplied
graphical neural and mechanical modeling [Delp et al., 2007, Caggiano et al., 2022, Todorov
et al., 2012, Nourse et al., 2023]. My contributions are the model implementations, custom
blocks, actuator integrations, and evaluation procedures built with those packages.

6.4     Methods and Verification of Software Tools

Figure 6.2 shows the model-construction routes and controller interfaces. Chapter 7 places
them within the broader simulation-to-hardware workflow (Figure 7.4).
      The comparisons below report circuit responses, coupled motion, and the effects of
controlled interventions in the order of Table 6.1. Detailed configurations and evaluation
sets are provided in Appendix D.


6.4.1    Li–Deng/W2L Walker and Controller

For cross-implementation checks of the contact-driven W2L rhythm core, I used a com-
mon connectivity specification and identical drive and contact inputs in NumPy and
Simulink. Closed-loop experiments held a saved controller fixed while changing selected
sensory pathways, drive, or support. Appendix B.5 documents the bilateral pathways and
conductances of the independently replayed W2L configuration.
Verification. The contact-driven W2L rhythm core was implemented in NumPy and native
Simulink blocks from a common connectivity specification. Both implementations received
the same input protocol. Burst periods matched at 1.000 s, all ten expected bursts were
reproduced, and voltage-trace correlation was at least 0.99996 (Figure 6.3). The agreement
is specific to this circuit and input protocol.
      Removing the phasic heel-contact inputs caused the circuit to latch in both implemen-
tations. The alternating activity therefore depends on the specified sensory drive.

                                                                                       135

   The saved W2L controller was tested with all saved effective gains delivered to the
compiled network. With controller, drive, and support settings fixed, disabling all sensory
feedback shifted median bilateral contact phase from 0.498 to 0.223 cycles. A phase of 0.5
cycles indicates alternating foot loading; the supported-stepping criterion accepts 0.35–
0.65 cycles. Forward progression and joint cycling persisted, but the feedback-disabled
condition failed this criterion for supported stepping (Figure 6.4). Over the 4.5 s to 30 s
scored window, forward displacement was 2.239 m with feedback and 1.996 m without it.
Thus, the demonstrated feedback contribution is to bilateral contact coordination, rather
than the existence of oscillation or forward motion. Additional pathway deletions and
stimulus tests are summarized in Appendix D.5.2 (Figure D9).

mechanics.

6.4.2   Gait2392-Based Models

My first conversion attempt used the robot model, which combined BPA routing on the
Gait2392 skeleton with unchanged human muscle parameters. MyoConverter’s muscle-
equilibrium checks rejected infeasible combinations of routed length and muscle parame-
ters. I therefore separated conversion of the human reference from reconstruction of the
robot routing. This distinction matters because an error caused by an artificial-actuator
                                                                                        138

route should not be interpreted as a property of the original human model.
   The human-model conversion also required specific repairs. Three small hip rotators
per side, the quadratus femoris, gemelli, and piriformis, were disabled at model load.
The converted model retains 92 actuator entries, but these six contribute no force. The
converted knee retains the OpenSim flexion-negative sign convention. Spatial frame
transformations and individual-actuator tests check joint motion and torque directions;
the CAD-derived model uses the anatomical frame specified in Appendix D.5.4.
   The human-model repairs addressed coupled moving muscle-path points, patella
tracking, joint limits, and unintended contacts. Conflicting limits were removed from
coupled path-point joints while anatomical driver limits were retained; rectus femoris
required a patella-tracking via point, and contact was restricted to intended foot–ground
pairs. These choices restore specified geometry and contact behavior rather than establish
general model equivalence. Appendix D.6 records the repairs and force comparison.
   Comparisons with the OpenSim attachment paths and single-actuator tests checked
the transferred geometry and force directions, though not that every muscle reproduces
the source force throughout its range. The neural and mechanical models exchange signals
at a 2 ms solver time step.
   For artificial-muscle simulations, I implemented an actuator-force calculation that eval-
uates the quasi-static BPA model from Chapter 3 at the current simulated actuator length
and commanded pressure. The calculated tensile force acts along the model’s specified
tendon route. Length, velocity, force, and foot-contact measurements are then returned
to the neural controller through sensory channels. This closes the neural-command–
actuator-force–sensory-feedback loop. The calculation represents the measured static force
                                                                                        139

dependence; it does not add valve-flow or pressure-transient dynamics. Routing geometry
and dynamic force transmission therefore require their own checks beyond the isometric
force fit.
    The SADb of Chapter 5 supplies the physiological context for selecting Ia, II, Ib, and
contact pathways. A database link identifies the supporting observation; it does not
by itself determine a numerical gain or guarantee the same function in another model.
The implemented encoding, connection sign, phase gating, and tested response therefore
remain explicit parts of the controller specification.
    The human Gait2392 reference retains biological-muscle actuators, while the robot coun-
terpart uses custom artificial-muscle paths and BPA forces. The Li–Deng/W2L walker and
the SolidWorks-derived robot are separate mechanical models. Figure D3 contrasts the con-
verted Gait2392 robot with the reduced-order knee model used for block demonstrations.
Appendix D distinguishes their configurations and support conditions.
Verification. The conversion checks test the quantities needed to interpret a neural-control
experiment: the direction of each muscle’s joint action, the coupled motion of path points,
the force scale, and the intended ground contacts. They exposed reversed hip actions,
inconsistent path-point limits, unintended contact pairs, and incorrect force scales in
individual actuator entries. Correcting those faults made the converted human model
usable for the subsequent controller tests. Appendix D.6 gives the individual repairs and
the full force comparison.
    At matched poses and OpenSim static-optimization activations (Figure D10; Table D10),
eight of twelve sampled muscles had force-prediction R2 between 0.87 and 0.98 and
mean forces within 10 % of the reference. Rectus femoris and biceps femoris short head
                                                                                      140

had negative R2 , indicating errors greater than a constant mean-force prediction. This
comparison checks force predictions in the repaired MyoConverter-derived human model
while identifying remaining muscle-specific differences. Because the comparison sets
pose and activation frame by frame without reproducing trajectory velocities or tendon
dynamics, it does not establish dynamic equivalence between the muscle formulations.
That distinction matters when interpreting feedback based on muscle force.
   The Gait2392-based evaluations use the same reference-library and pathway-removal
procedures to compare alternative neural organizations. The full graded, pruned, six-
synergy, and W2L-layout configurations show different responses to feedback removal and
different agreement with the tuning and library references. Removing Ia feedback elimi-
nated detected cycles in the tested production configuration, whereas other removals and
other network organizations had different effects. The library comparisons also exposed
asymmetric activity and limited transfer beyond the tuning reference (Appendix D.3.1).
This demonstrates that the models and experiment drivers can discriminate controller
dependence and transfer limits; the comparison does not establish normal human gait.
   A replay of the saved human-model controller produced ten detected cycles per leg.
Figure 6.5 compares the joint trajectories directly with the Gait2392 inverse-kinematics
reference and its associated ground-reaction forces. The model’s mean stance fractions
were 0.71 and 0.65 on the right and left, compared with 0.61 in the reference. Substantial
hip and knee timing differences remain visible despite the detected cycles. The overlay
therefore complements the scalar gait score by showing which parts of the movement
disagree. Appendix D.3.2 reports the angle errors and alignment method.

6.4.3   SNS-Simscape Block Library and Editor

The SNS-Simscape library implements neuron, synapse, afferent-encoder, activation, and
muscle-force elements as editable Simulink blocks. The library retains the conductance-
based neuron and synapse formulation supplied by SNS-Toolbox; it does not introduce a
new neuron model. Verification uses selected circuits under matched inputs. Figure 6.6
shows the core blocks and graded-neuron signal flow.
   The library also supports reduced-order demonstrations in which the sensory pathway
and mechanical response can be inspected directly.
Verification. The library assembles Ia stretch excitation, reciprocal inhibition, and Ib
autogenic inhibition in a reduced-order antagonist knee model (Figure 6.7C). After the
model reaches a steady operating point, a 2 nA, 30 ms current pulse at the extensor Ia
sensory-neuron input evokes 1.09˝ of extension, peaking 111 ms after pulse onset. The knee
then returns toward the matched no-stimulus trajectory, meeting the specified recovery
thresholds after 6.355 s. The displayed configuration broadens the MN and Ia synaptic
saturation ramps; the original narrow-ramp settings maintained oscillations. This is a
transient test of the implemented feedback and actuator equations, rather than a physiolog-
ically calibrated tendon-tap model. Appendix D.2.1 gives the configuration and numerical
checks, with the complete response in Figure D4.
                                                                                    143

   A second demonstration tests feedback in an arm holding a beverage container as it
is loaded (Figure 6.8). With the reflex enabled, arm sag was 2.00˝ , compared with 7.85˝
when it was disabled. As the forearm sags, biceps stretch feedback excites the biceps
motoneuron and reciprocally inhibits the triceps motoneuron. The separate Ib pathways
provide autogenic inhibition of each muscle. The reduced sag demonstrates a corrective
effect of the implemented sensory pathways in the coupled neural and actuator blocks.

6.4.4   SOLIDWORKS-Derived Robot Assembly

I imported OpenSim-derived and SolidWorks-derived mechanisms into Simscape Multi-
body and coupled the SolidWorks robot assembly to a neural controller and BPA actuators
using my force model. The separately assembled MuJoCo CAD model was evaluated
under specified mechanical assistance and actuator control (Appendix D.5.4).
Verification. A newer CAD-model comparison tests whether additional abductor control
prevents the legs from crossing (Figure 6.9). Under the same assistance settings, muscle-
mediated separation feedback changed reversed ankle ordering from 67 % of sampled
states to zero. The model uses a layered SNS for timing together with an engineering force
allocator. Its MN target-current compensation cancels incoming PF and reflex currents,
so this comparison demonstrates supported model integration and a separation-control
effect, rather than full neural pattern control. Appendix D.5.4 gives the matched measures
and foot-mount sensitivity.
   A separate CAD-derived model completed a coupled stance demonstration under
substantial rig support and reference actuator tensions. Appendix D.5.4 documents the
support and actuator conditions.
                                                                                       148




Figure 6.9: Anterior (A) and oblique (B) views of the same recorded CAD-model state.
Right and left legs are blue and gold; selected hip-abducting routes are green. The 75 kg
model uses 54 reference-tension actuators with hybrid neural and postural control. Vertical
assistance supplies 80% of body weight in this interval; lateral and angular assistance re-
main active. Attachments, the welded foot mount, and passive knee stops are provisional.

6.4.5   MuJoCo–Simulink Integration

The MathWorks MuJoCo–Simulink bridge provides an alternative to importing the me-
chanical model into Simscape. I connected SNS-Simscape neural outputs to the bridge’s
actuator-command interface and returned muscle length, velocity, force, and joint measure-
ments to the controller. This arrangement allows a block-programmed neural controller
to act on an existing MuJoCo model. The integration tests compare activation and sensor
readback against a Python reference before interpreting the coupled motion. The bridge
supplies the simulation interface; the neural and actuator integrations and their verification
are the work described here.
Verification. The MathWorks MuJoCo–Simulink bridge exchanges activation commands
and mechanical measurements between a Simulink controller and an existing MuJoCo
mechanical model. I paired this bridge with SNS-Simscape and tested single-muscle acti-
vation, different control rates, and sensor readback against a Python integration. Readback
was bit-exact over the scored window. An exported neural network also drove all 92
actuator channels during a 5 s run without afferent return; a separate closed-loop reflex
experiment exercised both commands and sensory measurements. These tests verify
signal exchange through the interface. Under constant neural drive, one Simulink circuit
realization reached a steady, nonoscillating state while its NumPy reference oscillated,
showing why successful signal exchange must be followed by circuit-specific dynamical
checks before transferring tuned parameters.
                                                                                       150


6.4.6   Curriculum and Experiment Drivers

The larger models require a tuning procedure as well as a working interface. I organized
the evaluations as a staged curriculum, beginning with suspended rhythm generation,
adding sensory feedback and standing requirements, and progressing to supported ground
evaluations (Table 6.2). This ordering separates a failure to generate rhythmic commands
from a failure to support or coordinate the mechanical model. Free network gains, which
scale connection strengths and sensory contributions, were searched with a tree-structured
Parzen estimator. This sequential search uses earlier simulation results to propose new
parameter combinations; each candidate is then evaluated through simulation. Saved
configurations and fixed evaluation protocols allow the candidate to be replayed before a
new condition is introduced.
   The experiment drivers then hold a selected controller fixed while changing sensory
pathways, drive, mechanical support, or external perturbations. Finalists are also replayed
under small parameter perturbations. Gait scoring requires detected cycles before a score
is interpreted as movement quality; unsuccessful runs cannot be promoted by a reserved
numerical failure value. The reference-library comparison tests transfer beyond the tuning
reference. These procedures provide a repeatable way to identify where a controller works
and which changes disrupt it, rather than making a single favorable trajectory the endpoint
of tuning. Detailed scoring and configuration records are in Appendix D.3.
Verification. A separate W2L configuration reproduced its supported-stepping trajectory
in four independent replays, and all twelve reruns with 1 % parameter perturbations
retained supported stepping. This demonstrates that a saved controller can be replayed
                                                                                          151

Table 6.2: Five-stage controller-tuning curriculum. Each selected controller seeds the next
stage. Air-stepping stages are suspended; ground stages retain mechanical support. The
table describes the training tasks and evaluation measures.

Stage     Training task             Condition introduced           What is evaluated
  1       Deafferented air          Legs suspended; sensory        Rhythm generation and swing
          stepping                  feedback disabled              depth
  2       Afferented air stepping   Heel, toe, and load feedback   Rhythm and swing with
                                    enabled                        sensory feedback
  3       Standing balance          Standing posture and push      Ability to maintain posture
                                    perturbations                  during a push
  4       Ground walking            Ground contact and             Joint kinematics against a
                                    forward-progression task       human reference cycle
  5      Contact-driven ground      Contact-driven pathway         Joint kinematics against a
         walking                    variant                        human reference cycle


and tested in its local parameter neighborhood. The repeatability tests and the pathway-
removal panels use separately specified support settings; their event counts are therefore
reported separately. Appendix D.5.1 retains the event counts, bilateral phase, displacement,
speed, and support accounting.
      Mechanical support remains part of these results. Removing a constant weight-support
term did not remove the position-control harness, and contact duty remained below the
strict walking criterion. No harness-free walking was demonstrated.


6.4.7    Literature Circuits and Spiking Variants

Selected literature-derived circuits were tested with matched inputs and layer-specific
stimuli before interpreting coupled motion. The reduced Rybak circuit was tested without
mechanics; the Deng-derived circuit drove a suspended W2L model. Spiking variants
retained a continuous motor-activation interface while changing the neural dynamics. Ap-
                                                                                          152

pendix B gives their equations and calibration, and Appendix D.4 gives the comparisons.
Verification. A complementary neural-only test distinguishes mechanisms that can pro-
duce a missing motor burst. In the reduced Rybak implementation, pattern-formation
stimulation suppresses flexor motor output while the rhythm generator continues; rhythm-
generator stimulation shifts the subsequent phase. Reproducing that distinction demon-
strates the use of controlled interventions to test circuit organization before adding me-
chanical coupling (Appendix D.2.2).
   A missing motor burst can arise from a disturbance of the rhythm generator or from
suppression farther downstream. I applied stimuli separately to the RG, PF, and MN
layers of a reduced Deng-derived circuit [Deng et al., 2019, Nourse et al., 2023]. A resetting
deletion suppresses expected motor activity and shifts the subsequent rhythm; a non-
resetting deletion suppresses activity while preserving the original schedule. Matched
baseline and stimulus runs distinguish the rhythm-generator clock from the motor output.
   Stimuli lasting two baseline periods suppressed two expected flexor motor bursts at
each layer. RG stimulation shifted both RG and motor timing by 0.211 cycles, whereas
PF stimulation and direct MN inhibition preserved the original schedule. A separate
one-second PF stimulus shifted motor timing by 0.208 cycles while leaving the RG clock
unchanged. Thus, a motor phase shift alone did not establish resetting of the rhythm
generator. The smaller-current sweep produced no observable deletions. Appendix D.2.3
gives the protocols and all stronger-current controls; Figure D5 shows representative
neural responses. These are controlled tests of a translated circuit coupled to a suspended
model, rather than a replication of the original rat’s locomotion.
                                                                                        153

      Spiking-neuron comparisons preserved a continuous motor-activation interface while
changing rhythm timing and, in some runs, limb coordination (Appendix D).


6.5    Discussion: What the Tools Establish

Matched neural traces support the tested circuit and input protocol. Successful command
and sensor exchange through the bridge establishes the interface needed to couple models,
while the fixed-controller W2L comparison identifies a sensory-feedback contribution to
bilateral contact coordination. The loaded-arm comparison gives a directly interpretable
corrective response in the coupled neural and actuator blocks. These results answer
different questions and should be interpreted at their respective scales.
      The implemented workflow supports controlled circuit comparisons and virtual-
harness-assisted evaluations of the human and BPA robot models. It makes alternative
controllers accessible to repeated tests with specified mechanics, sensory pathways, and
assistance. Chapter 7 connects these capabilities with the actuator characterization, torque
models, and SADb, then considers their use in broader robot behavior and hardware
experiments.
                                                                                          154




                                       7   Discussion


7.1    Overview

The research program connects two goals: using biology to improve robots and using
biomimetic robots to investigate biology. Actuator mechanics and neural control must
be considered together if a legged robot is to adapt to uneven terrain, disturbances,
and changing loads. A sufficiently characterized robot also permits controlled changes
to mechanics and sensory pathways that would be impractical or unethical in human
experiments. Prosthetic and orthotic devices provide a further application of the resulting
design and control knowledge.
      This dissertation advances that program through four contributions: BPA force charac-
terization, isometric joint-torque design factors, the Sensory Afferent Database, and tools
for neuromechanical experiments. They address different sources of uncertainty between
a proposed biological mechanism and a robotic test. The force and torque models support
first-pass static sizing of the tested actuator and knee configurations; the database connects
controller choices to published evidence; and the simulation tools make the resulting
coupled systems available for controlled evaluation. Figure 7.1 places these contributions
within the broader development process.
                                                                                              155

7.2     Research Contributions and Future Work

7.2.1    BPA Actuator Characterization

Chapter 3 establishes that the maximum isometric force of Festo BPAs depends on resting
length, a design variable not represented adequately by the manufacturer’s tool or the
compared force models. The resting-length-dependent maximum-force model and three-
coefficient nondimensional force surface provide a basis for selecting actuator length
and predicting force across the measured contractions and pressures. This closes an
actuator-sizing gap; it does not identify the complete dynamic behavior of a pressurized
actuator.


Future Work

The maximum-contraction function remains a useful next target. A predictive relationship
between ε 620 and resting length was not established, so each actuator’s free contraction
must still be measured. Tests across product batches and the 10 mm, 20 mm and 40 mm
diameters could distinguish a repeatable length dependence from batch variation. The
characterized 20 mm actuators came from a single batch.
      Dynamic characterization should distinguish active pressurized-braid force, passive
resistance, and contraction dynamics. Biological muscle models provide a useful organiza-
tional framework for these components [Thelen, 2003, Millard et al., 2013]; Appendix A
gives their constitutive relations. Constant-speed, constant-load, and constant-pressure
experiments would extend the isometric measurements. The pressure–force model of
                                                                                       157

Elzein et al. [2025] supplies a complementary starting point for pulse-modulated actuation.
   Tensile tests with the Onyx and aluminum end caps, under pressurized and unpres-
surized conditions, could test the proposed end-effect explanation for resting-length-
dependent force. A finite-element model of the rubber layers, crossed aramid braid, end
constraints, and contact interfaces would connect that explanation to the construction
described in the Festo patent literature [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2026]. The proposed geometry
in Figure 7.2 provides a starting point for comparing predicted deformation with measured
behavior.


7.2.2   Isometric Joint Design Factors

Chapter 4 shows why actuator force and rigid-body moment arms alone are insuffi-
cient to predict the tested knee torques. Length offsets, effective series compliance, and
wrapping-induced loss of usable actuator length affect the transmitted force. The iden-
tified corrections improve prediction across the tested configurations and support the
redesigned flexor and extensor routes. Their measured capacity meets or exceeds the
selected human-muscle references at the tested knee angles. These results extend the
earlier attachment-placement work [Bolen, 2019, Bolen and Hunt, 2019, Morrow et al.,
2020] from geometric prediction toward experimentally informed joint design.


Future Work

The next mechanical questions concern transfer and packaging. The staggered flexor
paths need physical clearance, load-sharing, and off-axis-load checks before extension to a
                                                                                               158

                                                                                             159

complete leg. Reeved routes require space as well as sufficient torque, and the broader
route search still contains incomplete configurations (Appendix C.8). A cam or artificial
patella offers another way to control moment arm and protect an actuator from joint
contact, but friction and contact transitions must be included when assessing such a design.
The cam result supports further investigation; it does not establish the performance of an
artificial patella.
   The correction set also leaves a mechanical hypothesis to test: an additional force-
path stiffness, χ4 , may explain effects presently absorbed into the orientation-dependent
stiffness pair. This can be investigated through the existing identification framework
without treating the current effective stiffnesses as literal bracket material properties.
   Controlled-speed and controlled-load knee tests would extend the static capacity
measurements to movement. Loading–unloading sequences would characterize hysteresis,
and pressure-step tests would characterize the pneumatic response. A torque-controlled
motor and a less compliant loading connection would reduce winch slack and permit these
tests on the existing rig. These experiments would establish where the isometric model
remains useful and where velocity dependence, damping, or pressure regulation needs an
additional model.


7.2.3   Sensory Afferent Database

Chapter 5 addresses a different gap: selecting sensory feedback requires evidence dis-
tributed across animal studies, human studies, and computational models. SADb makes
that evidence searchable through curation notes, linked records, classifications, and cita-
                                                                                          160

tion neighborhoods. It supports tracing a proposed pathway to its source and comparing
the conditions under which a response was observed. The contribution is the research
collection and its navigation tools, rather than a claim that every record is fully classified
or every proposed feedback rule transfers directly to a robot.


Future Work

The implemented monthly literature watch produces candidate shortlists for manual
curation (Section 5.1). Continued work should complete useful classifications and connect
model assumptions to the primary experiments that support them. Each pathway record
should identify its sensory signal, connection sign, task dependence, and source; each
model record should identify the pathways it implements. Search and summarization
tools can assist this process, while inclusion and interpretation remain research judgments
[Bolen and Hunt, 2024].
   Vestibular, visual, and multisensory contributions to posture provide useful areas for
expansion. Better links between their experimental conditions and modeled mechanisms
would support the sensory extensions below. Source availability should be checked during
curation; records whose sources cannot be located should be removed from the working
research base rather than treated as verified evidence. This is a curation rule, not a claim
that a particular number of existing records has already failed that check.
                                                                                      161


7.2.4   SNS Tools and Neuromechanical Experiments

Chapter 6 begins with a controller-transfer problem: placing an existing neural controller
on a different biomechanical model and tuning it by hand produced stepping-like motion
in the air, but not walking. That experience motivated tools for constructing, inspecting,
tuning, and comparing more complex neural–mechanical systems. The implemented
models, graphical blocks, simulation interfaces, and curriculum support those tasks.
Matched-input tests establish selected implementation agreement, reflex demonstrations
show corrective feedback, and fixed-controller pathway removal tests sensory dependence
in supported stepping.
   The later virtual-harness-assisted evaluations establish coupled balance and locomotor
behaviors while retaining their stated support conditions. Human-model stepping persists
after removal of externally supplied neural drive, in place under the support rig; the
robot model progresses under its harness with reduced drive (Section D.5). Neural-drive
dependence and mechanical-support dependence are different experimental questions.
The balance-platform studies provide a separate hardware precedent for BPA-actuated
balance with pseudo-vestibular feedback [Bolen et al., 2024b,a,c].


Future Work

The next controller-transfer experiments should compare defined neural and mechanical
configurations under matched stimuli and tuning conditions. Stimulation at the rhythm-
generator, pattern-formation, and motoneuron layers can distinguish resetting the neural
clock from suppressing motor output (Section D.2.3). Physiological and modeling studies
                                                                                       162

supply reference phenomena [Selionov et al., 2009, Rybak et al., 2006, Markin et al., 2010,
Shevtsova et al., 2016]. Pathway additions and removals should retain the other settings so
that an observed change can be associated with the intervention. Spiking and non-spiking
controllers require the same care when comparing their responses.


Sensory and Higher-Level Control Vestibular feedback could extend the balance-
platform work using estimates of body orientation and angular velocity [Hilts et al.,
2019, Bolen et al., 2024b]. Visual measurements could complement those estimates and
help correct inertial drift. Their integration should follow the task-dependent sensory
weighting established in the relevant literature, rather than assuming that one channel
always dominates [Akay and Murray, 2021]. Figure 7.3 separates these proposed additions
from the implemented spinal and body-model components.
   Cerebellar and basal-ganglia models offer further questions about adaptation, coor-
dination, and action selection. Multilevel synthetic nervous systems for insect-like legs
[Grummer et al., 2024] and cortico-cerebellar-spinal models of adaptive step length [Chen
et al., 2025] provide starting points. Such extensions should be evaluated for a defined
change in behavior, rather than added solely to make the controller more anatomically
elaborate.


Hardware Transfer Figure 7.4 places the implemented simulation interfaces within the
proposed hardware workflow. The model-construction and controller-editing routes are
detailed in Figure 6.2. Computer-in-the-loop testing would connect the actual embedded
controller to the simulated robot before physical deployment; valve-control interfaces and
treadmill deployment remain integration tasks.

                                                                                        165

      The hardware objective is low-latency joint control on Teensy-class microcontrollers
and neural-network evaluation on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin. Encoders, strain gauges,
pressure sensors, contact sensors, and IMUs would supply the sensory channels. Length
and velocity estimates are needed for Ia/II feedback, while Ib force estimates must account
for actuator length and loading direction. The static force and torque maps could supply
feedforward commands, complemented by pressure–force dynamics [Elzein et al., 2025].
      Physical PAM-biped control [Li et al., 2024] and the transfer of Muscle Mutt’s SNS
controller from MuJoCo to hardware [Lutz, 2025] support further investigation of that route.
They do not validate the present biped. Each new body model, actuator configuration, and
controller combination needs its own geometry, interface, and behavioral checks.


Dynamic Whole-Body Maneuvers Beyond steady locomotion, the program aims at
gait transitions, reactive balance, and dynamic whole-body maneuvers. My longer-term
aspiration is a robot that can perform a kickflip, requiring coordinated extension, aerial
orientation, and controlled landing. That goal connects the actuator and routing questions
to contact sensing, spinal coordination, vestibular estimation, and higher-level adaptation.


7.3    From Design Tools to Neuromechanical Experiments

Together, the four contributions connect mechanical designs and neural hypotheses to
controlled tests. Dynamic characterization, sensory integration, and hardware transfer
could extend this foundation toward autonomous robots and more informative models of
biology. Chapter 8 summarizes the established findings and their implications.
                                                                                         166




                                      8   Conclusion


This dissertation moved the biomimetic humanoid robot project from an unvalidated
design concept to a quantitatively characterized actuator set and experimentally tested
knee-torque models, and it defined the path from those experimental foundations to
neuromechanical control.
   Chapter 3 produced equations for isometric force in Festo 10 mm and 20 mm-diameter
BPAs. The resting-length dependence F620 (l0 ) corrects the manufacturer tool’s opposing
trend at 620 kPa, and the nondimensional surface F˚ (ϵ˚ , P˚ ) uses three diameter-dependent
coefficients. Together, these fits support actuator selection and route evaluation across
resting lengths before prototyping, within the tested isometric conditions.
   At the joint level (Chapter 4), the BPA model was combined with transmission correc-
tions for effective length, series compliance, and wrapping. Identification on the pinned
knee improved torque prediction, and the shared correction terms also improved predic-
tions on the biomimetic four-bar knee. The corrected model supports route redesign: the
optimized two-BPA flexor and cam-extensor configurations are predicted to meet their
human-scaled torque targets across the design range. The isometric tests demonstrate
that the redesigned configurations meet or exceed their selected human-muscle reference
torques at all fifteen flexor and nine extensor measurement points. This establishes capacity
at the sampled angles and pressures, while agreement with pressure-specific predictions
remains uneven.
                                                                                      167

   The SADb application in Chapter 5 makes physiological evidence searchable by sensory
pathway, animal or human study context, and modeling relevance. Linked records,
curation notes, and citation navigation connect findings to their sources. This resource
helps researchers compare evidence and select pathways for neuromechanical models.
   The tools in Chapter 6 turn selected pathways into testable neural–mechanical mod-
els: the Li–Deng/W2L and Gait2392 implementations, SNS-Simscape blocks, MuJoCo–
Simulink integration, and staged tuning and intervention procedures. Demonstrations
establish matched-circuit agreement, corrective feedback, command and measurement
exchange, and pathway-dependent stepping under specified support. These tests pro-
vide a repeatable framework for comparing neural and mechanical hypotheses. General
controller transfer and independent balance remain open.
   These actuator and joint models, the sensory-afferent application, and the neuromechan-
ical tools make design assumptions testable: force against measurement, routing against
torque requirements, and sensory pathways against controlled changes in movement. The
future studies in Chapter 7 extend these tests toward dynamic actuation, independent
balance, and hardware. This work provides an experimental foundation for biomimetic
robot design and for studying how neural control interacts with mechanical structure.

APPENDICES

                Appendix A: Muscle Force and Joint Torque Calculations


This appendix documents the reference implementation of the force, torque, and compli-
ance equations used in Chapters 3 and 4. When present in MATLAB code, file names are
given so that any number in this dissertation can be traced to the code that produced it.


A.1     Human muscle force calculations

A.1.1    OpenSim Thelen2003 and Millard2012 Formulations

The Thelen2003 and Millard2012 equilibrium muscle models describe active and passive
fiber force, pennation, and a series tendon [Thelen, 2003, Millard et al., 2013]. Their
common force balance is


                   FT (lT ) = FM (l M , l˙M , a) cos α,     l MT = lT + l M cos α,       (A1)


where a is activation, α is pennation, and l M , lT , and l MT are fiber, tendon, and musculo-
tendon lengths. The cosine projects fiber force onto the tendon direction. When tendon
compliance is enabled, fiber length is a state determined by the equilibrium calculation
rather than the full musculotendon path length.
   In the damped Millard formulation, fiber force has the form


                        FM = F0 a f L (l˜M ) f V (ṽ M ) + f PE (l˜M ) + βṽ M ,
                                                                             
                                                                                         (A2)
                                                                                        188

with normalized fiber length l˜M , normalized velocity ṽ M , maximum isometric force F0 ,
and damping coefficient β. The active, passive, and tendon curves and the treatment of
singular configurations differ between the Thelen and Millard models. The OpenSim
implementation can also disable tendon compliance or activation dynamics, so the model
class alone does not establish which dynamics were exercised in an analysis [Millard et al.,
2013].


Thelen activation and contraction dynamics

The following seven relations correspond, in order, to Equations (1)–(7) of Thelen [2003].
They specify the activation state, passive and active force–length curves, tendon force, and
fiber velocity. Bars denote normalization by maximum isometric fiber force F0M or optimal
fiber length L0M , as appropriate.
   Activation follows
                                           da    u´a
                                              =            ,                           (A3)
                                           dt   τa ( a, u)

with an activation-dependent time constant

                                         $

                                         &τact (0.5 + 1.5a),
                                         ’                        u ą a,
                          τa ( a, u) =                                                 (A4)
                                         ’
                                         %τdeact /(0.5 + 1.5a),
                                         ’                        u ď a.


Here u P [0, 1] is neural excitation, a is muscle activation, and τact and τdeact set the
activation and deactivation time scales.

   Here F̄ M is the normalized active fiber force entering the force–velocity relation,
                                               M is the maximum shortening-speed scale.
V M = L̇ M is negative during shortening, and Vmax
Table A1 defines the curve parameters. A shape factor is a dimensionless parameter con-
trolling a curve’s curvature or width; it is not a stiffness in N/m. For example, increasing
γ broadens the active force–length curve while preserving its peak at L̄ M = 1. Changing
k PE changes the passive curve’s shape while retaining unit force at strain ε M
                                                                              0 .
                                                                                              190

         Table A1: Length, strain, and shape parameters in the Thelen formulation.

Symbol            Definition
L̄ M = L M /L0M   Fiber length relative to optimal fiber length.
εM 0              Passive fiber strain at force F0M .
k PE              Passive force–length exponential shape factor (5 in the paper).
γ                 Active force–length width parameter (0.45 in the paper).
εT                Tendon strain relative to tendon slack length.
ε T0              Tendon strain at force F0M .
ε Ttoe            Strain at the end of the nonlinear tendon region; 0.609ε T0 .
    T
F̄toe             Normalized force at that transition; 0.33.
ktoe , klin       Tendon exponential shape factor (3) and linear slope (1.712/ε T0 ).
Af                Force–velocity shape factor (0.25 in the paper).
    M
F̄len             Maximum normalized lengthening-force factor.


   The passive expressions describe tensile loading; they do not imply that a slack muscle
or tendon transmits compression. These are the original paper’s constitutive relations.
Numerical implementations must also handle slack states, minimum activation, pennation
limits, and the inversion of the force–velocity relation. Consequently, the original equations
alone do not specify every numerical safeguard in an OpenSim implementation.


Millard equilibrium and damping

The equilibrium relation of Millard et al. [2013] can be written in normalized form as


                            a f L (l˜M ) f V (ṽ M ) + f PE (l˜M ) cos α ´ f T (l˜T ) = 0.
                                                                 
                                                                                             (A10)


The damped formulation adds βṽ M inside the brackets, as in Equation A2. The functions
f L , f V , f PE , and f T describe active force–length, force–velocity, passive fiber force, and
                                                                                         191

tendon force. Millard’s smooth characteristic curves and damping address numerical
behavior while retaining the series force balance. Figure 3 of that paper illustrates the
equilibrium, damped-equilibrium, and rigid-tendon formulations. A rigid-tendon model
removes tendon stretch as a state; it does not simply set the passive fiber force to zero.


A.1.2   OpenSim Moment Arms and Human Torque Targets

The human torque targets of Section 4.1.5 are OpenSim tendon-excursion moment arms:
for each muscle, the moment arm about the knee is the partial derivative of muscle-tendon
length with respect to the knee coordinate, with positive tensile force and torque positive
in the coordinate direction, r (θk ) = ´BlMT /Bθk , evaluated across the range of motion. The
exported analysis tables archived with the test data (OpenSim_Vasti_MomentArm.txt,
OpenSim_Vasti_Results.txt, OpenSim_Bifem_MomentArm.txt, OpenSim_Bifem_Result
s.txt) carry the per-muscle moment arms and the corresponding maximum-isometric
torque curves (moment arm multiplied by each muscle’s maximum isometric force) for
vastus intermedius, lateralis, and medialis, and for the biceps femoris short head; the
optimization loads the vastus medialis and biceps femoris short-head columns as its targets
(buildKneeExtContext20mm.m lines 119–140; Knee_Flexor_Data_20mm.m line 546). For an
unconstrained planar rotation, this derivative definition and the geometric perpendicular-
foot construction of Equation (A14) below are equivalent for a straight muscle path crossing
a planar revolute joint; the constrained and moving-path-point geometry is evaluated by
OpenSim rather than by substituting the robot-side straight-path construction.
                                                                                         192


A.2     Converted MuJoCo Muscle Formulation

A.2.1    MuJoCo Muscle Actuator

The converted model uses MuJoCo muscle actuators, which combine activation-dependent
active force with passive tensile force. Let L denote the routed musculotendon length, L T
the constant biological-tendon length, and L0 the optimal fiber length inferred from the
actual and normalized operating ranges. The normalized muscle length is L̄ = ( L ´ L T )/L0 .
The signed actuator force is

                                                                              
                       Fact = ´F0 a f L ( L̄) f V ( L̇/( L0 vmax )) + f P ( L̄) .      (A11)


Here vmax is the maximum shortening speed in optimal fiber lengths per second. The
negative sign denotes a pulling actuator force; both active and passive terms contribute to
tension. The rigid tendon has a constant length, but no elastic state. MuJoCo also omits
pennation, so its differences from the OpenSim equilibrium formulations are not limited
to tendon compliance [MuJoCo Developers, 2023].


A.3     BPA Force Model Implementation

A.3.1    Strain Measures

The musculotendon path length l LMT is the sum of straight segments between route via
points, transformed into a common frame at each frame crossing. The contractile strain
used by the force model subtracts the artificial tendon and both end-cap fittings from the

path length before normalization by the resting length,

(Fmax20.m lines 26–27). Total force is the product F = F˚ (ε˚ , P˚ ) F620 (l0 ), resolved into a

A.4     Muscle Path Length and Moment Arm

Path length accumulates the Euclidean norm of each segment between via points, applying
the body-frame transformation whenever the path crosses between the femur and tibia
frames (MonoPamDataExplicit.m lines 78–95, total at lines 122–133).
   The vector moment arm is implemented as the perpendicular foot of the joint origin
onto the line of action,
                                     r = p ´ û (û ¨ p) ,                                (A14)
                                                                                                 195

where p is the vector from the joint origin to the muscle-path crossing point and û
is the unit force direction (MonoPamDataExplicit.m lines 156–168; identical implemen-
tations in MonoMuscleData.m lines 111–123, the Mom function of minimizeFlxPin.m at
line 539, and MonoPam_mult.m lines 186–201). This is the same quantity as the cross-
product projection of Equation (4.2); the planar scalar moment arm about the joint axis
is the magnitude of the in-plane components, computed as hypot(mA(:,1), mA(:,2))
(predictKneeFlexor20mm.m line 217). Torque about the joint z-axis is m = r ˆ f, evalu-
ated only where the BPA is in tension (values are discarded for ε ă ´0.02, the excessive
stretching region; MonoPamDataExplicit.m line 308).

                          Appendix B: Neuron Model Equations


This appendix defines the neuron and synapse equations and records the parameters used
in the paired graded/spiking comparisons. The archived AnimatLab and 410-neuron
Gait2392 comparisons are distinct from the independently replayed W2L configuration in
Chapter 6. The final section documents that W2L configuration directly from its compiled
network.


B.1     Spiking Neuron Model

Each spiking neuron is a conductance-based leaky integrator with threshold and adapta-
tion,
                         Cm V̇ = ´ gleak (V ´ Erest ) + Isyn + Iapp ,                 (B1)

where V is the membrane potential, Cm the membrane capacitance (gleak = Cm /τm with
membrane time constant τm ), Erest the resting potential, Isyn the summed synaptic current,
and Iapp any applied (tonic stimulation) current. A spike is emitted when V exceeds the
firing threshold, after which V resets and an after-hyperpolarization (AHP) of amplitude
AAHP with time constant τAHP is applied; the threshold itself accommodates with the firing
history (relative accommodation racc , time constant τacc ). Optional calcium channels are
defined but disabled in the archived AnimatLab model (Gmax,Ca = 0).
      The parameter values shared by the neurons in the walker model are given in Table B1.
                                                                                      199

Table B1: Spiking neuron parameters in the AnimatLab bipedal walker model (Biped_2xCP
G_wSubs). All neurons share these values except the motor neuron threshold noted below.

Parameter                      Symbol    Value
Resting potential              Erest     ´60 mV
Membrane time constant         τm        5 ms
Initial firing threshold       Vth       ´55 mV (motor neurons: ´59 mV)
Relative accommodation         racc      0.3
Accommodation time const       τacc      10 ms
AHP amplitude                  AAHP      1 mV
AHP time constant              τAHP      3 ms



B.2   Non-Spiking (Graded) Chemical Synapses

Pattern-formation and motoneuron-drive connections in the walker use graded chemical
synapses, whose conductance is a clipped linear ramp of the presynaptic potential,

                                Vpre ´ Vth
                                                
                                                                               
      gsyn (Vpre ) = gmax clamp            , 0, 1 ,    Isyn = gsyn Esyn ´ Vpost ,     (B2)
                                Vsat ´ Vth

Here inward current is positive, consistently with Equation (B1); an excitatory reversal
potential above the postsynaptic voltage therefore produces depolarization. The quantities
Vth and Vsat are the synaptic threshold and saturation potentials and Esyn the reversal
potential. The walker model instantiates three non-spiking synapse types:


   • Nicotinic ACh (excitatory drive): Esyn = ´10 mV, gmax = 0.5 µS, Vth Ñ Vsat =
      ´65 mV Ñ ´20 mV.

   • Hyperpolarizing IPSP (inhibition): Esyn = ´70 mV, Vth Ñ Vsat = ´55 mV Ñ
      ´40 mV.
                                                                                        200

   • Depolarizing IPSP: Esyn = ´55 mV, Vth Ñ Vsat = ´60 mV Ñ ´40 mV.

      Spiking chemical synapses and electrical synapses (rectifying coupling weight 0.1,
non-rectifying 0.2) are also defined in the model and are available for the sensory afferent
pathways of the MuJoCo/SNS implementation.


B.3     Relation to the SNS-Toolbox Implementation

SNS-Toolbox implements corresponding non-spiking and spiking conductance-based
neurons and synapses [Nourse et al., 2023]. The AnimatLab parameters initialized a 410-
neuron SNS-Toolbox network with per-leg rhythm-generator half-centers, phase-shifted
pattern-formation groups, motoneuron pools for all 92 muscle-tendon actuators, and Ia, Ib,
and II afferent populations. Its non-spiking equations have the forms of Equations (B1)
and (B2), with spiking threshold and accommodation removed. Afferent resting-tone
offsets are zero, so Ia and Ib encode activity without a tonic offset.
      The rhythm-generator layer oscillated under tonic drive with the legs in antiphase
(Section D.4). The layer-specific tests apply constant Iapp at MN, PF, or RG cells. The
matched circuit checks in Chapter 6 concern selected implementations and do not establish
equivalence of the larger networks.


B.4     Spiking-Mirror Implementation, Calibration, and Verification

The mirror builder and calibration script are Code/MuJoCo_SNS/spinal/build_network_s
piking.py and calibrate_spiking.py. Equations, calibration results, verification scripts,
and logs are archived under Code/MuJoCo_SNS/spinal/reports_spiking_20261002/.
                                                                                         201

The corresponding Simulink blocks and tests are Code/Matlab/SNS_Simscape/sns_build
_spiking.m and sns_units_test_spiking.m.


B.4.1   Adapting Integrate-and-Fire Cells and Hybrid Synapses

Between spikes, every spiking cell of the MuJoCo mirror obeys the membrane equation


                        Cm V̇ = ´ gm (V ´ Vrest ) + Isyn + Ibias ,                       (B3)


A spike occurs at threshold θ, after which V resets to Vreset and θ increases by ∆θ. Between
spikes, θ decays toward its initial value with time constant τθ , bounded by the adaptation
floor. Tonic bias Ibias places the equilibrium below threshold. Table B2 gives the values
used by SNS-Toolbox’s SpikingNeuron; this archived implementation uses gm = 1 µS
throughout to retain consistency with its specified current units.
   The rhythm-generator adaptation time constant is inherited from the analog persistent-
sodium inactivation value, 0.35 s at the rhythm test’s operating point. In the contact-driven
analog cores, that slowly inactivating inward current terminates bursts. Its inactivation
time constant is fixed in the tested implementations because the voltage-dependent form
quenched termination and left the network latched.
   Spiking chemical synapses, in both the toolbox class used in the MuJoCo mirror and
the Simulink SpikingSynapse block, carry a conductance that decays between events and
jumps by a fixed increment per presynaptic spike, saturating at its maximum,


         ġ = ´ g/τsyn between spikes,        g Ð min( gmax , g + ginc ) per spike,      (B4)
                                                                                            202

Table B2: Spiking cell parameters of the MuJoCo mirror (real millivolts; build_network_sp
iking.py, SPIKE table, with the rhythm-generator increment superseded by the calibration
of record). All cells rest at ´70 mV with membrane conductance 1 µS and the tonic bias of
Table B3.

Cell class                  θ (mV)           Vreset (mV)      ∆θ                 τθ (s)
                                                              (mV/spike)
Rhythm generator E/F        ´50              ´60              4.0 (calibrated)   0.35 (analog
                                                                                 value)

Interneurons,               ´50              ´60              —                  —
commissurals
Afferent encoders           ´52              ´58              —                  —
Renshaw                     ´52.5            ´58              —                  —


with Isyn = g ( Esyn ´ Vpost ) as for the graded synapse of Equation (B2). The Simulink
HybridSpikingSynapse implements the same dynamics with the spike detected from
the presynaptic voltage crossing a detection threshold that must sit strictly between the
presynaptic resting and firing potentials (a detection threshold equal to the firing potential
fires nothing, because the presynaptic voltage resets at threshold). Reversal potentials are
0 mV (excitation, above rest) and ´70 mV (inhibition, shunting at rest) in the spiking frame,
and +8/´5 mV in the motor neurons’ 0–5 mV frame. The graded-to-spiking command
pathways (descending drive, posture, and bias surrogates) keep the graded synapse of
Equation (B2).


B.4.2   Calibration of Record

Table B3 lists every calibration factor produced by calibrate_spiking.py (spiking_
calibration.json), with the measured residual of each. The spike-to-motor-neuron
                                                                                            203

factors are bisected so that a steady 40 Hz presynaptic train reproduces the non-spiking
steady-state motor-neuron voltage; the spike-to-spiking factors are rate-calibrated (a 40 Hz
source drives a relay cell at 40 Hz, and a calibrated inhibitory factor silences a 40 Hz-
driven cell); the readout-tap factor maps a 50 Hz train to the full-scale analog level. The
rhythm-generator threshold increment was set by an isolated-pair sweep followed by a
full-network sweep over alternation quality and period target.

was zeroed. Each graded chemical synapse became a per-spike synapse with its identifier
and amplitude retained, 10 ms decay, and maximum conductance max(5, 2 ˆ amplitude).
   The per-connection class tag was also changed to the spiking type; changing the synapse
type alone prevented loading. Structural checks confirmed that neuron, connection,
synapse-type, chart, and stimulus sets were retained and every connection class matched
its synapse type.

scoped, but did not attempt, two further conversions: the full 410-population Simulink

spiking conversion a full re-tune rather than a parameter port) and the spiking mirrors
of the alternative MuJoCo network variants (separate architectures whose fresh tuning is
future work).


B.5     Connectome of the Verified W2L Model

Figures B2 and B3 document the W2L configuration used for the independent replays
in Section D.5.1. The corrected bilateral template compiles to 96 neurons, 204 chemical
synapses, and 22 input channels. The node and connection lists were read from the
compiled SNS-Toolbox network using the saved configuration w2l_verified_config.js
on. This is a different network from the 410-neuron Gait2392 model used in the earlier
spiking comparisons; their neuron counts and results are not interchangeable.
      The two sides each contain 48 neurons. RG denotes rhythm-generator cells; RG-IN,
their local interneurons; PF, pattern-formation cells; PF-IN, their local interneurons; MN,
motor neurons; RC, Renshaw cells; Ia, Ib, and II, muscle-afferent encoders; and Ia-IN,
reciprocal-inhibition interneurons. The c1 and V3 commissural interneuron populations
follow the bilateral organization of Shinohara et al. [2025]: c1 mediates inhibitory coupling
between the flexor rhythm generators, while V3 excites contralateral extensor interneurons.
Contact comprises heel and toe encoders. The motor outputs drive six actuator channels
per side. External inputs and actuator outputs are interfaces, not additional neurons.
      Figure B2 groups individual connections by their presynaptic and postsynaptic popula-
                                                                                         209

tions. A symbol indicates at least one connection of that sign; a population pair can contain
both excitatory and inhibitory connections. The diagonal quadrants contain within-side
pathways, and the other quadrants show interleg pathways. The matrix exposes the
sensory projections to both RG and PF populations as well as to motor circuitry.
   Figure B3 shows the actual individual maximum conductance values, grouped by
postsynaptic population. Equal values overlap. This representation retains the spread of
synaptic strengths without implying that every connection in a pathway family shares a
single gain. Conductance values alone do not predict the closed-loop contribution of a
pathway; that requires the controlled interventions described in Chapter 6.
