      Appendix C: Model Geometry, Bracket Frames, and Compliance Parameters


This appendix documents the numeric geometry and compliance parameters behind
the force and torque models of Chapters 3 and 4 as they exist in the project’s MATLAB
code, with file names given so that any value can be traced to the code that produced
it. Route and bracket coordinates are coded in meters and reported here in millimeters;
stiffnesses are coded and reported in newtons per meter. The bracket reference points are
interpretation choices for the 3D-printed (Onyx FDM) attachment hardware, set from CAD
by me; moving a reference point changes the identified stiffness values, so the points are
recorded here alongside the values they produced.


C.1   Static BPA Pathway Data

The static BPA model routes each actuator as a straight-segment polyline through via points
stored in a per-test Location array of dimensions M ˆ 3 ˆ N, where N is the number of
joint frames in the motion sweep and M the number of via points; the first row in the
distal link frame is marked by a cross-point index. There are no binormal or surface-frame
quantities: at each frame the force direction is the normalized vector from the cross-point
row to the previous row transformed into the crossing frame,

                                                                   d
                        d = RTf t (pC´1 ´ t f t ) ´ pC ,   û =       ,               (C1)
                                                                  ∥d∥
                                                                                                   213

where pC is expressed in the tibia frame, pC´1 in the femur frame, and T f t = [R f t , t f t ; 0T , 1]
maps tibia coordinates to femur coordinates. The inverse transform includes both rotation
and translation, as in the implemented point transformation [Provenance: minimizeFl
xPin.m lines 160 and 524–537 (UD) and 201–235 (computeForceVector); minimizeExtX3.m
lines 687–700; BPA struct fields, minimizeFlxPin.m lines 28–35]. The pinned-knee test
routes are archived as FlxPinBPASet.mat (5 flexor tests, M = 2, knee angle ´120˝ to +10˝ )
and ExtPinBPASet.mat (9 extensor tests, M = 7, knee angle ´125˝ to +35˝ ).
   Table C1 lists the five pinned-knee flexor tests; their routes are two points, femur-frame
origin p1 = [´75.0, 100.0, 32.8] mm and tibia-frame insertion p2 = [´50.1, ´45.0, 32.3] mm
(test 1; FlxPinBPASet.mat, kf(1), frame 1).
   Table C3 lists the extensor pinned-knee route of test 1 at θk = ´125˝ ; rows 1–6 are
femur-frame and row 7 is tibia-frame, and as the knee extends, rows 2–6 release onto p1
(wrap released at +35˝ ).
   Table C4 lists the per-test constants of the nine extensor tests; the correction terms of
Section 4.1.4 used tests {1, 2, 5, 6, 7, 8}, of which the adopted fit trained on {5, 6, 7} and
held out tests 1, 2, and 8 for validation; tests 3, 4, and 9 were not used in identification;
their retrospective predictions are reported below [Provenance: held-out columns of
minimizeExt10mmX3_results.mat; Dig_crossPredict.m line 46].
   Table C1 lists pinned-knee flexor test BPA constants, read directly from FlxPinBPASet
.mat (kf(1)–kf(5)). Labels are the laboratory shorthand used in the fitting logs (minimize
FlxPin10mm_2brk.m line 51); l0 , tendon, and pressure are the values used by the model.

   The prediction inputs are distinct from the per-actuator measurements in Table C7. The
flexor predictions use a shared resting length of 561.49 mm, with the nominal 562 mm label

prediction input are related by


                         lten,model = lten,design ´ 20 mm = 5 mm,
                                                                                       (C2)
                        lm,geom (θ ) = Lroute (θ ) ´ lten,model ´ 2lfitting .


Here lten,design = 25 mm is the route parameter and Lroute is the total routed length. The
geometric BPA length lm,geom precedes the offset, compliance, and wrapping corrections.
The 20 mm input adjustment is distinct from the 21 mm end-fitting length lfitting subtracted
at each end. The measured cable lengths in Table C7 are hardware dimensions, not
replacements for this shared effective prediction input. The extensor predictions retain the
shared resting length of 827.74 mm and approximate 92.49 mm tendon parameter. These
original prediction inputs are retained; no new evaluation with the measured dimensions
is implied.

C.2   Joint Transformation Matrices

The knee transform is planar: every joint frame is a rotation about z by the knee angle
θk composed with a translation fitted to robot CAD tables of the migrating instanta-
neous center of rotation. With R = Rz (θk ), the robot (femurÑICR) transform is TPam =
[R, [ f 3 (θk ), f 4 (θk ), 0]J ; 0J 1] with f 3 , f 4 cubic-spline fits (ICR x: 23.30 to 10.47 mm; y:
´416.65 to ´395.66 mm over θk = 0.17 to ´2.62 rad), and the ICRÑtibia transform is
the pure translation [ f 13 (θk ), f 14 (θk ), 0]J (x: 29.66 to ´7.58 mm; y: 25.97 to 22.33 mm),
composed as Tt1Ñ f = Tt1ÑICR T´1                                     ˝     ˝
                              Pam over 100 frames spanning θk P [´120 , +10 ] [Prove-

nance: buildKneeFlexorContext20mm.m lines 34–80; buildKneeExtContext20mm.m lines
30–78; Functions/ModernRobotics/RpToTrans.m]. In the archived test sets the per-frame
transform maps tibia to femur/ICR and takes the form

                                                                       
                                     cos θk ´ sin θk        0 d x (θk )
                                                                       
                                      sin θk cos θk         0 dy (θk ) 
                          Tk (θk ) =                                   ,                       (C3)
                                                                       
                                      0        0            1     0 
                                                                       
                                                                       
                                         0      0            0     1


with rotation block Rz (θk ) and translation components d x , dy in meters. In the archived
flexor test set the translation is constant across the sweep, [d x , dy , 0] = [4.5, ´395.8, 0] mm,
and the rotation sweeps from Rz (´120˝ ) at full flexion to Rz (+10˝ ) at full extension
(FlxPinBPASet.mat, kf(1)).
                                                                                                 220


C.3   Bracket Reference Points and Frames

Table C8 lists the bracket reference points. Each compliant bracket carries an instantaneous
body-fixed frame anchored at its reference point, constructed by two rotations (yaw about
z, then pitch about the yawed y). With pb the vector from the reference point to the
bracket-to-BPA attachment target, the yaw and pitch angles are


      θbr = atan2( pby , pbx ),      ϕbr = atan2( p1bz , p1bx )   with   p1b = Rz (θbr )J pb ,   (C4)


and the bracket-frame pose is


                     Rkbr = Rz (θbr ) Ry (ϕbr )J ,      Tkbr = [Rkbr , pbr ; 0 1] ,              (C5)


The frame’s x axis follows the nominal load path, with angles calculated from the bracket
geometry. Both brackets in the pinned-flexor model and the pinned extensor use the
two-rotation construction of Equation (C5). Forces are transformed into each bracket
frame, and displaced attachment points are transformed back into the limb frame. The
symmetric insertion stiffness array is invariant to the additional rotation; the origin array
is not (Table C9).

bracket frame, Kbr = diag(k1 , k2 , k3 ) newtons per meter. The force-path projection and

flexor values and solves (χ0 , χ3 ), preserving one effective stiffness pair across both
configurations.
   Tables C11 and C12 report every recorded pinned-knee test before and after applying
the adopted corrections. “Before” uses the characterized BPA force model with zero length
and wrapping corrections and rigid attachments; “after” uses the adopted correction terms.

for FVU is given in Chapter 4, Equation (4.29).

C.6   Redesigned Extensor Route Geometry

The direct redesigned extensor route is preserved in Vas_Pam_20mm_Result_20260925.mat;
the cam-assisted route supersedes it as the design of record. Figure C1 shows the direct
route at full flexion, immediately before each contact release, and at full extension. The
plotted event pose precedes the first inactive grid pose used to report each release angle in
Table C2. All route points, including p8, are present in the saved geometry; plotting-title
metadata do not define the route.
   The optimized origin is p1 = [40.0, 137.0, 0] mm in the femur frame, and the insertion
is pEnd = [34.4, ´197.1, 0] mm in the tibia frame. Resting length is 827.7 mm, and tendon
length is 36.8 mm. The displayed envelopes and bend-radius checks explain the route’s
geometric constraints.
   Figure C2 places the same optimized route on the OpenSim bone-surface point clouds
(Open Sim Bone Geometry) at exactly zero knee angle: the full lower skeleton (femur, tibia,
pelvis and hip, sacrum, lumbar spine, and foot) is drawn behind the three human vastus
reference paths in light orange, the optimized p1-to-pEnd route is drawn in magenta with
its endpoints circled, and the optimized route carries a total BPA-plus-tendon path length
                                                                                           226

of 0.784 m at this pose. Animated sweeps of both redesigned routes over the full flexion
range are archived with the optimization results (Knee Extensor 20mm.gif, 2.3 MB, and
Knee Flexor 20mm.gif, 4.7 MB, in Code/Matlab/Mesh Optimization/Results).


C.7   Contact and Release Angles of the Wrapped Route

The route-elimination rule that schedules the wrapped extensor route is a route-topology
state machine rather than a shortest-path solver: the fully flexed route carries every
candidate contact, and each contact is released at the first more-extended knee angle at
which the straight chord between its surviving neighbors can replace it without entering
the exclusion envelopes. This section states the calculation in generic terms, so that it
applies to any wrapped actuator route on a rotating joint pair; the extensor route of
Table C2 is the worked example, and the release schedule the calculation produces is the
one quoted there.
   The route is stored as a fixed number of rows p1 , . . . , p9 : rows p1 through p5 are femur-
frame points, rows p6 through p9 are tibia-frame points, and the segment from p5 to p6
crosses from one body frame into the other. The endpoints p1 and p9 are the actuator origin
and insertion, and they are always active. Each interior row is either active, a genuine
wrap contact, or eliminated; an eliminated femur-side row repeats the preceding active
femur row, and an eliminated tibia-side row repeats the neighboring active tibia row,
so the route keeps a fixed size and its segment chain crosses the joint exactly once. Let
A(θk ) Ď t2, . . . , 8u denote the active interior rows at knee angle θk . The sweep over the
design grid, here 100 poses from ´120˝ to 10˝ at a spacing of 1.31˝ , proceeds from full
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       227

                                                                                         229

flexion toward extension, each pose inherits the active set of the previous pose, and rows
are only ever removed, so A shrinks monotonically as the knee extends.
   At every pose, every active interior row is tested between its nearest active anchors,


             i´
              j = maxti ă j : i P A Y t1uu,         i+
                                                     j = minti ą j : i P A Y t9uu,      (C6)


with the anchors expressed in the candidate’s native frame through the per-angle joint
transforms. Write A, B, and C for the planar, sagittal positions of pi´ , pi+ , and p j , and
                                                                           j    j

let s( x, y) denote the signed angle from vector x to vector y, positive counterclockwise,
evaluated as a single atan2 of the planar cross product and the dot product so that no
principal-value seam can corrupt the comparison. The elimination margin of row j is

                                         $
                                         &s(C ´ A, B ´ A) ,    j ď 5,
                                         ’
                                         ’
                           µ j (θk ) =                                                  (C7)
                                         ’
                                         ’
                                         %s( A ´ B, C ´ B) ,   j ě 6,


a signed angle between the through-contact leg and the bypass chord, measured in the
native wrap sense of the candidate’s side of the crossing segment; the two cases differ only
in that sense of rotation, because the femur-side contacts turn the route one way around
the distal femur and the tibia-side contacts turn it the opposite way around the proximal
tibia. The rotation rule releases the contact once the margin turns positive, the orientation
at which the chord, rather than the contact, provides the outer boundary of the route on
that side. The gate is opened slightly early,


                                 µ j (θk ) ą ´∆,      ∆ = 1.5˝ ,                        (C8)
                                                                                             230

as a release hysteresis: in the last poses before the contact becomes collinear with its chord,
the computed moment arm of the wrapping segment collapses, and releasing the contact
slightly before that orientation, with the collision gate below still controlling validity,
removes the artifact without changing the route family.
   Opening the rotation gate is necessary but not sufficient. A removal additionally
requires the straight bypass chord AB to clear the exclusion envelopes of the candidate’s
side,
                                               
                           dist ABtrim , E j       ě g,   g = 0.5 mm,                        (C9)

where dist is the planar clearance between the trimmed chord and the envelope and
tangency is allowed. The chord is trimmed by 2 mm at each end, capped at 45 % of its
length, because a legitimate contact departs from the clearance boundary itself. On the
femur side the envelopes E j are the femoral-cylinder clearance circle, the condyle clearance
polygon, and the femoral-wall span; on the tibia side they are the two tibial-cylinder
clearance circles and the wall band between them. Every envelope is the normal offset of a
bone profile by the fully inflated BPA radius RBPA ,


                                  q(ϑ ) = e(ϑ ) + RBPA n̂(ϑ ),                              (C10)


where e(ϑ ) traces the physical profile and n̂(ϑ ) is its outward normal; the offset curve
shares the tangent of the physical profile, its radius of curvature is ρ(ϑ ) = ρphys (ϑ ) + RBPA ,
and the candidate contact positions themselves are seeded at tangent points of this curve.
Because the envelopes are drawn at the fully inflated radius while the routed BPA is
contracted at the release angles, each envelope radius may be relaxed by ρ = 3 mm inside
                                                                                           231

the gate, floored at the bone profile plus 0.5 mm; a rotation-gate opening that still fails the
relaxed collision gate indicates that the seed placement, not the gate, must move.
   At the first pose of the sweep, full flexion, the seed route is settled to a fixpoint: every
contact whose two gates already open there is removed, and removals repeat until no
further gate opens, so that the sweep starts from a valid topology; a colinearity guard may
additionally remove a seed contact that starts the sweep already aligned with its neighbors,
and its tolerance is zero in the design of record, so every seed row of Table C2 starts active.
At every later pose at most one contact is released: if several rows open both gates in the
same pose, the row with the largest elimination margin, the one whose rotation rule is
most decisively open, is released first, and the remaining rows are retested at the next pose.
The release angle of row j is recorded as the first grid pose at which the row is inactive.
The activation angle is read from the same record with the sweep reversed, as the last pose
at which the row is active, which is the pose at which the contact re-engages when the
route is driven from extension toward flexion.
   The same active-set record drives the geometric bend measure that scales the fictive-
strain wrapping loss of Equations (4.25) and (4.27) inside the stiffness-aware force balance.
Each wrap family, the femoral-cylinder contact, the femoral-condyle curve, the tibial
contact curve, and the crossing-point curve at the ICR, contributes an arc length over its
effective radius, and the total

                                             ÿ
                                  b(θk ) =       R f (θk ) α f (θk )                     (C11)
                                             f


is the bend signal handed to the force model. Each family’s turn angle α f ramps with
                                                                                          232

knee angle between the two route-state events that bound it, holding zero before the first
event and its plateau after the second, so the topology state and the bending signal are
two readings of one record. The formulation is joint-agnostic: it requires only a fixed route
representation with one crossing segment, per-angle transforms between the two body
frames, and clearance envelopes on each side, so it carries unchanged to the other wrapped
routes of this program.


C.8   Supporting Route Searches and Transmission Sweeps

The transmission studies extend the route search by allowing an artificial tendon to pass
through a tackle before insertion. A redirect changes the force direction; a multi-part tackle
also changes the relation between actuator travel and insertion force. The comparisons
below re-solve actuator sizes for each transmission configuration and evaluate insertion-
side torque against the same human reference. They remain model predictions; physical
tests of the redesigned flexor and cam extensor are reported in Section 4.3.4.
   For the knee-flexor route, the pulley variant converged to a collision-feasible layout
with zero bound violations and a peak predicted flexion torque of 85.56 N m on the
insertion-side measure, the torque applied at the insertion after the tackle’s force loss.
At unity transmission, the knee-flexor pulley route increased peak predicted knee-axis
torque from 81.23 N m to 85.74 N m, while limiting off-axis torque to 0.803 N m. This
comparison measures the effect of the two optimized routing configurations; their different
objective functions do not isolate the pulley alone. The pulley serves as a redirect in this
configuration rather than a force multiplier.
                                                                                            233

   The robot-body model includes an adductor magnus attachment update to P2 =
(´0.1357, ´0.0929, 0.0591) m. At this location, the model delivers 34.28 N m of flexion
torque, 1.12ˆ the human target of 30.61 N m, and 27.62 N m of adduction torque, 0.82ˆ
the human target of 33.60 N m. The OpenSim model retains its eight original moving-
path-point vias for rectus-femoris and vastii patella tracking; the candidate pulley routes
were not added to those paths, and the model contains no pulley entities. The MuJoCo
conversion used for the simulation work predates this attachment update and the pulley
batch. Physical tests of the redesigned knee routes are reported in Section 4.3.4.
   The attachment-route redesign was extended separately from the knee to all 27 muscle-
actuators of the robot-body model and remains work in progress. The first pass reached 19
actuators. All but one selected route violated the feasibility constraints; the exception was
the biceps femoris long-head pair, with a worst-angle margin of +0.03 %. The formulation
was then restructured to enforce each route’s joint-angle task envelope as a hard feasible
set rather than a penalty.
   The restructured pass encountered a route-builder concatenation error for twelve actu-
ators, leaving fifteen finalized routes. Eight were constraint-feasible: semimembranosus,
biceps femoris long head, tensor fasciae latae, soleus, lateral gastrocnemius, tibialis anterior,
iliacus, and psoas. Six also met the torque requirement at every angle, with worst-angle
margins from +0.25 % for biceps femoris long head to +250.7 % for tensor fasciae latae.
Semimembranosus and soleus satisfied the geometry but remained below their torque
targets. Completing the extension requires the route-builder repair and sufficient actuator
capacity or relaxed routing corridors for these two routes.
   The unadjusted cam optimizer solution had sreq = +31.30 % and mh = +42.34 %; the
                                                                                        234

adjusted design in Chapter 4 has +30.83 % and +39.77 %, respectively. Equation (4.38)
distinguishes surplus normalized by peak human torque from the pointwise human-
reference margin.
   Figure C3 shows predicted insertion-torque magnitude versus joint angle and supply
pressure for two BPAs through a two-part tackle (force gain 1, travel gain 2), with actuator
sizes re-solved. Panels show soleus, tibialis anterior, biceps femoris short head (BFSH),
medial gastrocnemius with ankle fixed at zero, and rectus femoris evaluated at the hip.
Dashed curves mark equality with the maximum-isometric OpenSim requirement; no
equality curve lies within the soleus panel.

C.9     Master’s Robot Model: Joint Coordinates and Actuator Sizes

This comparison extends the 27 actuator sites of my master’s robot-leg model [Bolen and
Hunt, 2019]. Table C13 lists the two joint coordinates evaluated at each site and the nominal
BPA sizes. The evaluated RoM is the joint-coordinate domain sampled on the 5 ˆ 5 grid
used to compare each route with its same-named human-muscle reference; a route may
cross additional joints beyond the two evaluated coordinates. At matched pressure, braid
geometry, and relative contraction, ideal BPA force scales with diameter squared [Chou
and Hannaford, 1996], so four parallel 20 mm BPAs have the same theoretical maximum
force as one 40 mm BPA. The baseline uses this substitution at the nominal 40 mm sites
and one actuator of the listed diameter at the other sites.


C.9.1    Torque and Active-Force Coverage

Table C14 expands the means reported in Table 4.12. Torque coverage requires the reference
torque direction and magnitude; active-force coverage requires positive insertion tension.
Both exclude cells outside the evaluated joint-coordinate domain, and slack or infeasible
configurations do not count as active.

              Appendix D: Supplementary Neuromechanical Verification


This appendix supplies supporting model records and evaluation sets for Chapter 6. It
separates implementation checks, gait comparisons, pathway-removal tests, and spiking
conversions because they test different claims. Neuron equations and calibration tables
are provided in Appendix B.


D.1   Model Construction and Recording

The archived AnimatLab projects provide reference circuits for implementation compar-
isons. Figure D1 shows their neural organization. A later reorganized bilateral project
added commissural and biarticular pathways; it ran headless but latched under the tested
drives. That assembly result is not evidence of stepping. The phase-1 recordings below
concern an earlier instrumented configuration, not the reorganized network.
   A 10 s instrumented run recorded joint, neural, sensory, and body-motion channels at
a 0.2 ms interval. Contact-sensory recordings are membrane voltages, not contact forces.
The initial external horizontal force and the limited window prevent the joint traces from
establishing stable walking. Figure D2 shows the recorded hip, knee, and ankle trajectories.
   Figure D3 contrasts the converted Gait2392 robot geometry with the reduced-order
knee model used in the SNS-Simscape demonstration.

D.2     Controller Ports and Reference Checks

The matched W2L test compares implementations from a common circuit specification
under identical drive and contact inputs, using burst period, bilateral phase, burst count,
and voltage correlation. The Deng controller port was checked separately: a current-pulse
test produced a 1.938 s period against a 1.94 s NumPy reference, with antagonist correlation
r = ´0.86. Agreement on these tests does not establish long-term phase agreement in
every network.
   The archived original AnimatLab model produced a period of approximately 0.221 s,
compared with 1.938 s in the translated test. Both alternated antagonists, but the original
bilateral rhythm was in phase. Different parameterizations prevent quantitative equiva-
lence. In both implementations an RG perturbation disturbed activity without sustained
suppression; comparing perturbed and baseline sequences, rather than counting peaks in
a short window, establishes that qualitative response.
   The full exported network agreed with its NumPy reference to 4.2 ˆ 10´6 mV over the
early transient across 410 neurons. Its subsequent divergence and the tonic-drive fixed-
point discrepancy reported in Chapter 6 limit parameter transfer. The OpenSim-derived
Simscape mechanism contains 1,446 blocks and 149 joints; mechanism compilation is
distinct from completing its neural and muscle coupling.


D.2.1    Transient Knee Sensory-Current Response

The knee block model was tested from a settled operating point using a rectangular
current pulse added after the spindle transducer and before the extensor Ia sensory-neuron
                                                                                        247

membrane input. Stimulus and no-stimulus cases start from the same initial conditions
and run continuously for 50 s; the 2 nA pulse acts from 30.000 to 30.030 s. The original
configuration did not settle: its knee angle spanned 0.712˝ during the 25–30 s baseline
window. Its former startup comparison is therefore not used to demonstrate transient
reflex recovery.
   The graded-response comparison changes exactly six parameters: the two MN output
ramp widths from 1 to 10 mV, and the four Ia synaptic ramp widths from 0.5 to 10 mV.
Equations, thresholds, conductances, wiring, mechanics, tonic drives, and activation
dynamics are retained. This reduces loop gain as an explicit engineering comparison,
rather than fitting physiological parameters. The simple actuator-force law is distinct from
the characterized BPA model of Chapter 3. The flexor activation is zero at baseline, so the
experiment does not establish suppression of a tonically active antagonist.
   The modified configuration passes baseline settling tests for angle, velocity, all six
membrane states, and both activations. Its paper-coordinate baseline is ´42.6392˝ ; positive
excursions indicate extension. Extension peaks at 1.0908˝ after 111 ms. Recovery requires
displacement within 0.01˝ of baseline and absolute angular velocity below 1 ˆ 10´4 rad s´1 ;
both remain within those bounds from 6.355 s after stimulus onset. The late residual
is approximately 9.3˝ . A fresh-process replay reproduces the resampled pulse arrays
exactly. Halving the ODE45 maximum step from 1 to 0.5 ms, at unchanged tolerances,
changes the angle trace by at most 5.21˝ . Comparisons use linear interpolation onto a 1
ms grid, without smoothing. These checks establish numerical recovery in the model, not
physiological reflex fidelity. Figure D4 shows the stimulus, matched kinematics, neural
signals, activations, and forces.

D.2.2   A Published Neural Mechanism as a Verification Target

The reduced Python implementation of the two-level circuit of Rybak et al. [2006] passed
13 checks covering drive-dependent activity, repeatability, and the distinction between re-
setting and non-resetting deletions. Stimulating the pattern-formation extensor population
suppressed flexor motor output while the rhythm generator continued, so flexor activity
resumed at its expected phase. Stimulating the rhythm-generator extensor population
also suppressed flexor output but shifted the subsequent rhythm phase. This comparison
tests the functional separation between rhythm generation and pattern formation: similar
missing motor bursts can arise through different neural mechanisms. It is a neural-only
verification of the translated circuit, independent of a mechanical model and separate from
the Simulink demonstrations.


D.2.3   RG, PF, and MN Stimulus Tests

The fourteen-neuron Deng-derived implementation follows the circuit used in SNS-
Toolbox demonstrations [Deng et al., 2019, Nourse et al., 2023]. A 0.1 ms Euler step
reproduced the existing neural integrator’s complete 24-second baseline voltage trace
exactly in the tested process. The baseline period was 1.938 s. Neural output drove
the existing suspended, unilateral W2L mechanical model without afferent return. Hip
commands came from the MN outputs; knee and ankle commands used the existing PF-to-
activation mapping. This configuration tests transmitted circuit effects, not the original rat
mechanics or bilateral walking.
   The initial sweep applied +2 and +10 nA to each layer for 0.1 and 0.5 seconds, together
                                                                                      250

with two direct MN inhibitory controls. None met the deletion criterion. Subsequent
stronger-current control series, including stimuli lasting two baseline periods (3.876 s),
retained the same circuit and mechanical parameters (Table D1). RG and PF stimuli
began one-quarter cycle after a baseline RG onset; MN inhibition began 0.05 seconds into
a baseline motor burst. Positive currents target the extensor RG or hip-PF population;
negative currents target the hip-flexor MN. These currents are mechanistic controls, not
experimentally calibrated physiological stimuli.
   A deletion required at least 20 ms of baseline motor activity in the stimulus window
and suppression of at least half of that activity, using an activation threshold of 0.5.
Phase residuals compare the final four recovered onsets with the nearest baseline onset,
normalized by the baseline period. Residuals at or below 0.05 cycles were classified
as non-resetting, and those at or above 0.15 as shifted; intermediate values remained
indeterminate. RG and motor residuals were assessed separately. The two-cycle stimuli
suppressed both expected flexor burst onsets at all three layers. Baseline and stimulated
neural and mechanical trajectories were identical before stimulus onset. The one-second PF
pulse shifted the motor pattern without resetting the RG clock, whereas the two-cycle PF
pulse preserved both schedules. The duration dependence cautions against treating every
motor phase shift as an RG reset. Figure D5 compares RG voltage and motor activation for
the two-cycle stimuli.

D.2.4    Archived AnimatLab Models

Headless reruns tested whether archived models reproduced their reference behavior in
the original simulator. Eleven targetable rhythm and gait metrics passed their acceptance
bands. The bilateral contact-driven model’s rhythm generators had periods of 0.450 and
0.467 s; the modern W2L model showed joint oscillations at 2.25 Hz. The Li-model rerun
reached 0.637 m s´1 with step periods near 1.3 s [Li et al., 2017]. These are reference-model
checks, not results for the combined Deng–Li model or evidence of unsupported walking.
   A separate MuJoCo translation of the Li controller and body did not reproduce that
original-simulator walking result. In a 20-second ground test, mean forward speed was
0.040 m s´1 , with eleven detected right-foot stance episodes and none on the left. The
right-side period of 1.294 s alone therefore did not establish bilateral stepping. This
failure distinguishes reproduction of the source model from successful transfer to another
simulator.
                                                                                      253


D.3     Tuning and Supported Evaluations

Free network gains were searched with a tree-structured Parzen estimator, with each can-
didate evaluated through simulation. The staged procedure separates suspended rhythm
generation, sensory feedback, standing, and ground evaluations (Table 6.2). Finalists
were checked under 1 % multiplicative parameter perturbations and changes in drive.
Such checks probe the neighborhood of a selected configuration; they do not establish
robustness to untested loads or removal of the harness.
   The cycle-shape metric assigns zero to perfect agreement. A run without detectable
cycles returns ´25 as a reserved failure value. Because this value is numerically greater
than the genuine-gait scores below, failed runs must be identified before ranking scores.
That value itself is not a valid measure of gait quality.
   The curriculum is shown in Table 6.2 in Chapter 6.


D.3.1    Reference-Library Comparison

The 43-cycle library contains 32 running trials from eight subjects at four speeds, ten
walking references, and a predicted-walking cycle. The walking references span prescribed
speeds of 0.5 m s´1 to 2.0 m s´1 and self-selected trials. The production network (s3k),
reduced-connection network (s3kpruned), six-group activation network (syn6), and W2L-
organization network (w2lvar) were scored without treating a good tuning-reference score
as evidence of generalization. Library scores were substantially more negative (Table D2),
and the evaluations showed unilateral or asymmetric gait. This comparison bounds
transfer to the available references; it does not validate normal human walking.
                                                                                          254

   Table D2 uses the production pipeline’s cycle-shape kinematic score. The tuning-
reference column reports each configuration on its tuning reference; the library mean
averages that score over all 43 reference cycles.

Table D2: Four controller configurations evaluated on their tuning reference and the 43-
cycle reference library. For genuine gait cycles, larger kinematic scores indicate closer
agreement; zero is perfect agreement.

D.3.2   Direct Kinematics Overlay

The saved human-model controller reproduced its cycle-shape score of ´145.07 in an
independent process. The direct overlay in Figure 6.5 uses the Gait2392 walking inverse-
kinematics trajectory and its paired ground-reaction-force file. Model cycles begin when
vertical foot loading crosses 50 N; reference stance is defined using the same force threshold.
Ten cycles per side, with durations between 0.35 and 2.5 seconds, were retained using the
existing cycle detector. Each leg was aligned to its own onset and resampled over the gait
cycle, without fitting phase, subtracting the mean angle, or scaling amplitude.
   Mean cycle periods were 1.285 s on the right and 1.254 s on the left, compared with
1.233 s and 1.213 s in the reference. Model stance fractions were 0.710 and 0.649, compared
with 0.610 in the reference. Table D3 reports absolute-angle errors between mean cycles.
                                                                                     255

These errors retain posture offsets and are distinct from the mean-removed cycle-shape
errors used in tuning. The hip and knee mismatches show why cycle counts and a scalar
score must be accompanied by the trajectories and contact timing.

D.3.3   Pathway-Removal Tests

Individual pathway classes were removed from each selected configuration and the model
re-evaluated on its tuning reference (Table D4). Removing Ia feedback from the production
configuration eliminated detected cycles. Other removals changed the score without
necessarily eliminating movement. The different response of the six-synergy variant
shows that these dependencies belong to the tested network configurations, rather than
constituting a general claim about biological necessity. The systematic pattern-formation
and motoneuron stimulation series for these full Gait2392 networks was not completed.
The reduced-circuit tests in Section D.2.3 address a separate, smaller model.
                                                                                         256

D.4     Spiking-Neuron Comparisons

The spiking variants replace afferent, rhythm-generator, and interneuron populations
while retaining graded motor neurons. Synapses onto motor neurons are calibrated by
mean conductance, preserving the continuous activation interface. This construction
does not require the network dynamics or resulting movement to remain unchanged.
Calibration and full conversion tables are in Appendix B.


D.4.1    MuJoCo Network and Ground Evaluation

Under constant drive, the non-spiking and spiking rhythm periods were 0.399 and 0.621 s,
respectively (Figure D6). The spiking period is 55.6 % longer. Timing agreement there-
fore requires more than copying the selected parameters. The untuned spiking variant
produced bilateral cycling in a ground evaluation where the non-spiking winner cycled
predominantly on one side (Table D5; Figure D7). This individual comparison motivates
further controlled testing; it does not establish superiority of spiking control.


D.4.2    Simulink Demonstrations

The graded and spiking knee-reflex demonstrations were compared over matched 5 s runs
(Table D6; Figure D8). The beverage example retained approximately the same sag, 2.02˝
versus 2.00˝ , compared with 7.85˝ without the reflex. These examples demonstrate transfer
of particular reflex responses after calibration, not invariance of all controlled behavior to
neural coding.

D.4.3     AnimatLab Conversions

The tested spiking conversions did not preserve the graded models’ rhythm (Table D7).
Converting thresholds while retaining graded synapses preserved near-baseline oscilla-
tions, whereas converting synaptic amplitudes and decay changed or eliminated alterna-
tion. These results identify the synapse conversion as a limitation of this mapping. They
do not demonstrate that a suitably calibrated spiking implementation cannot reproduce
the reference behavior.
Table D7: AnimatLab spiking conversions versus their graded baselines (5.1 s headless
runs; joint ranges in degrees where the rhythm survives). The thresholds-only ablation
converts the neurons but keeps the graded synapses.

Two-layer walker               0.443 s rhythm;                      dead (2 spikes;
                      hip ´15 to +23, knee ´1 to +60             hip pinned near +6)
Bilateral variant      0.462 s; antiphase ´0.42 cycle    latched; one side at 442 Hz–450 Hz
Reorganized project        latched (tonic drives)         synchronous 11.8 Hz co-bursting;
(standalone export)                                     same-leg envelopes r = 1.000 at lag 0
Thresholds only                                             oscillates: hip ´15.1 to +23.2,
                      hip ´15 to +23, knee ´1 to +60
(graded synapses)                                                 knee ´0.8 to +47.9




D.5     Supported BPA-Robot Evaluations

The BPA-actuated walker exercised the chain from neural activation through the actuator
force model to multi-joint motion. In the supported air-stepping run, the virtual harness
carried 70 % of the 411 N body weight. The period was 1.542 s, knee RoM was 63.5˝ , and
peak forces were 880 and 1372 N, at a peak pressure of 372 kPa.
   With split left/right drives and retuned feedback, the W2L robot progressed 1.72 m
                                                                                         263

over 30 s under the support protocol. It retained progression at 60 % drive and passed 12
of 12 perturbed reruns, but did not satisfy the strict contact-duty walking criterion; below
that drive the gait ceased. The mechanical support was not weaned.
   In the separate human-model drive-weaning study, the selected score improved from
´161.57 to ´145.07, with ten contact cycles per leg and contact duties of 0.71 and 0.65.
It passed eight of eight perturbed reruns and retained bilateral stepping down to zero
hand drive. The motion remained in place under support. These two studies distinguish
reduced neural assistance from reduced mechanical assistance.


D.5.1   Independent Replays and Remaining Mechanical Assistance

A separate W2L configuration was evaluated after correcting the delivery of saved per-
target gains to the evaluator. Four independent 30 s replays produced identical trajectories.
Over the 4.5 s to 30 s analysis window, the run had 28 left and 21 right contact events, a
median bilateral phase of 0.496 cycles, forward displacement of 2.246 m, and fitted speed
of 0.0918 m s´1 . All twelve independent reruns with 1 % parameter perturbations passed
the supported-stepping criterion. These are results for this configuration, distinct from the
drive-weaning study above.
   Removing nominal constant weight support did not remove the position-control har-
ness: its mean vertical force still supplied approximately 16.76 % of body weight. Contact
duty remained below the strict walking criterion. Thus, repeatable supported stepping
was demonstrated, while harness-free walking was not. An accelerated neural backend
preserved the complete reference trajectories and reduced the isolated neural-step cost by
                                                                                       264

a factor of 1.425; this does not establish the same speed improvement for the complete
neuromechanical simulation.


D.5.2   Pathway Deletions and Stimulus Tests

The recovered W2L configuration was evaluated with nominal weight support set to 0.25
and the remaining controller settings fixed. Mild and strong drive reductions, forward
and backward 40 N pushes, and separate Ia, Ib, Renshaw, crossed-heel, and heel-cascade
deletions retained the supported-stepping criterion. Their effects differed: Renshaw dele-
tion reduced forward displacement to 0.081 m, while commissural deletion failed bilateral
stepping. Disabling all sensory feedback retained joint cycling and 1.996 m progression
but shifted median contact phase to 0.223 cycles, outside the accepted alternation range.
The intact comparison produced 2.239 m and a phase of 0.498 cycles. These experiments
distinguish rhythm generation, progression, and bilateral contact coordination, rather than
treating them as interchangeable outcomes. Figure D9 compares the drive, push, and
pathway interventions.


D.5.3   Di Russo Controller Replication Attempt

The original Di Russo controller uses a reduced planar Delp/Thelen model with nine
active coordinates and 18 Thelen muscles, rather than the full Gait2392 model. The source
mechanical model loaded and its OpenSim system initialized successfully; this checks
model construction, rather than gait. Its optimized parameter file contains 256 parameters,
including individual reflex connections, muscle delays, and balance terms. Recovering

                                                                                         266

that source clarified differences from the exploratory adaptation used here. Corrections
to the adaptation addressed phase units, synchronous updates, output saturation, sensor
ordering, contact-force direction, and initialization. Its compiled neural equations agreed
with the corrected Python equations within 3.34 ˆ 10´16 over 2000 steps, establishing
agreement between those two implementations.
   A 960-trial exploratory duration-and-speed search nevertheless selected a tumbling
trajectory, with pelvis rotation reaching approximately 223˝ . That candidate was rejected as
walking, and further optimization of the incomplete adaptation was stopped. The original
controller’s custom runtime was unavailable for a native baseline replay. The useful
outcome is therefore the recovered source and the identified implementation requirements;
replication of the published walking behavior remains incomplete. Source inventories,
corrected-equation checks, and the rejected trajectory are retained in Code/MuJoCo_SNS/sp
inal/reports_20261027_codex/.


D.5.4   CAD-Derived Robot Integration

A separate CAD-derived robot model retained the four-bar knees and spherical hips, with
27 selected actuator routes per side. Its anatomical frame is x anterior, y superior, and
z right; explicit frame transformations distinguish it from older converted models. The
controller drives simulated reference tensions rather than calibrated BPA forces, so this
model does not yet validate the complete actuator-to-controller integration proposed in
Chapter 7.
   The model completed a 20 s supported crouched-stance test. During the final five
                                                                                         267

seconds, knee excursions were 1.35˝ right and 0.95˝ left, while the vertical rig carried
94.68 % of body weight and angular stabilization remained active. Disabling muscle forces
under the same support settings caused lumbar failure after 0.202 s. This comparison
establishes a contribution of the muscle controller under strong assistance, not independent
balance. A separate run with rhythmic drive completed its horizon, but alternating gait
and progression after settling have not been verified.
   A subsequent matched 40-second comparison retained the same geometry, initial pose,
reference-tension capacities, and external assistance in both conditions. During 15–40 s,
vertical assistance supplied 80% of body weight. The additional control combines muscle-
mediated separation feedback (160 N/m, capped at 20 N) with a 6% activation floor on
currently abducting routes, compared with the 5% global floor (Table D8). Full recorded
trajectories reproduce bit for bit between fresh reference and accelerated runs. This is
numerical replay within the specified model, rather than a robustness test.

Table D8: Matched CAD-model comparison over 15–40 s. Vertical assistance remains 80%
of body weight in both conditions.

Measure                                                              Control off   Control on
Reversed ankle ordering (%)                                                  67             0
Minimum ankle separation (mm)                                             ´38.3         142.4
Median ankle separation (mm)                                               ´6.2         159.0
Mean right TFL activation (%)                                              22.3          81.8
Mean left TFL activation (%)                                               99.6          99.8
Maximum sampled knee-loop error (mm)                                      4.485         0.387
Maximum sampled interleg penetration (mm)                                 2.700             0


   The 262-neuron controller receives 230 inputs and drives 54 reference-tension actuators.
Its MN target-current compensation cancels incoming PF and reflex synaptic currents to
                                                                                        268

track the force allocator’s targets. Consequently, the effective comparison concerns RG
timing, engineered force allocation, and mechanical response, rather than normal PF/reflex
authority over activation. Toe-out and small post-ramp knee excursions remain, and the
stepping acceptance tests fail.
   The foot attachment is an unverified assembly datum. A separate hypothesis trans-
lates each whole foot 60 mm toward the proximal shank, retaining tibial geometry and
mass. With otherwise unchanged hybrid control, median ankle separation changes from
159.0 to 57.3 mm, and sampled interleg penetration reaches 1.316 mm. A separate W2L-
organization timing probe has 544 neurons and 230 inputs; four named ankle indices alias
knee-PF cells and are not additional neurons. Its shifted-foot condition passes bilateral
unloading and lifting counts but fails repeated alternating foot apices and forward pro-
gression. The inherited MN compensation remains active, so this is not a full PF-driven
controller comparison.
   Route sites can lie outside CAD surfaces and transmit force through their named parent
bodies, equivalent to ideal rigid offset brackets or guides. Bracket mass, compliance, pack-
aging, and collision-aware wrapping remain unmodeled. These attachment assumptions
are separate from verification of the source moving-point functions and complete path
derivatives. The older 20-second stance test and newer 80%-assistance comparison are
different configurations, not a matched support-reduction experiment.
   The W2L replay metrics and support accounting are archived in Code/MuJoCo_SNS/
spinal/reports_20261027_codex/verification.json and the accompanying support-
ladder records. CAD-model stance metrics are in Code/MuJoCo_SNS/robot_torso/contro
ller_assessment.json. These records retain the configurations and evaluation conditions
                                                                                        269

needed to distinguish the two models.


D.6   OpenSim-to-MuJoCo Conversion Repairs

The conversion work in Section 6.4.2 involved two distinct problems: importing the
robot’s artificial-muscle routing with human muscle parameters, and repairing the con-
verted human reference used for controller development. The first combination failed
muscle-equilibrium checks because the routed lengths were incompatible with the retained
human parameters. The subsequent human-model repairs described below belong to that
reference-model loading path; they are not evidence that the BPA-routed model reproduces
every human muscle force.


Joint directions and coordinate conventions. Single-actuator tests exposed reversed hip-
flexion and hip-adduction actions. The corresponding hinge-axis directions were negated
so that the muscle actions agreed with the adopted OpenSim conventions. The knee
required a different treatment: its converted flexion-negative convention was retained. Ro-
tating the knee without updating its coupled translational coordinates gives a misleading
geometry comparison, so those coordinates must follow the prescribed coupling during a
pose or actuator test. These are specific joint and path checks, rather than a change from a
left-handed to a right-handed model.


Small muscles and force scales. The quadratus femoris, gemelli, and piriformis actuators
were disabled on both sides. Their six entries remain in the 92-actuator model, with no
force contribution. A separate check found eight muscle-actuator entries with an active
                                                                                          270

maximum-force scale of only 1 N. Their active-force scales were restored from the source-
model values. Table D9 lists the corrected values; each applies separately to the left and
right actuator.

    Table D9: Active maximum-force scales restored in the converted human model.

           Muscle                      Converted scale (N)     Restored scale (N)
           Erector spinae                                  1                 2500
           Internal oblique                                1                  900
           External oblique                                1                  900
           Extensor hallucis longus                        1                  162


   These are source-model maximum-force parameters, not measured BPA capacities or
the realized force at every pose. The passive-force scales already contained the source
values. The converted length ranges for these entries remain a limitation, so restoring the
active scale alone does not establish full force–length agreement.


Patella-related paths and coupled joints. The converted moving muscle-path points use
joints coupled to knee angle. Their sampled limits conflicted with the coupling constraints
away from the sampled pose, producing large constraint forces and restricted motion.
The coupled path-point joints were therefore left without those conflicting limits while
retaining their prescribed trajectories. The anatomical driver joints were treated separately:
limits were re-enabled on 14 hinges using the converted ranges. The rectus femoris route
also lacked the patella tracking needed for knee-extension action; its intermediate path
point was replaced by the vastus medialis patella-tracking point. The force comparison
below quantifies the remaining differences at matched poses and activations.
                                                                                           271

Contact and virtual geometry. The converted model contained 19 explicit ground–body
contact pairs, independent of geometry contact flags. Suspended tests remove these
pairs; ground-contact tests retain only the six foot–ground pairs. Path-marker geometry is
noncolliding. These settings isolate intended foot loading from unintended contact with
other model components.
   These checks verify specific joint actions, coupled paths, and intended contacts. They
retain the rigid-tendon approximation and do not establish full OpenSim equivalence or
walking; the matched-pose force comparison below quantifies the remaining discrepancies.


D.6.1   Cross-Platform Force Comparison

The comparison script reads OpenSim static-optimization forces and activations and
assigns the corresponding poses and activations to the converted MuJoCo model. It calls
the forward calculation at each pose; it does not assign the trajectory velocities or integrate
muscle activation and fiber dynamics through time. OpenSim static optimization likewise
solves frame-by-frame force allocation rather than a compliant-tendon forward simulation
[OpenSim Project, n.d.]. The comparison therefore tests force predictions along sampled
poses at matched activation, not dynamic equivalence or tendon-energy storage.
   Figure D10 shows representative force sequences normalized to each curve’s peak.
Table D10 retains the saved per-muscle statistics: R2 = 1 ´ SSE/SST, squared Pearson
correlation, and the best-aligning lag in analysis frames. The calculation and saved values
are in Code/MuJoCo_SNS/spinal/_muscle_force_compare.py and muscle_force_compa
re.csv.
                                                                                         272

Table D10: MuJoCo vs. OpenSim muscle force along the subject01 walk at matched
activations. Lag in analysis frames (« 17 ms each).

                   Muscle                   R2    shape corr2   lag [frames]
                   soleus                 0.742         0.800             1
                   med. gastrocnemius     0.888         0.894             2
                   lat. gastrocnemius     0.884         0.887             2
                   tibialis anterior      0.945         0.945             0
                   vastus lateralis       0.941         0.945             1
                   rectus femoris         ´1.15         0.384             0
                   semimembranosus        0.565         0.621             0
                   biceps femoris short   ´0.26         0.868             0
                   psoas                  0.927         0.934             1
                   gluteus maximus (2)    0.926         0.943             1
                   tensor fasciae latae   0.933         0.935             0
                   peroneus brevis        0.977         0.989             0


   Eight of the twelve representative muscles have R2 between 0.87 and 0.98 and mean-
force ratios within 10 %. Soleus and semimembranosus have lower agreement, and the
negative R2 values for rectus femoris and biceps femoris short head indicate errors larger
than predicting the corresponding mean reference force. Shape correlation alone does not
remove those errors. The rectus femoris path was altered during conversion, while the
biceps femoris short-head result has a substantial force-scale difference. These observations
identify muscles requiring closer inspection; they do not isolate routing, force-law, and
velocity effects from one another.
   The frame-lag calculation describes alignment of these saved sequences. It cannot
establish a physical delay caused by tendon compliance because the comparison does
not simulate that mechanism. The results support selected pose-wise force checks, with
the reported exceptions, rather than a general claim of equivalence for neural-control
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         273

studies. A dynamic comparison would require matched velocities, activation histories,
and explicitly specified tendon dynamics.

                           Appendix E: Supporting SADb Records


This appendix records the curation snapshot and pathway examples supporting Chapter 5.
Counts refer to the stated audited or retained set; they are not estimates of the size of the
neuroscience literature.


E.1    Record Sets and Coverage

The application contains 1,393 records from the five-table base: 943 Papers, 247 Models,
157 Review Papers, 42 Feedback pathways, and 4 Recorders. Of these, 436 are in the
app but not in Airtable due to their 1000 entry limitation. The retained working base in
Airtable contains 957 records: 561 Papers, 244 Models, 109 Review Papers, 39 Feedback
pathways, and 4 Recorders. The application table includes all record types, while its pivot
and citation views use the paper set.
      Coverage of the application’s Papers category is reported in Chapter 5: among its
943 entries, 833 have curation notes, 606 preparation tags, 316 animal-study assessments,
119 pathway links, 95 afferent classifications, 363 robot/simulation translation notes, 892
DOIs, and 318 attached-PDF indicators. The following retained-set counts describe the
maintenance subset only and use a different denominator.
      Among the 561 retained papers, 523 carry curation notes, 426 preparation tags, 240
notes assessing animal-study potential, 118 links to named feedback pathways, 86 an
afferent classification, and 258 robot/simulation translation notes. There are 550 DOIs.
                                                                                        275

These fields overlap and should not be summed as disjoint categories.
      The citation graph covers the 943-paper audit: 730 connected papers, 11,130 directed
in-corpus citation pairs, and 18 Louvain groups. Citation proximity is a navigation aid,
not mechanistic evidence. Chapter 5 shows the citation view (Figure 5.1); Figure 5.2 shows
the paper table.


E.2    Afferent Classes and Linked Pathways

Table E1 summarizes the afferent classes, receptors, and signals available as candidate
model inputs. Table E2 connects representative feedback pathways to their reported
actions in the step cycle and the linked studies. Their record names are database labels;
pathway action depends on the experimental preparation and locomotor state described in
the cited sources. Translating these records into a controller requires an explicit encoding
and verification step.
